# Chapter Summaries

## How to read this file

**This file is a rolling window. It holds the whole of the volume currently being written, one section per batch, and nothing older, and everything older is in `state/archive/chapter-summaries-volumes-01-08.md`, verbatim, with every chapter section exactly as it was here.** The window moved for Volumes 01 to 07 at the Volume 07 close and for Volumes 01 to 08 at the Volume 08 close, **and after that cut this file holds the guide and nothing else.** As of the review of Batch 0004 this file was **1.19 MB across 340 chapters**; it was about 140 KB through Volume 07 and **is now about 4 KB, which is the size a rolling summary is supposed to be.** **Nothing is deleted, only moved, and nothing is renumbered, and a volume is never archived while a close is reading it, so the window moved for Volumes 01 to 07 at the Volume 07 close and not before.**

**Read this guide, then read the section for the volume you are writing, and go back further only when a thread demands it. For the Volume 09 first prompt the useful reading is this guide, `state/current.md`, and `outline/volumes/volume-08-close.md` §§3 and 4, which carry the volume in two tables; the five Volume 08 batch sections and the Volume 08 close section are in `state/archive/chapter-summaries-volumes-01-08.md`.** **The live window is empty and opens on Volume 09; the Volume 01 index and every batch section for Volumes 01 to 08 are in `state/archive/chapter-summaries-volumes-01-07.md` and are reachable by grep, not by reading.** A Volume 08 writer who needs a per-chapter summary of a Volume 07 chapter greps the archive; the summaries are all there under the numbers they always had.

**The same warning as in `state/continuity.md` applies. Do not trust a line-number pointer into a chapter file. Refer to a passage by its chapter and its opening words.**

## The window, at the Volume 08 close

**The window moved forward for Volumes 01 to 08 at the Volume 08 close, and this file now holds the global guide and nothing older.** The Volume 01 to Volume 06 index and every batch section for Volumes 01 to 07, and every section for Volume 08 including this close's own, are in `state/archive/chapter-summaries-volumes-01-08.md`, byte for byte, **under unchanged numbering, and a chapter number means the same thing whether you find it here or there.**

**The live window opens on Volume 09 and holds nothing yet.** For Chapters 351 to 400 the durable record is the five batch sections and the Volume 08 close section in the archive, and for a summary of the volume in one place, `state/current.md` and `outline/volumes/volume-08-close.md` §§3 and 4 are the shorter reads.

---

## Volume 09, Batch 0001 — Chapters 401 to 410, "Nine Words and a Road"

**Days 931 to 972, the twenty-sixth of Mudmonth YR 318 to the seventh of Longlight YR 318, a Monday to a Sunday, forty-two days inclusive, two market mornings at 936 and 957. The year does not turn inside Volume 09 and the whole of it is YR 318. Block 0001 is 22,935 words in ten chapters, counted file by file on a plain whitespace split with the title line included, and the manuscript is 1,312,189 in four hundred and ten chapters. The block's argument in one sentence: *a name that nobody has to answer to is the only kind of record that cannot be got at, and the block finds nine practices that nobody is keeping and one name that everybody knows, and it loses the name in a market by accident on the seventh day.* Nothing in the ten chapters crosses, nothing is explained, no System panel appears, no stage or ledger or qualification word appears, and no name is spent on anything but three working people.**

**Ch 401 · the twenty-sixth of Mudmonth, a Monday, not a market day · the kilns, two days west · the song, and the ninth line held.** A yard of four lime kilns, four families not related for about sixty years, sharing a yard, a water butt, a cart and a song on four different terms none of which is written down. **The fourth kiln is cold since the middle of Harvestmonth and is the first thing a buyer sees from the road, and about forty people know what the buyer thinks and none of them has said it.** **Brenna Tull, 44**, runs the second kiln, calls the protagonist *Aren* once, and has kept a figure on every one of the three things he has done in two years. The draw: a hole made small at the second hour, nine rounds over about nine hours, a bar, a hook, a wheelbarrow, and **a man who has done it twenty-two years can put a word on any one of about seventy and does not choose.** The song in full, nine lines, in parts, on the beat of the bar coming down on the rim — **Ask the far side / Two books / Never a person twice / Read it yourself / Say it in a room / Leave the name off / Anybody may come / Feed the stranger first / — nothing.** **Ivo Serrat, 17**, wheels barrows and has carried glass on that road for four years; he has about four hundred entries in a book of his own that are figures and marks and no words, he can count a barrow of bricks and cannot read, he has never shown it to anybody, and he says so before anybody asks. **The ninth line is ruled in his book and empty, ruled four times because the page wears, and a clerk of about sixty would say about four in ten of the nine hundred such lines in that book were meant to be filled in.** She will not sing the ninth in a yard with a stranger in it, and the reason she gives is that a road said once in a yard of four kilns is a road nine people have heard, and nineteen years of not saying it has been undone by a man standing still for nine hours. Cost: **nine loads of peat at 46 pence running to 414, eleven sacks of quicklime at 22, 436 pence, nine marks and four pence, and none of the four who drew it paid out of any part of it.** Ending: discovery.

**Ch 402 · the first of Greenmonth, a Saturday, a market morning · a back room over a cooper's, ten people, about nine hundred in the street · nine words and no column.** The room is taken for the day at four pence for a man of about twenty, who is told twice not to put a head on the sheet and rules nine lines for eight words. **The first of the eight is asked about four times and four people who have never met each other answer it, and the man of about forty-seven who stood with his back to the board says afterwards that he could tell they had never been in a room by the shape of the answers.** The eighth takes the room for forty minutes: a woman of about fifty at the salt warehouse has put a pot on at the back of her house since Embermonth of the year before last for a man with no shoes, has a figure, will not say it, and says why in nine words — *if I have a figure and you have a figure, then there is a number of me.* **A man of about thirty-four says it is eleven. Nobody writes eleven down, because the man of about twenty had the pen in his pocket, and he will be a man of about fifty in that town.** The ninth line is asked about four times and answered four ways — a name, a place, not a word at all, a line — and a man of about twenty-four with a load of glass says a line ruled for nothing is a line somebody meant to fill in and has four hundred of them in a book, and does not say where, and does not say he cannot read it. The man of about twenty asks whether he wants a column headed *who*; Aren says no, and gives the reason in about thirty words, and the man of about thirty-three with a barrow completes it: **nine rows and one of them would be a man and eight of them would be a thing a man does.** The man of about forty-seven argues the other side for nine minutes, unprompted, and it is not resolved. **A woman of about twenty-nine is in the room all day, is not asked anything, says she has nothing when she is asked what she makes of it, and no one asks her twice.** The sheet stays on the bench-board; the door is not locked and the room is let by the day. Ending: discovery.

**Ch 403 · the fifth of Greenmonth, a Wednesday, not a market day · the arch over a beck, nine miles off the kilns · a mark under a stone.** A single arch, forty years old, about nine feet of dressed approach on either side and nothing on the arch, which is right. **A road nine miles long called Coldharbour, and the harbour nine miles further on is a field, and the road has been the road to a harbour for about four hundred.** The beck is about eleven inches over the sill and the ring is nine feet up, and a man of thirty-two with a limp has to come down onto both knees first. **A mason of about sixty-eight is under there with a lamp on his belt: do not put a hand on the ring, because a hand takes the skin off it and in nine years there is a mark on that arch that is not the mason's mark.** **The mark is on the underside of the ninth voussoir from the left, cut into the bed — the face nobody sees, which is why a mason uses it — and the ninth is not special.** A mason counts a ring off his own hand in the dark, and a ring is wider at the haunch than at the crown, and that is the one thing everybody who has built one knows and nobody who has only looked at one does. **Saber Vahl, 68**, cut the mark forty years ago at twenty-eight, has never seen a man in that county who knew it, and has never once said so out loud. **A clerk came out twice and asked whether he had put anything on the stones, and he said no both times, because he wrote it down, and a man who says no twice is a man who has made a decision.** He will not do the top course: it wants two men, four days, a new stone and a barrow, and **a man who has not seen the stone in nine years does not put his mark on it, and my trade is arches.** His want, said once: there was an arch over a drain at a house in Cask in Longlight of the year before last, he was not taken, he was drunk, both men are dead, and **he is after a day's work at four shillings and sixpence and is not after anything else.** Then the fuse: **he has been saying that road's name to anybody who stops at that arch for forty years, about nine men, not one of whom he knows and not one of whom knows what he is, and there is not one piece of paper in this kingdom with it in it except a man's mouth.** He says it twice in a field beside a beck, and nobody is there to be asked about having said it. Cost: a cart at four shillings a day for two days, 96 pence, and the mason at two days, 108 pence, **204 pence in all, and neither of the two of them said a word to the other about a word.** Ending: discovery.

**Ch 404 · the tenth of Greenmonth, a Monday, not a market day · a room over a cooper's on a street of working yards · the plan, and the refusal, and the reason he cannot have it.** **The sheet is still on the bench-board on the ninth day and has been seen four times by a cooper's wife who does not move it because furniture is not swept round.** The keeper of records is **Mara Vey, 29, four years and a half**, down on the fixed-day cart with about four hundred sheets in a banded bundle tied at one corner with a cord, and she is the person in the room who knows most about what a copy is by about nineteen years. The plan is made in eleven minutes: **eight words into eight places, and the places chosen for what they are, and the sheet itself to go to the eighth and not be a copy of the sheet.** He has four places and two of the people will say no to a sheet of paper and the third cannot read. **He says in about forty words, standing up, that he will hold the ninth, and that about four hundred people went without a road for four months because a man of about fifty-eight kept a store for nineteen years and a clerk wrote his trade in a column — and that he would be doing it with a better hand and would be in nine counties instead of four.** She agrees in the first nine minutes: **he is the person least able to hold it, and I am the person everybody would look at second, and if the ninth one is in that room it is not a ninth one, it is a first one with a witness.** Four seconds of silence, the only silence in the chapter. **And then she refuses the second of the eight, which is *Two books*, which is the only one anybody could keep twice, and the reason is that she keeps a list of nine places, could produce it inside a quarter of an hour, and a person who can do that is holding eight copies in one hand** — and she asks for that to be said out loud, because she has had things said softly in rooms and quoted at her for a year and a half. Then the change of decision, out of a list of nine rooms she keeps and has never shown: **who is hardest to send a form to — a man on no roll, in no yard's book, on nothing that goes out on a cart, in no place anybody has a reason to be, and therefore the only person in four counties a form cannot reach.** She names the man of about nineteen on the bar, who is not in any of her nine rooms, and four lines are written in a book of his own, **and one of the four is hers and he wrote it down because he wanted the words he had written to be hers, and she looked at the four lines for about nine seconds and said one of them was wrong and would not say which.** The cart back is on the fifth morning. Ending: decision.

**Ch 405 · the fourteenth of Greenmonth, a Friday, not a market day · the head of a made road four miles west of Marl · a sheet with nine figures on it and a man who cannot read.** The bar: up Monday, Wednesday, Friday, Sunday, down three, up in about four seconds on a lever and a fulcrum, sockets cut by a man of about thirty with a mallet in about two hours. **He is paid nine pence a day for the three days in seven it is up, is not paid the four hours, and is not paid for the four months of the year when nobody comes, and a person in that position is one this kingdom has no rate for at all.** A hundred and seventy days gives about ninety-seven up-days and about seventy-three paid days, and **there are about seventy-three twists of paper in the left pocket of a coat that is not his and he has counted the twists and not the days.** **The morning: about nine carts a week, about a second and a half each, and no negotiation, because a man of about nineteen has a lever and nine feet of oak.** The man of about thirty-three with a barrow comes up, stops nine feet short of where he has come in about four months, and puts a sheet on the end of the bar. **He was told to leave it and not to say what it is, and he has been on that road about nine years and had never left anything for anybody and did not know until that morning that he could.** Then he says the thing that made him stop coming up that road in about nine words, and it is a ditch four hundred yards west that takes a loaded barrow down about two inches. **The sheet has nine figures, no head, no seal, no signature, and a date at the bottom in a larger hand.** He looks at it and says *that is a number* and *I cannot read them*, and the man of about thirty-three works it out in about four seconds. **I can count. I can count a barrow of bricks. I have been counting cartloads off that road for a hundred and seventy days and there is a man who used to take four and he stopped in Thawmonth and it was nothing to do with me.** Put his finger on the date, which is the fourteenth of a month, two digits, and says he knows which day it is because there is one of these most days. **About eight people in that town have worked out his four months ended on the twenty-fourth of a month in Thawmonth, four are right and four are wrong by a day, all four of the wrong ones are wrong the same way, and the reason is that nine men set the date in a yard and never said it out loud.** The barrow man had the day in front of him and could read it and did not read it out, because he was told not to say what the sheet was. The sheet goes flat on a bench under a stone, because the draught in that shed comes off the floor. **And a bundle has been in that shed for about a hundred and twenty days, unopened, and it is about the size of a small dog, and he has a sister of twenty-two four hundred miles off he has not spoken to in about a year.** Ending: cost.

**Ch 406 · the seventeenth of Greenmonth, a Monday, not a market day · a fold of ground with a byre, thirty miles south, about two hundred people · *Never a person twice*, and what it has grown into.** What the third of the eight means: **the person who keeps a thing and the person who uses it are not to be the same person in the same season, because a person who writes a figure and then spends against it will write a figure that spends** — it is about four hundred years old, it is about a forge, and it is why no man in about nine counties is both clerk of a store and the man who draws from it. **In this fold it has meant, for about a hundred years, that one man walks the boundary and nobody walks it with him.** His father died in a winter; he was eleven; he was the eldest of three; the boundary is two miles and a boy of eleven can walk it, and he has done it about thirty times. **Nobody asked me — you have been told it, I did not hear it until I was standing in a yard at eleven with about four other people in it, and a woman of about sixty-nine said you are your father, and that was the whole of the asking, and it was him asking me, and she did not wait for an answer.** **He is good at it and that is said early and plainly: nine stones of which four are old and two are about a hundred and forty years old and three are his grandfather's, a stream under water about four months in the year, no list of where, four errors in thirty years, and every one of the four caught before the season turned** — three by a man of about fifty who put a stone back and never said so, one by a girl of about eleven who told her mother. They walk about nine hundred yards of the two miles, which is half of it, in about a quarter of an hour. **A boundary walked is a thing you do with a man of about nine on your left and a stream on your right, and every nine or ten yards one of you looks back, and you look past him at about four feet off the ground, and the boy could do it before he could count to nine.** At about four hundred yards is a place four generations have all walked a foot to the left, and he points at it and will not go and look at it: **that is the one nobody argues about, and the two that are in the water are the ones that get argued about.** A boy of about nine, correcting an adult about his father's age and being right, then asking from thirty feet off with both hands in a hole: *are you going to tell him to do it.* **He has had nine years to decide and has got to the end of them about twice, and the boy is nine, and he has decided twice that nine is the age and has decided it twice this year — that the boy does not have to do it, that nobody in that fold has ever not done it, that he would be the first and his grandfather's people would have called him a soft head, and he has made his peace with that because there is going to be one soft head or there is going to be nobody in four generations.** Then the refusal, in about twenty seconds, on the practice itself: **if I take a sheet off a man with a stick and put it on a bench in this fold then I am a person twice, and I will be a person twice in a song and in a room, and there is no way to be the man who walks it and the man who has it, and that is the whole of what my father taught me in about nine words before he died.** **And on the first of Longlight he walks it alone and the boy does not, and about two hundred people in that fold know the boy did not walk it and not one of them knows why, and there is no form in this kingdom on which a man can write down why he walked a boundary on his own when his own son was in the fold with him.** Ending: cost.

**Ch 407 · the twenty-second day of Greenmonth, a Saturday, a market morning · a market town of about two thousand on the salt · the ninth line said in a market, by accident.** The market runs on two days in seven; a rope at one end marks the two; the bar at the other end goes up on five because the market trades on two; about two hundred yards between them, about nine hundred people, about four hundred of them about a cart. The argument is about dressed stone, a chalked corner, and the words *that is my corner* said four times. **The man of about thirty-three with a barrow puts his barrow down in nine feet of ground with nine carts going round him and sings the nine lines in about eleven seconds with a barrow wheel against his boot, and he sings it well, because a man who carries glass and lime has a voice that four hundred people in a market street have heard at seven o'clock for four years.** Nobody sings the ninth with him because nobody there knows there is a ninth line. **About forty people heard it, nine within about nine feet, thirty-one within about sixty feet of a nine-hundredweight load, and about nine hundred in a street of two hundred yards, and there is no wind in that street at the fourth hour.** He got the ninth line right, from a back room, from a man of about twenty writing on a sheet. He does not know what it is worth and never will. **A road's name that is said where a load is being settled is not a road's name any more**, and he says the fourteen words back flatly, as though reading a rate off a board, and adds that he has said nine lines about nine times in four years and did not think about it. **Nobody put it back, and it took about nine minutes to establish and no one wanted to establish it.** By the seventh hour a man of about forty-three said there was no such road and a man of about twenty-six said there was, and that his grandfather had a field by it, and it went nine miles, and it was nothing now, and it had been a harbour once — **and a name that had been a road became a road that a grandfather had a field by, in about four minutes, in the ordinary way, with nobody's voice raised.** Then a woman of about nineteen says it to a man of about thirty who writes it on the back of his own hand, and a man of about thirty-eight with a crowbar asks whose hand it is, and she says she does not know and is sorry. **He put a bar of soap over it in the evening and did not put anything over it in the morning, and the whole of the difference between a name in a song and a name in a county is about nine hours and a bar of soap.** And the man of about thirty-two with a limp worked out at the cross end of two hundred yards, badly, on a bad leg, that the plan had been right about everything except the one thing it was about. Ending: cost.

**Ch 408 · the twenty-eighth day of Greenmonth, a Friday, not a market day · a roadhouse nine miles on from the kilns · four feet of board with no head on it, and a boy who cannot read.** She is called nothing, has been there about thirty-one years, bought it off a man of about sixty, never shut the door in the daytime, about nine people a week. **The eleven o'clock is not an hour anybody chose: it is the first person off the made road, and about four people come in at the eleventh hour and are still there at the second hour of the afternoon, and the figure is four a week in Mudmonth and eleven or twelve in Harvestmonth, and that difference is the whole of what a roadhouse on a made road is, and there is no figure for it except a woman of about fifty and a plate.** The practice: **feed the stranger first — the argument for about a hundred years is whether the stranger is fed before he is asked what he wants or before he is asked who he is, about four places do it the first way and about five the second, and this one does both, in that order, and does not do it in a hurry.** The column: **four feet of a board, no head, thirty years, three hands, about three hundred and seventy figures, no rule about how often one goes on, and she cannot say what any of them means except that every one of them is a plate — and she will not read it out, because if she reads it out you have the figure and she has said it, and she has said a figure out loud twice in thirty-one years and a man of about forty came to the door with a sheet and wanted it in writing for a bench and she did not give it to him and does not know where it went.** Four shillings a week in bread and cheese, about two hundred and eight shillings a year, done in a man's head in about four seconds, and she says he is the second person in thirty-one years to do it and the first one did it in writing. **A man of about forty-three says he has never once been called a stranger in his life and had not noticed until about eleven minutes ago, and a man of about twenty-four says a line ruled for nothing is a line somebody meant to fill in and has four hundred of them in a book and does not say he cannot read it.** Then the best thing in the ten chapters, in about four minutes, with no speech: **she brings the board in off the kitchen wall and the boy of seventeen opens his book, and four feet of board and about four hundred entries lie side by side with no head on either, kept by two people who cannot read what the other has written, about nine miles apart on a made road, neither of them knowing ten days ago that the other existed. They find about nine marks in common, and the ninth is a mark neither of them can read, because the person who can read it is a mason of about sixty-eight about forty miles off.** The barrow man comes in at the fourth hour, is fed, and is told she heard him in a market on Saturday and it was a good song, and every person in the room stops for a second and a half. **The boy of about eleven with a pail is four hundred yards west on a shoulder at every hour of the daylight, is the reason the barrow man stopped coming up that road, is not the roadhouse's, and does not know.** She reads seven of the eight out loud in the road and does not read the eighth. Ending: discovery.

**Ch 409 · the first day of Longlight, a Monday, not a market day · a field four miles off a made road, a stone in a hedge, a figure twenty-one years old · a clerk's two kinds of minute and no third.** Longlight is when a boundary oath-stone is renewed: the neighbours walk it and a figure is cut into its face with the year and the stone goes back in the hedge. This one is about three feet and undressed and eleven years overdue. **It is not the stone the records speak of, and nobody in that field has ever heard of the other one, and there is about nine inches between them.** About nine people walk about two hundred yards of line in about forty minutes; **the first of them is a man of about sixty-seven who farms the near field and was forty-six when the figure was cut, and he is the reason it is eleven years overdue and nobody has ever asked him about that.** **A clerk of about sixty keeps two kinds of minute for about nineteen years: a walk, which is who came, in what order, which side of a hedge they stood on; and a figure, which is what was cut and when. He is not going to keep a third, and he says so in about eleven words: if I wrote down the names to go with them then in about nine years there would be a man in a town two hundred miles off who could tell me that a field of about nine acres had nine people in it on a Monday in Longlight, and he could not tell me one thing about the hedge.** Tovan Kest is the man of twenty-five; the yard sent a barrow, two men and a length of board, and he agreed in about nine seconds because a barrow going four miles for a day is not a job anybody has to think about, which is the whole of his opinion and he was right. **He did not come to see the man of thirty-two with a limp and was not asked to and was not asked why he was not, and they had about four sentences together in about four hours and all four were about load.** *Put a board under the near wheel.* *It is soft for nine feet and then it is not.* *It is soft for nine feet going and it is hard coming.* **And that is all that happened between the two of them, neither of them has said one word about any year, and about four hundred people in that town would not believe it either way.** The mason of about forty-three and an apprentice of about fifteen: **a mason does not offer, because a man who offers to do that has told you he does not know which of the two of you does it, and the clerk cut the year with the mason's tool and the assistant held the head and did not swing it, and the mason said afterwards it was good work and the clerk said it was the same work he has always done.** Nine inches of play in a hole, four inches to the lane, and the number is in the clerk's head and not in either minute: **if I lose it then a field of about nine acres and a hedge of about forty yards will be argued about in about eleven years, and the arguing will not be about the hedge — it will be about which of nine men said what, and I have watched what it turns into, and it turns into the thing itself, and the hedge goes.** Cost: **a mason at three shillings and sixpence, 42 pence, two men at six pence, 12 pence, 54 pence, and a clerk paid by a county and not by a field.** Ending: decision.

**Ch 410 · the seventh day of Longlight, a Sunday, not a market day · a town of about four thousand in a fen county · eight words into eight places, done by somebody else.** **Eight places: four he has been in and four he has never been and is not going to be told about.** The other four were named on the fourth of Greenmonth in about nine seconds, off a list of nine rooms the keeper of records keeps and has never shown and could produce inside a quarter of an hour, **and that is the whole of what she gave, and it is the largest thing anybody has given anybody in about nine days.** The paper: **a quire at two shillings and sixpence, thirty pence, two sheets between eight copies, a quire about twenty-four sheets** — and the four feet of board in a kitchen that has been kept for thirty-one years cost nothing and is worth more. **The cart was loaded on the Saturday: about a hundred and four sheets, banded in dozens, a band is a hundred and eight and about a hundred and four is a band with four taken out of it, tied with a cord, and eight of the hundred and four are the same eight words, and they are in the middle of the load, and the man of about thirty-three with a barrow loaded the cart and said he had seen those eight lines before in a market in a road, and would not say where, and tied the band and went on loading.** **The room does not know which eight they were and the man who loaded the cart does not know what he said, and there was no plan in it, and nobody on that cart is a keeper of anything.** **The man of about twenty was not in that building and does not know and will not find out for about eleven years, and he is the only person alive who has seen the whole of it twice.** Four of the eight have already sent word and none was angry and **three refused the paper and did the thing anyway**: the woman of about forty-four will not have a sheet in a kiln yard because a sheet can be picked up and she is right and nobody has the answer to it; the man of about forty-one said the thing already said at length and a boundary will be walked by one man and not by the boy; the woman of about fifty read seven of them to an empty room and did not read the eighth and the paper went back on the same cart in the same band. A fourth, a man of about thirty-one with a canvas bag, about nine days' ride away, says there is a better place to put the words and will not say where. **A fifth word came back from a road, and it is the only one of the five that asked a question** — *whether the first of the eight is still being done anywhere* — and the keeper of records read it standing up in about four seconds and then sat down and did not answer it for about a minute and a half, which is the only time in about nine days that anybody in that building was not doing what they were doing. And **the man of about thirty-three with a barrow is going to have to ride that cart in about a fortnight to find out whether a sheet with eight words on it got to a man in a byre, and he has not been asked to and has offered, and there is no form in this kingdom on which a man volunteers for a delivery.** The ninth copy is under a stone on a bench; the first is in nobody's hand on a beat of about seventy times a round; the second is nowhere; the fifth is in a drawer; the seventh is nine days of a cart ride from a man who does not know he is going to fetch it. **And a man of about thirty-two with a limp is holding none of the eight and none of the ninth, is on no list that any of the nine places is on, and has no form anywhere in nine counties with his trade against any of the eight, and it is the first thing he has wanted in about four years that he was not also the person to pay for.** Ending: decision.

### The ten endings, as a list

1. **Ch 401 · discovery** — a ninth line ruled four times and empty, and a woman of about forty-four who says the price of not saying the ninth is nineteen years and has just had it undone by a man standing still.
2. **Ch 402 · discovery** — eight words on the back of a form with no head, on a bench-board, in a room that is let by the day and whose door is not locked.
3. **Ch 403 · discovery** — a name of a road said twice in a field beside a beck with nobody in earshot, and the only man in nine counties who knows it does not know that it matters.
4. **Ch 404 · decision** — four lines written in a book of his own because he wanted the words he had written to be hers.
5. **Ch 405 · cost** — a stone on the corner of a sheet with nine figures and no head, in a shed nine feet by seven, and a man who counts a barrow of bricks.
6. **Ch 406 · cost** — a man who walks two miles of boundary alone on the first of Longlight and a boy of nine who does not, and about two hundred people who know and one reason and no form.
7. **Ch 407 · cost** — nine hours and a bar of soap, and a name that had been a road now being a road a grandfather had a field by.
8. **Ch 408 · discovery** — four feet of board and about four hundred entries side by side with no head on either, and nine marks in common, and a woman who read seven of the eight to an empty room.
9. **Ch 409 · decision** — a year cut into a stone four inches from the last one, and a clerk who will not keep a third kind of minute, and a board under a near wheel.
10. **Ch 410 · decision** — a hundred and four sheets on a cart, eight of them the same, and a man of about thirty-two with a limp who is not a keeper of anything.

### What the block did not do, one line each

- It did not resolve the shut road four hundred miles off and did not schedule its reopening, and no character in that place appears in any of the ten chapters.
- It did not answer the offer, and the offer does not appear, and the man of thirty-two with a limp is not put in it.
- It did not put the about eleven hundred on anything and did not destroy or withdraw the register.
- It did not put the two villages back on a road and did not tell them why.
- It did not improve on the corridor's rule and did not print the corridor's rate.
- It did not restate the want of the woman of about twenty-nine; she is in one room in one chapter, is not asked, and says she has nothing.
- It did not repair the breach between the two men and did not print the barred kin-term; the three sentences they exchanged in Chapter 409 are about a board under a wheel.
- It did not report the span built over nineteen mornings as a repair, and it does not appear.
- It did not name the antagonist and did not put the marshal on a page.
- It did not put the city, the valley, the roadmark, the press's opening day, the leaf with a seventh line that says *a person*, either line in a stone, the marginal instruction, the word in front of a place name, or the account of the worst winter in eighteen years on a page.
- It did not explain the mechanism, print a System panel, make a cut, or schedule one.
- It did not print a stage word, a ledger word or a qualification word; a sweep of the ten files returns zero for each.
- It did not print the fifth unattributed two-line statement, and the budget is four of four and spent.
- It did not move the count on the page under the fourth step, did not add a tenth asking or a tenth not-asking, did not add an eighth register entry, and did not add a speech-length figure; there are none in the ten files.
- It did not merge the three bars; the only bar it touches is the one four miles west of Marl, and the market bar at Marl gets its own figure of up on five days in seven because the market trades on two.
- It did not take the Hushwater thread, and it says so out loud in the outline rather than pretending otherwise.

### The review repair to this block, what changed in each chapter and what did not

**A review was run over the ten chapters and returned forty findings; seven were canon-level and all seven are repaired. The findings that changed a chapter, chapter by chapter, and the rest of the chapter is untouched.**

- **401** — Brenna Tull's allocated flaw was absent and is now on the page: **she cannot write and she will not dictate, and her house has no door on the back and has not had one since she was thirty-one, and that is the whole of what she has got instead of a copy** — which is why the first of the eight is in her mouth on a beat and not on a sheet. Nothing else in the chapter changed.
- **402** — the man of about twenty-four who owned four hundred ruled lines and did not say he could not read them is now **a man of about twenty-seven who mentions a boy on a road and does not give the boy's name**, because that boy is Ivo Serrat, who is seventeen, and the block was making its central disclosure twice and the first time to a stranger who walks away. The man of about forty-seven no longer stands in the doorway and at the board in the same chapter. One unclosed bold marker closed.
- **403** — **the mason is named**: *His name is Saber Vahl and there is nobody else in nine counties who could tell you whether it is the same man, because he has not been in a room in about eleven years.* The arch is dry **forty** years and not a hundred and forty. The refused job is dated by its own interval — *about eleven years ago* — and not by a month in a year that is two years off. The two speech figures that did not foot are gone.
- **404** — **the press is no longer dated**, which the volume's own rule forbids and which the first printing broke. The roadhouse keeper is a woman. The four words the protagonist speaks about his own handwriting no longer say the press has stood open since a named Monday. The silence is four seconds in a room and not in a record of a conversation that nobody wrote. The standing-up speech is **ninety-four words**. The cart carries about a hundred and four sheets and not about four hundred.
- **405** — **the dateline's near-verbatim lift of Chapter 400's Sunday is gone**, and the Friday now carries carts and not a chapel, because a parish goes to the next parish on a Sunday and not on a Friday. **Nine carts in about four hours on a Friday, and about six times that many on a Sunday**, which is Chapter 400's own figure reconciled. He now says that he can count to a hundred, do a sum of two things, and cannot read, and that the three are three separate things. The two distances between the town and the room two hundred miles off are one distance.
- **406** — **nine hundred yards is a quarter of two miles and no more**, and the clause that said half is gone. The count of pages is a count of hours. Nothing else changed.
- **407** — **the volume's one repair of consequence.** The man of about thirty-three with a barrow now has a cause for the ninth line: the first eight from the kilns over four years, and the ninth his own, from a road his father used to send him along, with one road of that name in the county and a sign painted out. **And the chapter now says the finding: one of them was in the room and the other one was on the road and neither of them chose anything.** The rule about who is asked about a thing is now shown to have been broken before it was written, by the plan itself. About forty people heard it and the thirty-one are not additive to the nine. *Say the twenty words* is now footed. Nothing else in the chapter changed.
- **408** — **the board is four feet and about three hundred and seventy figures at nine to the inch**, and a month of plates is about four hundred and thirty figures and she has never once added a plate to the board in her life. The year's figure is about two hundred and six shillings on a three-hundred-and-sixty-day year. The man of about twenty-four who does the arithmetic in his head is a man of about forty-three. The two of the three hundred and seventy that are a fortnight apart are a fortnight apart. The ninth mark is no longer identified. The barrow man comes round the ditch on the grass and does not say why.
- **409** — the clerk's fifty-nine words and fifty-seven words foot. The hypothetical figure is nine people, which is who walked it. The new figure went into the stone about a third of an inch from the last one, and the four inches is the play facing the lane, which is a different measurement and is now a different measurement on the page. The two men had three sentences together and there were three.
- **410** — **the eight-place ledger is broken into paragraphs and made to agree with itself**: three of the eight are nowhere, and that is the finding. The fourth place is nowhere because a mason of about sixty-eight said a stone is not a place to keep a piece of paper. The fifth is a drawer. The sixth is a cart. The seventh has offered and has not been asked, once and in one place only. The first is the kilns. **About eighty and three twists of paper, not seventy-three, on the day twenty-three days later, with the derivation printed.** The four places were visited inside about forty-one days, not nine. The other four were named on the tenth of Greenmonth, which is the day of Chapter 404. About four hundred and fifty quires a year, not nine hundred. The last line's chapel figures now agree with its own dateline.

**And what the repair did not touch, which is the larger half.** The ten datelines, the ten dates, the four money columns, the nine words of the song, the eight practices and where they are held, the bar and the shed and the stone and the bundle, the four refusals, the clerk's two kinds of minute, the two collections with no head on them, the nine marks in common, the three sentences about a barrow wheel, and the last line of the volume are all as they were, and the words in them are the words the block was written with.

---

## Volume 09, Batch 0002 — Chapters 411 to 420

**The test stated as a question in a room.** Day 978, Longlight 13, a Saturday market, a room over a saddler's shop in a fen town of about four thousand people, taken for the day, about eleven people in it, nine of them because it is a Saturday. **A man of about thirty-two with a limp asks one question eleven times in the ordinary way, standing at the back, one person at a time: who has to be here for it to go on.** Not who does it, not who is to blame, not who would be missed. A woman of about thirty-four tells him he is the third person to ask her that in about four years and answers as she answered the other two. Six answers come out of the room: a family, a chair that goes round, a hat anybody may put a name in, nobody at all, whoever has the next one coming, and a room that is let by the day and whose keeper goes home. A man of about twenty hired for the day at four pence numbers the four, and the numbering is wrong inside about four minutes, because a family and a nobody cannot be counted against each other and a chair and a hat cannot either. **A man of about forty-seven says in nine words, one is first now, and there is no first, and nobody agrees with him and nobody disagrees.** The board is five lines and not four, because the four kinds and the fifth that would not sort and the room itself are five answers, and the fifth line has nothing numbered against it and is still legible at the right angle. **And not one of the four people he came about was in the room**, and the four answers came from four people who do a thing rather than four who have a practice. He does not number the four and the four go back. Ending: decision.

**A public lot.** Day 983, Longlight 18, a Thursday market, a meeting room over a store in a township of about nine hundred people two days off. The room has a chair on a platform, a hat on a shelf and a book of what was agreed, and has had both for about a hundred and forty years. **The fifth of the eight, say it in a room, five words long, is offered to it in the ordinary way at the third hour and accepted in about four minutes, because a room that has a thing said in it is the right place for a thing that is said in a room.** A woman of about sixty-three says the room has said things out loud before and that a thing said in a room is not kept by the man who says it, because about forty other people heard it. **And then the room turns it over to the chair and the chair turns it over to the hat, and that is where a hundred and forty years of a good arrangement meets the one thing it has never been asked about: the fifth is said to somebody, and the lot answers who is in the chair.** The chair is empty three months in every twelve and nobody in that township can tell you which three. A woman of about twenty-nine says, in the ordinary way, that the man of about thirty-eight has already been asked in a kitchen four days ago and said no, and the name stays in the hat, and what the man of about thirty-eight seven miles off does not know is that his name is in it and that he has already said no to that chair in a kitchen nine miles off, and nobody asked her where she had heard it and nobody asked her twice. **A woman of about forty-nine says the man in the chair is not the person it is for, and that you cannot put him there by drawing him out of a hat, and that if you can then you have made a room into a bench.** Nobody improved on it. Ending: cost.

**A rotating clerk.** Day 992, Longlight 27, a Saturday market, a room over a door on a lane. The bench is nine men, a board, a rail and about nine feet of floor in front of it, and the nine feet is the part that matters. **The bench has been asked nine things in about nineteen years and the nine is on a page under the fourth step of an outside stair in another building, and the page is not moved.** The minutes go round: a clerk is a year of a man's life and not an office, about eighteen men in that county have been it before the present one, about eleven did not want to be, and not one of the eleven said so in the book. **The man of about fifty-eight, on his second year with four months left, says in forty-seven words that nobody asks him whether he will do a third, and that if he said no they would have to write down that he said no.** A man of about thirty-four with a sheet puts nine lines on the rail and asks whether the bench will take up one of the eight, and the bench is asked nothing, because two men from the Crown's own road are in the room and the officer of about thirty-eight says in nine words that an officer in it makes it not bench work. What happens instead happens on the floor, in about nine minutes, from a man of about twenty-seven who sells nails. **The officer comes back at the sixth hour on his own, says into the nine feet that the county wants a place and a figure and a trade and that a road has none of the three, and says that he will not say it twice. One line goes in the book and the thing is not written.** Ending: cost.

**No gate at all.** Day 998, Rainmonth 3, a Friday and not a market day, a hurdle across a lane nine miles off a made road in a dale of about nine hundred people. **The reason is nine words from a man of about forty-four who farms the near side and puts the hurdle across twice a day before he has had his breakfast: a man with a form stayed here four years.** Nobody improves on it. **Nobody in that dale is unfriendly, which is the finding, and a woman of about thirty-one feeds him and does not ask him what he wants or who he is, and a man of about nineteen with a dog does four seconds of arithmetic about why a man who cannot find his way back comes for a plate, and the dog does not bark.** A man of about fifty-eight gives the same nine words and then forty-four words that add nothing and take nothing away. **And the thing the protagonist got wrong, he got wrong in a lane on foot: he has been carrying three refusals as three doors with faces on them, and a place with no gate does not say no. It has a hurdle and a man of about forty-four, and there is nobody in nine hundred people to be asked, and not being able to be asked is not a refusal.** A woman of about twenty-nine tells him that a man with a stick who takes the bank is a man who wants to be asked to be helped. He takes the long way round, which is half a mile further. The cost is a loaf and a quart of small beer at the two ends of the lane, three pence, the smallest column in nine counties. Ending: cost.

**The fifth place.** Days 999 and 1000, a Saturday market and a Sunday, on a cart and at a byre eleven miles off a lane about forty miles from a town of about two thousand on the salt. **The man of about thirty-three with a barrow gets on the cart and nobody sent him and there is no form in this kingdom on which a man volunteers for a delivery.** The sheet got there on the Saturday a fortnight before. A man of about fifty-three mucking out a byre says, in the tone of a man telling somebody how many hurdles he has to mend, that there have been three of them, and they are on a shelf above the milking pails, folded in four by three different hands, and he has never known what they are for and has never asked and has not thrown them away, because a sheet with writing on it is not his to throw away. **The finding is that a list of nine places is not a distribution and is not a list of people, and that the three of them in that clock case and the one in a drawer in a market town and the one in a coat on a cart are five of the nine, and that the man of about thirty-three was in the room where four of the nine were said out loud in about nine seconds and it did not occur to anybody there, including him.** The barrow man is asked whether it is going to keep coming and cannot answer. He puts his hand in the inside pocket of a coat that is not his for a twist of string and there is a fold of paper in it instead, and he does not open it, and he rides back to a crossroads at the ninth hour, walks the two days home, and has four days of his own trade gone with a fold of paper in the coat that is not his the whole of the way. Ending: discovery.

**The question from the road.** Day 1002, Rainmonth 7, a Tuesday, at a single arch over a beck nine miles off the kilns and at a drain arch in a wall at a house in the next village. **The first of the eight is ask the far side, four words, a builder's practice about four hundred years old, sung in a yard of four kilns two days west and nowhere else in nine counties.** It came in on a cart with eight sheets and a woman of twenty-nine read nine words in the time it takes to say nine words and then sat on the edge of a bench-board for about a minute and a half and put it in a drawer, **and she has not answered it in about thirty days and nobody in nine counties is going to answer it for her.** A mason of about sixty-eight came to stand about nine feet off the drain arch for an hour and a half and was not asked to do any of it and the man who should do the top course of the arch over the beck is standing nine feet away and has not been sent for. **Asked whether anybody still asks the far side, he takes it as a question about a job and answers in nine words: I do. I have done it four hundred times.** And then, to the mortar, that the far side of a drain is a man in a house with a garden, that he goes to the door and asks and does it either way, and that he has said the words to about nine men in forty years and not one has come back and he has not stopped asking. **The mason of about fifty-one has never heard of it and the boy of about fourteen has never heard of it and the man of about fifty-one said his father's people did a thing with a word he could not remember.** The answer is in the kingdom and nine miles from a drawer and nobody carries it. He writes none of it down. Ending: discovery.

**Hereditary custody, paid.** Day 1004, Rainmonth 9, a Thursday market, a fold of ground about thirty miles south. A man of about forty-one mends a hurdle at the byre fence and a boy of about nine takes a stone out of a wet hole with both hands. **At the fourth hour, about four hundred yards down the boundary, in the middle of a job, he says it in nine words: you do not have to. I have, thirty times.** Neither of them says anything for about two hours. The boy's first question of two that day is *then who walks it*, and the second is about his mother, and the price is that there is nobody: the knowledge of where that fold's boundary is is in the head of one man, on nothing, in neither kind of minute a clerk of about sixty keeps two hundred miles off, and it has been wrong four times in thirty years and each of the four was caught before the season turned, three by a man of about fifty named somebody who put the stone back and said nothing and the fourth by a girl of about eleven who told her mother. **The second nine words are say you have not been asked. That is all.** The boy asks whether his mother knows, and about the wrong version that is already in his mother's sister's house. A man of about forty-one did not tell his mother, who is sixty-three, and there is no form in this kingdom on which a man tells his mother that he has stopped being the second of nine stones. **The cost is paid and the boots are not in the boundary's column: a pair of a boy's boots, second hand, at two shillings and two pence, and the money for a second pair as well.** A boy of nine in new boots walks about forty yards of the boundary on his own and turns back at the second stone. Ending: decision.

**The bar, again, and the same three figures.** Day 1007, Rainmonth 12, a Sunday, at the head of a made road four miles west of Marl. Nine feet of oak, nine pence a day, and the twists of paper. **The derivation is printed on the page: a hundred and ninety-three days to the seventh of Longlight, about a hundred and ten up-days and about eighty and three twists, then thirty-five days more, which is five weeks exactly, twenty up-days and fifteen paid days, eighty and three and fifteen is about ninety-eight, and two hundred and twenty-eight days at four up-days in seven gives about a hundred and thirty.** Nine pence for about ninety-eight days is eight hundred and eighty-two pence, which is eighteen marks and eighteen pence, and there is not one figure of that sum in this kingdom on a page or a board or in a mouth. **The day he is not paid for is a Sunday and a Sunday is about six times as busy and about fifty-four carts come up in about four hours, and the pocket does not get a twist.** The man of about thirty-three with a barrow stops nine feet off, looks past the bar at the sheet under the stone, **does not ask what it is, and cannot say afterwards why he did not ask**, and two hundred yards up the road tells a man of about thirty-four that he could not have said why he stopped. **About eight people in that town are right or wrong about the twenty-fourth of a month in Thawmonth, four of the eight wrong by a day and four of them right, and about eight of the eight are in that town today on a Sunday in four different houses and nobody is going to be the first.** The sheet has been on the bench about fifty-eight days. The cost of keeping the bar is thirty-one pence out of a twist of paper. Ending: cost.

**The nine words against each other.** Day 1013, Rainmonth 18, a Saturday market, the room over a cooper's shop, taken by the day. **Four of the eight laid out side by side in chalk on a bench-board that has had three sheets on it since the first of a month in Greenmonth, and a ninth line ruled and nothing on it.** A man of about thirty-two with a limp writes a column with four heads: what the thing is, where it is, how often, and a fourth head which is who holds it. **He writes the fourth head last and about four men in the room can see that he writes it last.** He fills the first three and then writes four names in the fourth. A woman of about thirty-nine with a stall of hinges and a daughter of about eleven reads the two lists and says she would not know what to do with either, and says, in the ordinary way, that a form has a head on it that says the name of the person who is to do it, and nobody in that room knows she is talking about the column, and nobody asks her twice. **A mason of about sixty-eight, in a room for the first time in about eleven years, says the whole of it in nine words: it stops being a thing and becomes a man. Nobody agrees with him and nobody improves on it.** He wipes the four names out and leaves the four heads, so that the fourth stands on about nine inches of clean board, and says that he is not going to be the person who decides which of four things is the fourth, and that a column with a head on it and nothing under it is a column anybody can put a name in later, and that this is the worst of the four possibilities. The four go back in the order they were sung. The cost of the day is fifty-six pence and the biggest single item is a cart that went about nine miles and came about nine miles, out of a yard about nine miles off, to bring a man who did not work. Ending: decision.

**The block's last page.** Day 1014, Rainmonth 19, a Sunday and not a market day, in four places two days, about thirty miles, about nine miles and about two hundred miles apart, and the four are not added. **A Sunday is the day the county does not trade, the bench did not sit, the room over the cooper's was let to nobody, and the room the bench keeps its minutes in was open because it is open every day of the week and a man of about fifty-eight came in to close a window and there was nothing to close up.** Then four mornings, in three paragraphs that do not touch: a township where a chair a hat fills once a year was empty because the man who fills it takes a drove to a market a day and a half off and has gone on the first of a month since he was nineteen; a fold where a man of about forty-one mended a hurdle and a boy of about nine set a stone back leaning, and at about the four hundredth yard four generations have all walked a foot to the left of where a man with a chain would put it, and the man of about forty-one is the only one in that fold who knows it; a dale where a hurdle went across a lane twice and about nine hundred people got a Sunday done; **and two hundred miles off, a room over a weigh-house with a door on a lane, a day-book of nine entries of a thing nobody asked that was not opened, a press standing open since a day nobody will date, and nine words in a drawer with about four hundred other returns that have been there about six weeks and have not been answered.** **And the finding: nine counties are running on four things a person can point at in the rain and a set of strings behind them, and not one of the people who hold any of it is on a list, and no two of them has ever met another of them, and the whole arrangement is in nine mouths and one board with no head on it and one boy's own book of about four hundred entries that he cannot read, and a man of about thirty-two with a limp is the only person alive who knows all of it, and there is no form in nine counties with his trade against any of it.** The cost of that Sunday is a column and it is in one place and it is the year and not the Sunday. Ending: discovery.

### The ten endings, as a list

1. **Ch 411 · decision** — four answers in a room, four numbers rubbed out inside four minutes, and a man who is not going to be able to ask any of the four the same question twice.
2. **Ch 412 · cost** — a name that stayed in a hat while the man it is for had already said no, and a chair that is empty three months in every twelve, and a room that has just become the only room in nine counties that says a thing out loud on purpose.
3. **Ch 413 · cost** — one line in a book that says a person stood in the nine feet and said a thing, and the thing not written, and a county that wants a place and a figure and a trade for a road and cannot have any of the three.
4. **Ch 414 · cost** — nine words at a hurdle, a man who took the long way round past the chapel, and the finding that a place with no gate is not a refusal and never was.
5. **Ch 415 · discovery** — three sheets folded in four in a clock case above the milking pails, a list of nine places that is not a list of people, and a fold of paper found in the wrong pocket.
6. **Ch 416 · discovery** — nine words said to a wall about a job that were the answer to a question from a coast, and nobody carrying them from a field to a drawer.
7. **Ch 417 · decision** — nine words said twice and a boy who asked two questions and the second one about his mother, and a pair of second-hand boots that are not in the boundary's column.
8. **Ch 418 · cost** — about a hundred and thirty up-days, about ninety-eight paid days, a Sunday that is not paid for, and a man who stopped at a bar and could not say afterwards why he did not ask.
9. **Ch 419 · decision** — four heads and nine inches of clean board, and the worst of four possibilities chosen in about four seconds.
10. **Ch 420 · discovery** — four places on a Sunday, four mornings that do not touch, and a man of about thirty-two with a limp who is the only person who knows any of it and is on no list.

### What the block did not do, one line each

- It did not answer the question that came back off a road, and the block's title chapter is the one where the answer exists in the world and does not reach the drawer.
- It did not soften the leak, un-say the ninth word, have anybody regret it in a speech, or have the protagonist call the distribution his.
- It did not resolve the shut road four hundred miles off and did not schedule its reopening, and no character in that place is on a page.
- It did not answer the offer and the offer does not appear, and it did not resolve the about eleven hundred, the two villages or the corridor's rule, and it did not print the corridor's rate.
- It did not restate the want of the woman of about twenty-nine beyond the ordinary courtesy of asking a person a question, and it did not ask her twice.
- It did not repair the breach between the two men, did not print the barred kin-term, and did not put a man of twenty-five and a yard anywhere in the ten chapters.
- It did not report the span built over nineteen mornings as a repair and the span does not appear.
- It did not have anybody overrule anybody, did not have him say a word about having not overruled anybody, and it contains no interior monologue about how hard it was to leave the four governances alone.
- It did not have anybody agree with the nine words that say a column turns a thing into a man, and it did not improve on them.
- It did not put the four practices in one column and call it a success; the column is on a bench-board with a head on it and nothing under it.
- It did not name the antagonist, did not revive the Volume 08 cooperation, and nothing in it is a reconciliation or a reunion.
- It did not put the city, the valley, the roadmark, the press's opening day, the leaf with a seventh line that says *a person*, either line in a stone, the marginal instruction, the word in front of a place name, or the account of the worst winter in eighteen years on a page.
- It did not explain the mechanism, print a System panel, make a cut or schedule one, and a sweep of the ten files returns zero for *stage*, *ledger* and *qualification*.
- It did not print the fifth unattributed two-line statement; the budget is four of four and spent.
- It did not move the count on the page under the fourth step, did not add a tenth asking or a tenth not-asking, did not add an eighth register entry, and did not add a nineteenth speech figure to a class that is at its number.
- It did not merge the three bars; the kerb bar and the market bar at Marl are named in Chapter 418 only to be held apart, and the fourth-months bar four miles west of Marl is the only one it touches.
- It did not take the Hushwater thread, and neither the living hamlet's near-sixty nor the erased eighty is printed or compared.
- It did not spend Ivo Serrat's arithmetic, did not have anybody read the boy's book out loud, did not identify the ninth mark, and did not point at the mason's mark on the ninth voussoir.
- It did not schedule the tide road, and the only mention of a coast in ten chapters is the man of about thirty-four about two days off one, which was already on the page.
- It did not give the mason of about sixty-eight a day's work, and his eleven years are exactly where he left them.
- It did not print a new name, so the volume's name budget stands at three spent of five and two unfilled.

### The review repair to this block, what changed in each chapter and what did not

**A review ran over Batch 0002 after it closed and returned fifteen findings. Twelve were real, every one is repaired in place, no planned movement in the block was changed, and no chapter was rewritten around a repair. The findings and the arithmetic are in `state/continuity.md` 1031 and the review record is `reviews/volume-09/phase-next-0003.md`. Three were process, and one of those is now in the state files and two are runner-owned and were not touched.**

- **412** — the chair's empty months. Nine names a year at a month each fills nine months of twelve, and the chapter closed on a month in every nine. **It is three months in every twelve, and nobody in that township can tell you which three.** The same wrong figure was in three state files and is corrected in all three. Nothing else in the chapter moved.
- **413** — the office's output. About four hundred sheets a month, of which about nine hundred carried a place, a figure and a trade. **About nine hundred a month, of which about nine have nine words on them and the other about eight hundred and ninety have a place, a figure and a trade on them**, which is the two nines the paragraph is built on and which foots. The seventeen-pence column in §Four and the nine figures and the ruled ninth line are untouched.
- **416** — the forty miles. The rate of four shillings and sixpence a day was said to have been named in a field at an arch forty miles off. **The arch is nine miles off the kilns and the forty is the mason's**, as Chapter 403 has it, and the sentence now attaches each to the right one. The hundred and thirty-five pence is untouched.
- **418** — four repairs in one chapter and the chapter is the one that needed them. **The pocket is one twist less tonight than on Friday**, because the month's column came out of a twist and nothing has come in since, and §One's statement that a Sunday pays nothing is unchanged and is what makes the correction necessary. **The man has been on that bar for two hundred and twenty-eight days**, which is the figure the derivation above it runs on, and the eight people have been right or wrong about the date for **about a hundred and eight days**, which is the twenty-fourth of a month in Thawmonth. **The ditch is given a duration instead of the middle of a month thirty-three days in the future**, and the sentence no longer uses a future month as the cause of a winter. **And the sheet is no longer on the end of that bar in §Four**, only under the stone on the bench, which is where §Two already had it. The thirty-one pence column is untouched, the fifty-eight days the sheet has been on the bench are untouched, and the fifty-four carts and the nine-hundredweight load are untouched.
- **419** — two repairs. **The market where about forty people heard the ninth line is about two hundred miles from the room with a door on a lane** and not four hundred, which is the shut road's distance and belongs to a place four hundred miles off that nobody in this volume is sent to. **And the mason's rate is named in a field at a beck nine miles off the kilns, with the forty miles attached to the man**, as in 416. The four heads, the nine inches of clean board, the four names wiped off and the fifty-six pence are untouched.

The four places, the four paragraphs that do not touch, the seventy pence, the boots as a line item and the four that a person can point at in the rain are untouched.
- **Batch 0001, Chapter 404** — one word of arithmetic, and it is the reason this repair ran at all. **The banded bundle the keeper of records came down on the cart with is about a hundred and four sheets and not about four hundred**, which is what Chapter 404 line 91 already said and what the block's own repair record claimed was done nine lines earlier and was not.
- **What was not repaired, and why.** The middle of Harvestmonth also stands in Chapters 401 and 408, where it is defensible only as a month of the year before and re-deriving the calendar is not a repair pass's job. **The rule is written into the next prompt's §7 instead, and a month name that cannot be resolved to a day is to be changed into a duration.**
