## Volume 09 Batch 0004, Chapters 431 to 440 · Block 4 · The Door That Is Not Locked

**Days 1057 to 1098, the second day of Fallowmonth to the thirteenth of Embermonth, YR 318, forty-two days inclusive, two of the ten chapters on a market morning, being Chapter 433 and Chapter 436. The frame is derived twice from the single anchor of day 930 and the two halves are reported as two figures in `state/continuity.md` 1066.**

**Ch 431 · the second of Fallowmonth, day 1057, a Monday, not a market day · a paved yard behind a house of assizes in a town of about nine hundred people · the yard, the slate, and the woman who is not alive.** About eleven men are clearing the yard for a circuit three weeks off and not one of them is on anybody's strength. A twelfth man stands by the east wall with a rolled thing under his arm and has come in by a door in the house, and the first minute of that is sixteen words between two men. The building is two rooms against the north wall, about four hundred years older than it, with a door that has had no lock since before the last woman died, dropped half an inch on a dressed sill stone about four feet long. A slate is set in the yard wall six feet off the corner, a name in letters an inch high and two lines under it and a month, a day and a year on the second of those, and about nine people in that town could say what is cut there, and the man on the stick does not. The finding is that the building has never been on the clerk's schedule of nine things, because every list is a list of what somebody is answerable for and nobody has been answerable for a shed in a yard in four hundred years. The door comes off its sill in a second and a half and the crowbar goes back on its bracket inside two minutes. **Ending: discovery.**

**Ch 432 · the second and third of Fallowmonth, days 1057 and 1058, a Monday and a Tuesday, neither a market day · the same yard · what the four men do, in the ordinary way, over two days.** Nothing is burnt, nothing is read, nothing is said that is not true, and nobody is lied to. The day-book falls open where a day-book falls open and there is no page they wanted, and a day-book is a record of days and not of shelves, so the paper with the seal on it can only be answered off the last line in the book. They take the loose run off the top board between the third hour and the seventh hour and count it twice, nineteen, fifteen and seven off the shelf and twenty-two, thirteen and six into the satchel, six figures and not one of them the same twice, and both counts foot to forty-one. The man of about thirty-four with a sheet says, in twenty-seven words, that he will not write which of the three the paper wants. He then writes the receipt in a fair hand on the back of the sealed paper, and copies the three things off the last line in the book, a place, a figure of men and a trade, in that order, and leaves it under the door. **A man of thirty-two reads it over his shoulder at the eighth hour and thinks nothing about it at all.** **Ending: cost.**

**Ch 433 · the seventh of Fallowmonth, day 1062, a Saturday, a market morning · a yard of four lime kilns two days west of Merefen · the first of the eleven, a woman of about forty-four, and what she has been told by nine people in four days.** She was told on the third at the gate by a man with a cart of lime, on the fourth on her own doorstep by a woman of about thirty-four, on the fifth in a yard in the middle of an argument by a man who buys stone, and by six more, of whom three said the same, two said it wrong and one added a boy and a well. The thing that made it go nine times in four days is not that it is strange. **It is that it is a door with no lock on it.** She says one sentence to the man with the stick, in twenty-three words, that she has not been to that yard, has not sent anybody, and does not want to know whose it is, and she was not defending herself because nobody had said she had done it. **Ending: discovery.**

**Ch 434 · the thirteenth of Fallowmonth, day 1068, a Friday, not a market day · a boundary of two miles and nine stones in a fold thirty miles south · the second, a man of about forty-one, and what a boundary does with a thing that is not on it.** He is asked a question about a drove and answers what a cart on the Monday and a man on foot on the Tuesday give him, and then he says, in twenty-one words, that he can tell you what came over and cannot tell you what anybody did on the far side of it, and he is right, and it is the answer a boundary gives. **A boundary of two miles and nine stones, walked by a man, is the only record in nine counties that can say whether a person has been there twice.** He goes back to the third stone, puts his hand on it, and walks the rest of it, and on the way he works out what a boundary is for, which is a dispute, and that a boundary asked a second question becomes a list of who comes and goes, and about two hundred people are on nine stones. **Ending: cost.**

**Ch 435 · the sixteenth of Fallowmonth, day 1071, a Monday, not a market day · a single arch over a beck nine miles off the kilns · the third, a mason of about sixty-eight, a mark in the bed of a re-used sill, and a top course nobody sent for.** A man of about twenty-nine who carts lime tells him about the yard in the ordinary way, in forty-two words about the stone, and does not know he has told a mason anything. A dressed stone about four feet long, dressed on four sides because it is going to be seen from below, came off something better. **The underside of it carries a figure cut with the flat of a cold chisel, and it is not a signature, and a figure cut in a stone bed is a figure with no head on it, and it is the ninth of nine ways of cutting a mark in a stone, and the ninth is a count and not a name.** Nobody asked him to read it out loud and he tells the man with the stick only that a thing was said about a door. Then at the second hour of the next morning he gets under his own arch in about eleven inches of water and puts his hand on his own mark, and about nine feet of the top course on the north approach is a hand's width out of level, and he knows what that wants, and he says nothing. **Ending: discovery.**

**Ch 436 · the nineteenth of Fallowmonth, day 1074, a Thursday, a market morning · a roadhouse nine miles on from the kilns · the fourth, a woman of about fifty, and what a number with no head is worth when a man's name goes on a receipt.** Four feet of board, no head on it, about three hundred and seventy figures down a column about forty inches long, thirty-one years, three hands, and about four of them at the foot in the hand of a man who was about twenty-three eleven years ago and came in four days a week with glass. She was handed a copy of the receipt on the second of Fallowmonth, late in the day, with seven words, because she is the only person on eleven miles of that road who hands a stranger a written thing. **She cannot read the words and read the hand in about nine minutes.** A figure with no head cannot be refused, because there is no line to refuse it on, and cannot be handed back, because there is nothing to put in the hand. **She is the only person alive who could say which of the three hundred and seventy figures belong to the man who wrote the receipt, and she knew before he came in, and she has not said it and is not going to.** **Ending: cost.**

**Ch 437 · the twenty-third of Fallowmonth, day 1078, a Monday, not a market day · the same yard, bare to the middle, with the circuit sitting in that town that afternoon · the transfer, witnessed and not conducted.** Nine packets, a stool, a day-book and a dressed stone go out on two handcarts in about an hour, and nobody burnt it, nobody read it and nobody counted it, and a second line went under the receipt saying the building was cleared and not saying what went out of it. The man of about thirty-eight with a crowbar gets the left handcart four feet and stops and says, in eleven words, that a stranger could take that end and save a trip. **He said no, in one word, and did not give a reason, and the reason he did not give a reason is that all four of the possible reasons were about himself: a man who helped is a man who was there on purpose, and a witness is a thing a person is and not a thing a person is put in.** And on that day he works out that he is the connection, in about nine feet of a yard, and says nothing to anybody and adds no line to a page of eleven lines with no words on any of them. **Ending: decision.**

**Ch 438 · the twenty-fifth of Fallowmonth, day 1080, a Wednesday, not a market day · a roadhouse, a yard and a bar four miles west, about eleven miles apart · the fifth, the sixth and the seventh of the eleven, and the distribution at last.** Four copies went out of that yard at dusk on the second, to every person who had come in from off the road, and the man who wrote them does not know how many he wrote out. A woman of about thirty-seven keeps hers in a till box under a pound of silver and has shown it to four people who cannot read. A man of about thirty-three with a barrow opens his on top of a fold of paper that has been in the same pocket for eighty-one days, and **it is the most complete piece of paper about anything that has ever been handed to him and there is not one thing on it he can do anything with**, and he does not open the other one, and the rule that not looking is the same as not saying held. A man of about nineteen cannot read the third and has not asked anybody to. **And the bar is counted: three hundred and one days, forty-three weeks exactly, a hundred and twenty-nine paid days, a hundred and twenty-nine twists of nine pence, twenty-four marks and nine pence, printed outside the column and added to nothing.** **Ending: discovery.**

**Ch 439 · the twenty-ninth of Fallowmonth, day 1084, a Sunday, not a market day · a yard, a gate of a lane, and a doorway · the eighth, the ninth, the tenth and the eleventh, and a boy who finds out a thing by counting.** Three counts made that Sunday within a mile of each other, none for any reason, and none of the three knowing the others were counting: nine carts into the yard between the second and the tenth, one of which went in with a cart and came out with a satchel; forty-one sheets go on a handcart as eight fives and one; and four men in that yard on the second, four on the twenty-third, and about eleven on nine mornings in between. **Ivo Serrat, seventeen, counts nine worn places on the lower board of a shelf, and reasons that a string does not wear a board, and works out that about nine times about forty is about three hundred and sixty, and says it out loud to nobody in twenty-two words, and writes it in a book of about four hundred entries he made himself.** **And then the last arithmetic anybody does in nine counties this year: four men brought a cart to move about one basketful, counted it twice, and a boy of seventeen has been carrying a basketful nine miles four days a week since he was thirteen. A man who is in a hurry does not count a basket twice, and a man who does not know what is on a shelf does not bring a cart.** That is why nobody in nine counties is ever going to be told who sent the four men, and why the man who sent them is not going to be found. **Ending: cost.**

**Ch 440 · the thirteenth of Embermonth, day 1098, a Sunday, not a market day · a room taken by the day over a shop in a market town on the salt · the block's last page, a fortnight on.** The taking is in nine counties and eleven places and no two of the eleven have been in a room with one another, and the only person alive who knows how many there are is a man of thirty-two with a limp, and he is on no list, and there is no list. **Nobody in nine counties can say who sent four men with a paper and a crowbar to a door that was not locked, and that is not a failure of enquiry**, and the reason is the boy's arithmetic and a seal and a name on a piece of paper and an office that is a room four hundred miles off. **The receipt is in nine counties in a fair hand and it is accurate, and a man who reads it gets a place and a figure of men and a trade and a name at the foot of it, in that order, and a record with a place and a figure and a trade on it is the thing nine counties have spent a year arguing against**, and he has had that in his head for forty-one days and has not said a word of it to anybody. A man of about twenty comes up the stairs at the eighth hour with an unpaid fire and he gives it to him anyway. Then he sits down and decides not to make a list, **and the reason is not a reason anybody would call decent, because a list of eleven people in nine places is a column, and a column can be picked up by the next man with a form, and a man with a form is not a bad man and is doing his work.** **Ending: decision.**

### The ten endings, as a list

431 discovery · 432 cost · 433 discovery · 434 cost · 435 discovery · 436 cost · 437 decision · 438 discovery · 439 cost · 440 decision.

### What the block did not do, one line each, including the things it noticed and did not do

It did not name the antagonist, and it did not revive the Volume 08 cooperation, and nothing in it is a reconciliation or a reunion. It did not print a fifth unattributed two-line statement, the budget being four of four and spent. It did not print the word that names the founding paper in any construction, in a document, in a quotation, in a mouth or in narration. It did not print a second System panel, and a stage word, a ledger word and a qualification word are at zero, and no chapter says the ledger does or does not move. It did not make the three invented things a lie, a forgery, a fiction or anybody's work, and it did not have the taking turn them into one either. It did not convict the man of about thirty-four with a sheet, did not have him explain himself, and did not say in print whether his being one of the four is connected to the place, the figure and the trade he invented in Batch 0003, and that connection is the volume's and not a chapter's. It did not have the protagonist conduct the transfer, get the sheets back, overrule anybody, or be the reason it works. It did not have anybody find the bench-board, and it did not have anybody read it. It did not add an enemy, an office, a council, an order, a mechanism, or a person with a want that is about this volume. It did not reconcile the refusal figures, add any two of them, or add any of them to a figure of persons, and the two nines and the register's seven questions and the unattributed statement's four all stand where they stood. It did not move the count on the page under the fourth step, and it did not print a tenth asking of that bench; it introduced a clerk's schedule of nine things in a yard, which is a different nine and is a list of what a court is answerable for, and did not put a tenth thing on it. It did not merge the three bars, and it touched only the bar four miles west, and it called that one a bar and not a kerb. It did not resolve the shut road four hundred miles off, the offer, the about eleven hundred, the two villages, the corridor's rule or its rate, or the want of either woman of about twenty-nine or thirty-nine. It did not reopen the city, the valley, the roadmark, the mechanism, the leaf with a seventh line that says *a person*, either line in a stone, the marginal instruction, the word in front of a place name, or the account of the worst winter in eighteen years. It did not print the thirteen unlooked-at items, did not take the Hushwater thread, and printed neither the living hamlet's figure nor the erased one. It did not repair the breach between the two men in a scene, an aside, a document or an apology, and nobody walks a road with anybody for pleasure. It did not answer the question from a road, and it did not have the keeper of records answer it; it did not have anybody remark on the four unpaid things of Chapter 428, and it did not turn the first of a month inside the volume into a meeting. It did not explain the tenth mark, point at the mason's mark, or ask the boy to read his book out loud, and it did not re-spend his identification, which was made in Batch 0003 and is closed. It did not improve on, refuse, rank or make lose any of the four governances or the fifth answer of a room let by the day. It did not schedule a cut, did not ring the bell, did not date a press, and did not renew a second oath-stone. It did not say to the man of about nineteen when his four months came out. It did not give anybody the protagonist's sheet, lose it, or let it be read, and no clerk asked him for it. It did not spend a name; it spent none of the two slots that remain, and a capitalised two-word sweep over the ten files returns `Ivo Serrat` and one sentence opening.

## Volume 09, Batch 0005 — Chapters 441 to 450, "A Council With No Crown On It" — the close

**Days 1099 to 1140, the fourteenth of Embermonth to the twenty-fifth of Wolfmonth, YR 318, forty-two days inclusive, a Monday to a Sunday. Ten chapter days: 1099, 1103, 1104, 1110, 1113, 1115, 1126, 1130, 1133, 1140. Two market mornings, Chapter 443 and Chapter 448. The whole of Volume 09 is YR 318 and the year does not turn. Batch 0005 is 20,303 words in ten chapters, counted file by file on a plain whitespace split with the title line included and recounted after a third pass over the finished volume, the second pass having had 20,292, and the volume is 98,081 in fifty chapters and the manuscript is 1,387,335 in four hundred and fifty chapters. The block's argument in one sentence: *a thing nine people can be asked about is a thing with a handle on it, and the only kind of record this book has spent a year arguing for is one that nobody has to answer to, and so nine people met eight times in a room over a store and then agreed not to meet again, and the one thing they fixed was a name in the mouth of a man who is not keeping it.* No System panel, no stage, ledger or qualification word, no name spent, and the antagonist is not named.**

**441 · day 1099, the fourteenth of Embermonth, a Monday and not a market day · 2,089 words.** Nine people are in a meeting room over a store in a township of about nine hundred people, eleven paces by nineteen, with a chair on a platform at the top end and a hat on a shelf behind the door, both about a hundred and forty years old. Eight of the nine had said no before they came and the ninth says no in the doorway, and the nine refusals are not one refusal said nine times and there is nothing in the room that all nine have refused. A man of about thirty-two with a limp is the tenth and told four of them himself. A man of about twenty-two with a handcart says he is not the ninth of nine because he is hired by the hour and has not got a shelf. A woman of about forty-four at a kiln refuses to be the person anybody can be sent to. A man of about forty-one with a boundary refuses to say where nine stones are. A mason of about sixty-eight refuses to be asked in a room. A man of about thirty-three with a barrow refuses to open a paper in a coat that is not his. The chapter's inventory paragraph sets down a chair, a hat, a door, a boundary, a day-book and a board with no head on it, and none of the six is touched. Column: **30 pence**.

**442 · day 1103, the eighteenth of Embermonth, a Friday and not a market day · 1,746 words.** A woman of about fifty-eight comes with a slate and asks that a number of men in a yard be written down once, anywhere, by anybody, and says she is fifty-eight and her sister is sixty-two and cannot read. A woman of about fifty with a board asks what the paper would be for, and the man with the handcart answers that it would be for whoever is not in the room. A woman of about seventeen asks to have the room's own hundred-and-forty-year-old book for two days, and says why, and is refused, and the woman of about sixty-three who keeps it is four miles off and is not coming. The nine agree, in seven words, that nothing about it goes on any sheet, and the man with the handcart says in forty-seven words what that costs. Four sheets of paper are bought and nothing is written on any of them. Column: **53 pence**.

**443 · day 1104, the nineteenth of Embermonth, a Saturday and a market day · 1,843 words.** The ninth of the nine is a name and the only one of the nine that is. Eight are things people do and are set out in prose, and the ninth is the name of a nine-mile road, which is not a place and cannot go on a form. A woman of about fifty with a board says the word and the man of about twenty-two with a handcart says it back three times, then says he is not keeping it and has nowhere to write it. The mason of about sixty-eight is asked and says no, and the man of about thirty-four with a net does not say the word twice to get a second person to say it. The close of the chapter prints the collision between the road and the invented place, which have the same nine letters, and nobody in the room remarks on it. Column: **39 pence**.

**444 · day 1110, the twenty-fifth of Embermonth, a Friday and not a market day · 1,925 words.** A woman of about seventeen says three things and is asked nothing: a road nine miles long about nine miles west of the room, a gate at the end of it for about four hundred years, and a woman on the far side of that gate who is her mother. She gives no figure for the far side, gives the reason in eight words, does not say how she knows, and does not show a book of about four hundred entries. A man of about twenty-two with a handcart says in sixty-seven words that he is the one person who could go and stand at the gate and is not going to. Nobody sent anybody and nothing was opened. A man of about thirty-two with a limp says nothing at all, and the chapter says exactly why. Column: **63 pence**.

**445 · day 1113, the twenty-eighth of Embermonth, a Monday and not a market day · 1,910 words.** A man of about nineteen at a bar and a woman of about seventeen who carries glass ask a man of about thirty-two with a limp to leave, in a hundred and twenty-six words and a hundred and nineteen words, and both reasons are correct and neither is about him. He says in six words that he will be gone on Wednesday and does not argue. The chapter then sets out in full the argument he did not make, and says that the room is never going to hear any of it. Nobody argued with him and nobody defended him. A man of about thirty-four with a net opened his mouth twice and shut it. Column: **65 pence**.

**446 · days 1115 and 1117, the thirtieth of Embermonth, a Wednesday, and the second of Wolfmonth, a Friday · 1,943 words.** He leaves at the eleventh hour and walks out of the township, and the nine carry on for nine minutes and then stop and then start again. In a fen town of about four thousand people he goes up a street of working yards past an open gate where a man of about twenty-five has a barrow with a wheel out of it and a pin that will not go back in, and a man of about thirty-two with a limp can see from nine feet that the axle is soft, and he keeps walking. There is no word in the chapter for what the two of them are. A barrow with a wheel on it goes up the street at the fourth hour and does not stop. The room was let on the Thursday to a woman of about forty-one, and a coin went into a box on the shelf and not into the book. Column: **71 pence**.

**447 · day 1126, the eleventh of Wolfmonth, a Sunday and not a market day · 2,152 words.** A chapter with no protagonist in it. A drawer in a room over a weigh-house with a door on a lane holds about four hundred returns, and one of them has nine words on it, no letter, no seal and a place in the county on the back: *Is the first of the eight still done anywhere.* It went in on the fourteenth of a month in Longlight and on that Sunday it has been there a hundred and forty-seven days, which is twenty-one weeks exactly, and the figure the handoff carries is the day-1140 one of a hundred and sixty-one days. The keeper of records, twenty-nine and four years and a half, does not know what the first of the eight is, and the answer is nine miles from her door in a mason who has asked the far side of a wall about four hundred times. Her decision, told to nobody: she writes four lines describing what the room holds, puts no name at the foot, and puts them in the bag with the letters. Column: **97 pence**, and it is hers.

**448 · day 1130, the fifteenth of Wolfmonth, a Thursday and a market day · 2,120 words.** The bar four miles west of Marl is down on a Thursday and the boy of about nineteen is at the market two miles east of it, and he works his own figure out on the back of his hand: three hundred and fifty-one days, fifty weeks and one day, a hundred and fifty-one paid days, one thousand three hundred and fifty-nine pence, twenty-eight marks and fifteen pence, and it cross-checks from Chapter 0438. The sheet under a stone is a hundred and eighty-one days old and the paper in a coat on a barrow is a hundred and thirty-one days old and neither is looked at. A trade, a figure of four hundred and seventy, a sheet in a coat and one sentence not carried four miles are all in that market and no one remarks on any of them. A man of about thirty-three with a barrow walks four miles to say nine words about the boy's four months, and the boy does nothing with it. Column: **75 pence**.

**449 · day 1133, the eighteenth of Wolfmonth, a Sunday and not a market day · 2,054 words.** Nine people and no tenth, and they have nothing to do, and it is the first time in about a month that any of them has said a sentence that was not about a thing somebody asked for. The room does what the room does, and the nine do not take it over. A man of about thirty-four with a net says in a room that a receipt is a record with a place, a number of men and a trade on it, in that order, and nine of them talk about it and not one agrees. A woman of about fifty with the only hand in nine counties in her head is in that room and is not asked and is not thanked. The chapter then lists what nine people can do, which is meet and refuse, and what they cannot do, which is hold, keep a book, or add anything to anything, including three different nines. Column: **88 pence**, and it is the man of about thirty-four with a net's and it went in a box on the shelf.

**450 · day 1140, the twenty-fifth of Wolfmonth, a Sunday and not a market day · 2,510 words.** The last page of the volume. Three of the nine carry a number into the room that they did not want to carry: the boy of nineteen brings the bar's own figure, derived again for this day, and the two papers behind him are a hundred and ninety-one and a hundred and forty-one days old. The nine decide, in about nine minutes, that there is no ninth meeting and no tenth, and that if two of them ever stand in a room together it will be because they turned up for something else. The woman of about fifty-eight with the slate is against it and says so, and says she will not be thanked. A woman of about seventeen asks the man of about twenty-two with a handcart for the ninth thing and he says it, and a man of about nineteen is asked second and says no in twenty-nine words. The six objects are set down again and none is improved on. The room is given up at dusk and the keeper goes home, and about nine miles west a man of about nineteen puts his hand on a lever, and the twenty-sixth of Wolfmonth is a Monday. Column: **89 pence**, and it is the handcart man's.

### The ten endings, as a list

1. **441** — eleven words, a stool by a door, a notebook of eleven lines, and no list.
2. **442** — four sheets of paper with nothing on any of them and a basket of glass put down in a passage.
3. **443** — nine letters that are two different things, and a sheet of nine inches by six that was not taken out.
4. **444** — a gate exactly where it was, and a number of people who know it, and no figure of the far side on anything.
5. **445** — a form on which no man is entered as somebody who was asked to go.
6. **446** — a barrow going up a street at the fourth hour, and a door, and a hundred and forty yards of walking in one direction.
7. **447** — four lines on a sheet with no name at the foot of it, in a bag with the letters, and a question still in a drawer.
8. **448** — a man of about nineteen who looked at the middle of the street for about four seconds and then picked his basket up.
9. **449** — a chair empty on a platform, a hat on a shelf with four names in it, and no form in the kingdom on which that would go.
10. **450** — a lever and a fulcrum, cold, and a Monday, and not one crown on anything in that township or on that road.

### What the block did not do, one line each, including the things it noticed and did not do

It did not name the antagonist, and it did not revive the Volume 08 cooperation, and nothing in it is a reconciliation or a reunion, and the leaving in Chapter 446 is not the breach being repaired. It did not print a third of the barred kin-term. It did not print a fifth unattributed two-line statement. It did not print the word that names the founding paper in any construction. It did not print a tenth line of the song, and did not un-say the ninth word, and did not soften the leak, and nobody regretted it in a speech, and it did not reprint the twenty-word engine sentence. It did not print a second System panel, a stage word, a ledger word or a qualification word, and it did not let a chapter say that the ledger does or does not move. It did not open anything and it did not schedule a reopening of anything, and the gate at the end of nine miles of road is where it was. It did not resolve the shut road four hundred miles off, the offer, the about eleven hundred, the two villages, the corridor's rule or its rate, or the want of either woman of about twenty-nine or thirty-nine, and it did not answer the offer with a figure. It did not answer the question from a road and it did not have the keeper of records answer it. It did not repair the breach between the two men in a scene, an aside, a document or an apology, it did not report the span as a repair, and it did not print the word for what the two of them are. It did not improve on, refuse, rank or make lose any of the four governances or the fifth answer, and it did not present the nine as their answer. It did not add a tenth asking of the bench under the fourth step, a tenth not-asking, an eighth register entry, a ninth mark's explanation, or a tenth thing to any of the three nines, and it added none of the nines to each other or to a figure of persons. It did not take Hushwater and printed neither the living hamlet's estimate nor the erased village's. It did not merge the three bars and touched only the fourth-months bar four miles west. It did not have anybody find or read the bench-board, and the ninth line on it is still ruled and empty. It did not make any of the three invented things a lie, a forgery, a fiction or anybody's work, and nobody corrected any of them, and the receipt did not convict the man of about thirty-four with a sheet, and it did not say whether his inventing a place, a figure and a trade is connected to his being one of the four men with a crowbar. It did not have the protagonist conduct anything, get anything back, overrule anybody, or be the reason a thing works, and it gave him no sheet and asked him for no sheet. It did not point at the mason's mark, did not ask the boy of seventeen to read his book, and did not re-spend his arithmetic, which it states and does not show the making of. It did not put the nine in one column with each other or with the third collection in his coat, and it did not print their counts together. It did not date the press, ring the bell, renew an oath-stone, or schedule the seed vault or Chapter 900. It noticed that the volume's own calendar table is one day out on three rows and derived all three instead of copying them, and left the table for whoever owns the outline. It noticed that a man of about fifty-eight with a slate is the one the ending costs most and did not remark on it. It noticed that a woman of about fifty has the only hand in nine counties in her head and put her in a room and did not thank her. It noticed that the question from a road had an answer nine miles from a drawer for twenty-three weeks and left both where they were. It noticed that nine people had met and did not make a council of it.

### The third pass over the close, one line for the three chapters it touched, and the record at `state/continuity.md` 1102

A phase that inherited the ten finished chapters and the five state files repaired the review of the second pass. Three chapters, three lines, and nothing else moved: no dateline, no card, no plot beat, no ending, no money total, no name.

- **441** — the sentence about how the other five came now says the room *had* said a thing out loud on purpose *once before this*, which is what continuity 1101 recorded as the intent and certified as done, where the second pass had left a clause with no main clause, a present perfect in a past-perfect frame, and a reading of Chapter 441 as the second meeting of the nine on the first of the eight.
- **444** — the sentence closing Section Three now resolves both of its referents: a name that is not a practice went into that mouth on the nineteenth of this month and that speech came out of it on the twenty-fifth, which is six days. The name is not printed. The two speech figures, 67 and 168, were not touched and still foot.
- **450** — the boy's start is *fifty-one weeks and four days and four days short of a full year* again, 361 days against 365, where the second pass had printed it as *one day over a year*. Every other figure in that sentence, 204 up-days, 51 Sundays, 153, 155, 1,395 pence and 29 marks 3 pence, was already right at 361 and was left alone, and the method behind it reproduces all six of its canon figures.

## Volume 09 Close — Chapters 401 to 450, and the volume as a finished book

**This section is written by the phase that wrote the close record at `outline/volumes/volume-09-close.md`, and it is the per-chapter index for the whole of Volume 09. The five batch sections above are the phases that wrote the chapters; this one is the phase that read all fifty, and where the two differ about a figure, the close record and this index are right and the batch section is stale, and the standing reason is that a state file is the least reliable document in this repository and a chapter file is not.**

**Volume 09 — The Nine Names (Chapters 401–450).** Day 931 to day 1140, two hundred and ten days inclusive, the whole of it YR 318 and the year does not turn. **98,079 words in fifty chapters, counted file by file with the title line included, after this close's own repairs; the figure on arrival was 98,081 and the two words are itemised at `volume-09-close.md` §11.1.**

| Block | Chapters | Content | Days |
| ---: | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | 401–410 | **Nine Words and a Road.** A kiln draw, a song of nine lines on the beat of a bar, eight practices and one road, a mason of sixty-eight and a ninth voussoir, a boy of seventeen with a book he has never shown, the plan for nine copies, the reason he cannot hold the ninth said out loud in ninety-four words and believed, a bar four miles west and a boy who counts twists and not days, a keeper of forty who has held one since he was eleven, a market Saturday and about forty people who heard it, and eight words into eight places done by a woman. | 931–972 |
| 2 | 411–420 | **Four Words and Four Answers.** Four practices offered to four people who each have a governance already, and the four governances are hereditary custody, a rotating clerk, a public lot and no gate at all, and none of the four is wrong. A bench asked nothing. A boundary of two miles and nine stones. The first of Longlight and a clerk of sixty with two kinds of minute and no third. | 973–1014 |
| 3 | 421–430 | **What a Name Costs When It Is Sung.** An office that needs a place, a figure and a trade, a road that has none of the three, and a fortnight in which all three are invented and all three are true. A boy who works out from a worn board that a string does not wear a board. The volume's one System panel, one line, technically true. | 1015–1056 |
| 4 | 431–440 | **The Door That Is Not Locked.** A building nobody was appointed to keep because a woman died in a winter. Four men with a crowbar, a warrant, and no lock to break. Forty-one sheets and an accurate receipt under a door and four copies into four places in about two minutes by people who were not sent. Eleven witnesses in eleven places, none of whom has met the other ten. He is offered one end of a handcart and refuses in one word. | 1057–1098 |
| 5 | 441–450 | **A Council With No Crown On It.** Nine people, nine refusals, eight meetings, seven words not written down, and a decision on the eighth day that there is no ninth meeting and no tenth. A man asked to leave and gone in six words and the argument the room never heard. A name handed to a man who is not keeping it. Four unpaid things in a market and nobody remarking. A Sunday the county does not trade, a room with nine people in it, and nine miles west a lever and a fulcrum that are cold. | 1099–1140 |

**Per chapter, each file counted on its own, after this close's repairs: 401 2,518 · 402 2,353 · 403 2,200 · 404 2,169 · 405 2,331 · 406 2,135 · 407 2,506 · 408 2,262 · 409 2,079 · 410 2,384 · 411 2,046 · 412 2,073 · 413 2,003 · 414 1,748 · 415 1,877 · 416 2,009 · 417 2,058 · 418 2,052 · 419 1,956 · 420 1,952 · 421 1,857 · 422 1,640 · 423 1,762 · 424 1,581 · 425 1,775 · 426 1,636 · 427 1,679 · 428 1,669 · 429 1,696 · 430 1,675 · 431 1,753 · 432 1,786 · 433 1,691 · 434 1,654 · 435 1,670 · 436 1,679 · 437 1,759 · 438 2,054 · 439 1,967 · 440 2,084 · 441 2,082 · 442 1,746 · 443 1,843 · 444 1,941 · 445 1,910 · 446 1,943 · 447 2,152 · 448 2,120 · 449 2,054 · 450 2,512.**

**The thirteen chapters a Volume 10 writer most often needs, and why each of them is one.** **Chapter 0402** is where the eight are written down and the ninth line is ruled and empty, and it is the chapter this close repaired twice. **Chapter 0404** is the volume's best paragraph and the ninety-four words are exact. **Chapter 0407** is the market and the price. **Chapter 0409** is the two kinds of minute and it is the chapter this close repaired once. **Chapter 0410** is the relationship beat, in one plain sentence at the end, and a close that reads it as a reconciliation has spent the volume's only one. **Chapter 0426** holds the volume's one System panel. **Chapter 0427** holds the arithmetic and it is not to be re-spent. **Chapter 0429** holds the bar's money method as a second derivation and it is the chapter this close repaired twice. **Chapter 0437** holds the climax's refusal and the only knowing in the volume that is on the page. **Chapter 0438** holds the method and the four distributions and its cost column foots at sixty-four. **Chapter 0445** holds the argument the room never heard. **Chapter 0447** holds the four lines with no name at the foot and the question in the drawer. **Chapter 0450** holds the volume's last page, and the bar's 361 days, and the clause the third pass had to restore.**

**The six chapters this close repaired, and the class of each.** **Chapter 0402**, a speech-length figure against a printed speech. **Chapter 0404**, an opening speech with no closing mark, which the bold-parity sweep could not see. **Chapter 0405**, a doubled opening mark and a second speech-length figure. **Chapter 0409**, a speech-length figure in a chapter that foots another one exactly. **Chapter 0429**, a fold of paper at thirty-eight days where the anchored start makes it thirty-nine, at two sites. **And twenty-six chapters across Blocks 3, 4 and 5 for a block-boundary change in the punctuation mark, 272 characters.** `state/continuity.md` 1105, 1106.

## Volume 10, Batch 0001 — Chapters 451 to 460, "A Road That Runs Out Twice a Day"

**Days 1141 to 1188, the twenty-sixth of Wolfmonth YR 318 to the thirteenth of Hearthmonth YR 318, a Monday to a Saturday, forty-eight days inclusive, three Saturdays and one Thursday fall on it, and the county's market mornings are at 1144 and 1188, with 1153 a Saturday on which the word market morning belongs to a county about two hundred miles off and not to the yard the chapter is set in. The year does not turn inside this block. Block 0001 is 16,906 words in ten chapters, counted file by file on a plain whitespace split with the title line included, and the manuscript is 1,404,239 in four hundred and sixty chapters, all three recounted after the second review repair and none inherited. The block's argument in one sentence: *a working road produces a keeper's job whether anybody wants one or not, and the only protection anybody has found against that is a road nobody can be found about, and about nine hundred people are using one.* Nothing is merged, nothing is opened, no System panel appears, no stage or ledger or qualification word appears, the antagonist is not named, and three names were spent of a budget of five.**

**Ch 451 · the twenty-sixth of Wolfmonth, a Monday, not a market day · a room over a weigh-house with a door on a lane, a county about two hundred miles off a street of working yards · a letter that asks for a keeper.** A folded letter with a brown seal, eleven days on a road, addressed to a keeper of records, **and what it asks for in nine words on the first side and a list on the second is a keeper for a connection between three roads that have never been joined and whose owners have never met.** **Rusen Ball, 29**, named, at a factor's table in a fen town of about four thousand, coat gone at the right cuff, got one thing right in nine days on his own: he came to the only room in nine counties that answers letters about things with nobody's name on them. She does not fill one in. **The form is three heads cut about forty years ago — a place, a figure of men, a trade — and she says the order out loud because he has not and because it is cheaper in a room than in a corridor.** The problem is not that a road has no place, as in Volume 09; **the trouble is the figure: nine hundred is a figure of households and a clerk on a shore will not sign households on a line that says men.** He offers to hold the middle, in twenty-eight words in one turn of one speech, **and it is the second time in about six months he has offered to be the person a thing is got at**, and she tells the reason out loud and it is a new one: **a man who stands in the middle of three places is a man who can be sent for to all three.** Nobody offers a third thing. Cost: **24 pence.** Ending: the letter stays on the bench.

**Ch 452 · the twenty-ninth of Wolfmonth, a Thursday, a market morning · a taproom over a stable at a crossroads · the woman who came nine days, and the room exists.** **Marda Oakes, 51**, named, came up nine days with a stick with a driftwood head and a piece of driftwood the length of a forearm in the top of her bundle, **because a man had been onto that foreshore twice with a chain and a boy with a rod, and he had wanted a figure of men, a trade, and the name of the person who would answer for the road, and she could give him the second and could not give him the first and has never in twenty-two years been able to produce the third.** She came to find out who was asking. **She asks it in an ordinary voice in a taproom where about four hundred people are, and about nine people hear it, and two of the nine are in that room.** **Rusen Ball** shows her the letter and does not hand it to her, which is correct and which he does by accident; she cannot read it, and he reads the whole of it including the second side, which is a list of eleven items, and she counts the eleven on her fingers twice with the same hand. **The first of the seven things she says in that taproom is sixty-seven words in one turn, and a man of about twenty-nine counts it on his thumb without knowing he is doing it.** She counts the four and the seven and says the seven are the ones that are used. She says the thing the volume stands on, once, and does not say it again for four days: **you are asking me for a keeper, and I have kept that road for about twenty-two years without anybody asking me to, and I have never kept a day in my life, and there is a week in every month when I am asleep from the middle of one tide to the middle of the next and there is nobody on that foreshore who would wake me for a wagon, and that is not a fault and it is not a liberty, it is what the water does.** Nine seconds later she says the kind thing, and the two are nine seconds apart and the second is worse. Cost: **27 pence.** Ending: about nine people heard three ordinary words said loudly in a market.

**Ch 453 · the third day of Frostmonth, a Monday, not a market day · a foreshore · a road that is there now and will not be there again for about four hours.** Two miles and about nine hundred paces of hard sand and broken shell where a river runs into the sea. **A loaded wagon can go one way at the bottom of the water and cannot come back the same tide; a barrow can go either way; a man on foot can go at any hour and gets his boots filled and says nothing about it.** There is no line on it. **The sand turns in about nine places and none of the nine is a boundary anybody keeps and the ninth is three black stones a storm put there in a month nobody living can name.** Four of the eleven houses belong to somebody and seven belong to nobody and the seven are the ones that are used. **The driftwood is the block's central object and it was wrong in Batch 0001's first printing and was repaired: it has twenty-two notches in the edge of it, one for each year, and the twenty-second is not cut, and it goes in when the water is off a mark on that post that has not been off in twenty-two years, and it was not off in the year her husband died and she got it wrong twice on a wagon and the wagon went into the channel and nobody was on it.** A wall of stone eleven paces long is a hundred paces from the landward end and took four days, and **a wall takes the tide twice a day exactly as a post does and about four inches of sand banks against its far side in four months and nobody has to do anything to that wall for it to stop being in the way.** He gets down onto the sand four times without being asked and is told, once, that he is looking at it the way a man looks at a sill, and that he is not going to say what he is looking for because he has not decided. Cost: **32 pence.** Ending: the sea comes over the wall and goes back.

**Ch 454 · the eighth day of Frostmonth, a Saturday, which is a market morning in a county about two hundred miles off and is not a market morning in the yard the chapter is set in · a slipway and a yard behind a weigh-house in a port of about nine hundred people at the landward end of the foreshore · a market that is on a tide, and the first accent.** **A market is a day where people are gathered who would not otherwise be in the same street, and a man of thirty-four with a sheet has put the word *market morning* on about nine pieces of paper in nine counties, and on a foreshore two miles and about nine hundred paces long there is no such word and never has been.** A woman of about thirty-four with a barrow tells him there is a market and that it has been a market since before the wall and that he is going to keep saying that word and she is going to keep telling him it has no morning. **Then he says ten words in the accent of the far side of a salt-store and she repeats them back in the same accent and it is exactly what he meant and it is not the sound that came out of his mouth, and he hears both of them at once about four seconds apart, and about nine people heard and about six of the nine are on the foreshore.** Nobody says anything. **The market is nine hundred people and about four hundred of them are on the sand and about five hundred are in four houses and a yard; about nine cart-loads of shell a day go off four boats into four pans, a wagon of salt waits six hours for the tide and the driver is not paid extra, nine panes of glass came nine days on a cart and the seller stands beside it, and there are eleven baskets of preserved fruit of which nine are bought before the salt is off.** The woman with the barrow says the foreshore has four keepers, which is what the four houses are, and names the four, and says the man who owns the fourth is not a good man and that nobody will say so out loud on the sand. Cost: **35 pence.** Ending: the wall does not move.

**Ch 455 · the twelfth day of Frostmonth, a Wednesday, not a market day · a salt-store at the landward end · the first crossing, nine days, and a consent given in four words.** **A salt-store is the one place on that foreshore where a door can be stood in twice a day and still be shut at both ends of the water**, and a woman of about thirty-four put a bench against the inside of it on the third day and moved it back on the twelfth. **Nine people came and none of them works there and the man of about twenty-nine came with them and was not asked.** She states the only argument she has for letting him in, in about four minutes, and the argument is that **a man who is nobody cannot be asked whether he thinks he ought to be let in, and that the four would have had a vote about him in a week**, and she says it is not a good one. She asks three questions, he answers all three the same way, and she says yes in four words about the door and not about him. **Four of the nine did not agree, said so out loud, and were not overvoted because there was nothing to overvote, because nobody was keeping a count.** The other side is a courtyard with a wall about nine feet high and about forty people in it, **and there is no Wednesday on that side of the door and has not been for about nine hundred years.** Nine days. **What nine days does is a thing he does with his hands that he did not do before, a way of standing on sand, a handful of words in a language that is not his, and the ability to read a tide off a post, and one sentence from a person on that side that he will carry for the rest of his life.** He comes back at about the eleventh hour on the twelfth of a month, having been nine days on the other side, and a woman says, in six words, that he is standing like a man from the seaward end, and he looks at his own feet on a stone floor and they are pointing at the door. **Nobody said a word about the accent.** Cost: **41 pence.** Ending: nine days of another shore's bread is nobody's column.

**Ch 456 · the ninth ebb, and the fourth hour after the water went out · the coast frame, no month and no day of the week · a pan, a fire, and a want spoken once in forty-one years.** **Halla Wray, 63**, named. A pan a foot and a half across set in brick at the landward end, and **the fire under it has not been out since a month in a year she does not give, and the fire is older than her and both of those are true.** She has not come down to the sand in about nine years and sits on a low wall behind the fourth house with a stick and a bowl of brine. She tells him what a pan does in a season, and when he asks what a good season looks like she answers and then answers a second question he did not ask: **that is the same answer he would have got off any of the four, and there is nothing in it about me.** **That is a hundred and sixty-five words in two turns of two speeches, of eighty and eighty-five, and it is the whole of what she said to him, and in forty-one years she has been asked twice — once by a man who wanted to buy the pan and once by a clerk who wanted a figure — and has never been asked one single thing about what she wants, and this is the first time she has said that out loud to anybody.** About two hundred paces off, **a woman of about fifty-one says her own want to nobody at the ebb before the light: if the four ask the seven for one thing then the seven are eight, and the four are still four, and after that there is a count of eleven on a piece of paper and the eleven has a head on it and the head says men.** About nine people hear it and about four of them are the kind of person who will carry it to the four, and the four are not on that foreshore that day. He carries shell nine times. Cost: **51 pence.** Ending: a house with a door let to nobody, rewritten out — seven houses on the seaward half are shut when the water is in.

**Ch 457 · the thirteenth ebb, the second hour after the water went out · the coast frame · the refusal, and the protagonist asked for the middle and saying no.** **The nine did not gather. They came out to the pan over about an hour, in ones and twos, because it is eleven o'clock past the ebb and about nine hundred people have work.** There is no chair and they are not a council. The woman with the barrow puts the second side of the letter on the wall and does not read it out, and the man with nine hundred acres reads it badly. The woman of sixty-three says a thing about herself. **The nine refuse in about nine minutes on five grounds and none of the five is the same and none is wrong**: a middle with a name on it can be given a second name, and a lease on a piece of shingle is worth about nine times what it was before a wall went in a hundred paces off; a market that is four houses has never in nine years been made seven, and putting the seven in is the first step; the woman's reason; the ninth of the nine, a man of about thirty-four with a barrow, in nine words — **he would rather be sent for than be found** — which is the one reason on that sand that has nothing to do with the tide. **A woman of about fifty-eight who sweeps a yard behind a house of assizes sent nine words nine days back on a handcart: tell him the yard will outlive all of us, and nobody is going to carry an answer, and the woman with the barrow put them in the front of her shirt and said no when she was asked whether she was going to answer them.** **He is asked whether he will hold the middle and says no in thirty-four words in one turn: I will not stand in the middle of three places, I said so to a woman with a pen about a month ago and I am not going to say it twice for money. It is the fourth time in about seven months, and of the four he said yes to one and no to this one and the other two were offers he made and did not get.** Cost: **57 pence.** Ending: the twenty-second notch is not cut and the water has not been off that mark in twenty-two years.

**Ch 458 · the twenty-first day of Frostmonth, a Friday, not a market day · a hundred paces from the landward end · a surveyor, a rod, a wall, and a fourth head.** **A survey of a foreshore is not a hard thing to do and it took four days because the tide is in the way twice a day and a chain cannot be pulled through water a man is standing in.** A surveyor of about forty-four and a boy of about seventeen with a rod and about nine days of a notebook with no head on any page. The woman with the barrow answers three questions, and **says she is one of the seven and not one of the four, and that if the surveyor puts that down as keeper of a house she will say so on the sand and about nine people will be there in a minute and will back her, and not because of her**, and two of the three answers are written down and the third is not. **What comes back to a town of about two thousand is a piece of paper that wants three heads and a fourth, and the fourth is newer, and the office does the three and the fourth sits there, and a clerk of about nineteen in his second year asks the one question nobody has asked: who is to answer for it.** The man of about thirty-four with a sheet comes back with the wall and the survey and three filled heads and does not bring a fourth, and stands on the landward side of eleven paces of stone opposite a woman with a barrow for about nine minutes and neither says a word, and the sea comes in over both of their feet. **The surveyor takes his chain off the sand and says, to nobody, that he was asked for a figure of men and was given one by a man who did not have it, and has not said it since and will not.** Cost: **60 pence.** Ending: the water goes over the wall and over about two hundred paces of the seaward half.

**Ch 459 · the twenty-sixth day of Frostmonth, a Wednesday, not a market day · the room over a weigh-house · the accent, a drawer, and a kitchen he cannot place.** A four-line letter came in on the twenty-third day and one of the four lines is the question, written by a man of about thirty-four with a sheet and carried by a clerk of about nineteen who has never seen the foreshore. **She asks him to say a sentence out loud, and it comes out in the coast's accent, and in about nine seconds she says that a copy of how a person says a word is a thing about a person that anybody can be shown to a person, and that the difference between the two of them is four years and a half and is not a small one. The accent is first on the page on the eighth of Frostmonth, four days before he went through a door in a salt-store, and it is not caused by the crossing and does not stop at it.** He knows she is right and neither of them says one word about the accent. **And he cannot place a room: a kitchen with a blacked-in hearth and a window that does not shut, which is his mother's, and about two of whose details came from a courtyard about four hundred miles off.** On the twenty-fourth day he took out a notebook with eleven lines and wrote on the eleventh, which had never been used, the word for the blacked-in hearth in the courtyard, **which is not the word for a blacked-in hearth in a fen kitchen, and the two are the same word in about four hundred languages and neither of them is the word he uses.** He did not cross anything out and did not add a date and did not add a name, and there is no twelfth line. **Three crossings in about four weeks against a budget of about three a season, and the whole of the loss is that he cannot tell which of two kitchens he is remembering, and on the twenty-fourth day she said a sentence in that accent and did not notice.** Cost: **67 pence.** Ending: the eleventh line has one word on it that is the right word for the wrong room.

**Ch 460 · the thirteenth day of Hearthmonth, a Saturday, a market morning · a made road four days off the foreshore · the batch's last page, and there is nobody in a room on it.** **Seventeen days after a woman of twenty-nine said that a copy of how a person says a word is a thing about a person**, he walked four days from a port and stopped on a verge for about nine minutes and tried to remember which of two kitchens he had been in, and got it wrong twice, and on the third attempt got the window right and got the door wrong, and left it alone. **A woman of about thirty-four with a barrow came up behind him and said, after six words of her own, you have been on a beach, and then thirty-five words in one turn: you are standing like a man who has been carrying something for nine days, and you have not got anything, and that is the only thing I have ever seen you do that was wrong. He answered in the same accent in a sentence about the tide — there will be water over that wall this afternoon, and it will be off it again by the eighth hour of the morning — and about nine people going past heard the two of them, and about none of the nine said anything. **And this time he did not hear the difference, which is the fourth time the beat plays and the first time the man in it cannot do the thing that made it matter on the eighth of a month, five weeks earlier.** She tells him the surveyor is coming back on the ninth day with a second chain and a longer rod, and that the man with the sheet comes with him about half of the time, and that neither of them has ever been on that sand at the fourth hour of a morning. **He does not put up a stall on a market morning and does not say why and is not asked.** **A man of about thirty-four with a sheet is in the same yard with a man of about thirty-eight and about nine of them, and the two were about ninety feet apart for about an hour and neither went in the other's direction.** On the two days after that Saturday he carries stone for the woman with the barrow, unpaid, unasked, watched by about nine and understood by about four. **A woman of about fifty-one is at the seaward end and says nine words: go and tell him I have not cut it.** Cost: **77 pence.** Ending: about ninety feet is not a figure of men and is not in any book in nine counties, and there is nobody in a room anywhere on that made road.

**The three chapters a Volume 10 Batch 0002 writer most often needs, and why each of them is one. Chapter 0451** holds the request, the three heads and the new reason a man in the middle of three places is a man who can be sent for to all three, and it is a different reason from Volume 09's and must not be read as the same one. **Chapter 0455** holds the only crossing in the block, the consent in four words, the frame, and the first accent nobody mentions. **Chapter 0457** holds the refusal, the five grounds, the nine words from a yard, and the fourth time in about seven months he has been asked to hold a thing. **Chapter 0459** holds the second cost, the notebook's eleventh line, and the fact that a keeper of records is right about a thing about a person and is not thanked. **Chapter 0460** holds the block's last page and the two nine-word closings, one from a handcart and one at a seaward end, and neither is written down.

## Volume 10, Batch 0002 — Chapters 461 to 470, per chapter

**Ch 461 · the fourteenth day of Hearthmonth, YR 318, a Sunday and not a market day · a room over a weigh-house in a port of about nine hundred people four days off the foreshore · three assemblies in one room for the first time in about forty years, and the thing that is not on the agenda.** **Nine people, three from the landward end, three from the seaward end and three from the port, six of whom walked four days to be in it, and they have met each other twice in forty years and both of the times can be named and neither happened in this room.** There is no chair and there is no agenda and nobody kept a count, and the road was not on the agenda because there is nobody in that port to put it to. They talked for about three hours about six things, and then the man of about sixty with the slipway at the landward end said the thing they had all been circling, which is a loaded wagon that comes off the sand at the wrong hour and stands in the channel with a near wheel in the water. **And they agreed on one thing and no more, in about four minutes: whoever is nearest it gets it out, nobody is called for, nobody is paid, and nobody is asked afterwards.** The boy of nineteen who writes the quay slipway's book said that the wagon is not what they are frightened of and that the man who comes down the made road afterwards with a sheet and a chain is, and the chapter now prints that conversation instead of summarising it: the man of sixty puts it as four questions and gets three silences, the man of about twenty-seven admits he was not at the barn he has told people he was at, and the boy says that writing a book he cannot read is the whole of what he knows and it is enough. **This chapter stood at zero quoted speech in 1,797 words and the craft repair restored it to 21 spans, 1,963 words, and the six question marks that are the first in the manuscript since Volume 04.** The woman of forty-four who lets the room by the day took the fourpence out of a box on the shelf, wrote nothing down, put a key on the table at the eighth hour and went down the stairs, and the bolt was not drawn behind her, **and that room is the fifth answer and it is wrong for a road for the same reason it is right for a room.** Ends on four hundred yards of quay, a man of about thirty-four with a sheet who was not in that port, and eleven paces of stone taking the tide twice a day. **Money: the man of about twenty-nine's, 23 pence, footed on the page.**

**Ch 462 · the nineteenth day of Hearthmonth, YR 318, a Friday and not a market day · a fold thirty miles south of a fen town of about four thousand people · hereditary custody put to a road and found wanting.** Nine stones, a byre, a stream about nine feet wide, about two hundred people who have never argued about where their own boundary is. A man of about forty-one baring the third stone, which his grandfather cut and which leans about four inches out of true, does not stop and does not put the bucket down. **The boy who was about nine does not walk it and is still about nine.** They walk the four stones because the man of about forty-one was going to the fourth anyway, at the pace he has set since he was eleven, and it is not a walk anybody arranged and not a walk for pleasure. **The finding: a boundary is inherited because it is not being touched, and this sand turns in about nine places and none of the nine is a place anybody keeps, and the ninth is three black stones a storm put there in a month he could not name, and a man who wants nine stones cut into it would have nine stones and about four hours.** And then the thing that undid the walk: the fourth generation of men who have walked those nine stones is him, he is forty-one, and when he is gone the walking stops, and about two hundred people have never once asked him what happens then. **Money: the man of about twenty-nine's second, 26 pence, footed on the page.**

**Ch 463 · the fourth day of Goatmonth, YR 318, a Saturday and a market morning · a room over a door on a lane in a fen town of about four thousand people · a rotating clerk put to a road and found wanting.** The bench is nine men and is not a court and does not have a chair, and it has been asked nine things in about nineteen years and is not going to be asked a tenth. About nine hundred people are in the street below and the bench does not sit because of them. **The man of about fifty-eight is on his second year and has four months left of it, is not paid for it, and there are marks cut into the underside of the rail that run back about nineteen years and no further and nobody knows why they stop.** He says the good part out loud and will not give it up, and he says that a turn beats a keeper not because a turn is a better man and because a turn ends. **And then, unasked, the half that is the finding: your road is there about four hours out of every twelve and the question anybody will ask it is whether a man is standing in it, and I answer that question every week for four hours and then I am a different man for the rest of the week and he answers it.** He counts the whole speech on the board in about four seconds and does not read it out. **Money: the man of about twenty-nine's third, 28 pence, footed on the page.**

**Ch 464 · the thirteenth day of Goatmonth, YR 318, a Monday and not a market day · a meeting room over a store in a township of about nine hundred people two days off a made road · a public lot put to a road and found wanting.** A chair on a platform, a hat on a shelf, a book about a hundred and forty years old, about nine names a year out of about nine hundred, a month each, the chair about nine times a year and about a hundred and forty times in a hundred and forty years, and a bolt that has not been drawn in about nine years. **A band of about a hundred and four came back on the second day before and one sheet out of it came into that room, with a date at the top and no head on it, and the woman of about sixty-three read it and put it in the box on the shelf and not in the book, and about nine hundred sheets a month come into that county from a room two hundred miles off and the two are not the same number and neither is added to the other.** The question he asks is what happens if a thing is left and nobody knows who is holding it. **The finding: a hat is how a township says that it does not mind, and it is right for a pound and an oven and a set of nets and a trough because nobody minds being found about a pound, and there is not one thing he is describing that anybody would mind being found about either, and that is the trouble and it took her nineteen years not to be able to say it.** **Money: the man of about twenty-nine's fourth, 49 pence, footed on the page.**

**Ch 465 · the nineteenth day of Goatmonth, YR 318, a Sunday and not a market day · a hurdle across a lane nine miles off a made road, in a dale of about nine hundred people · no gate at all put to a road, and found wanting.** The nine words are a man with a form stayed here four years, said before breakfast, and the hurdle goes over twice a day and the lane sends no cart up it. **The finding, and it is the finding the whole block turns on: a place with no gate is not a refusal, a refusal is a thing a person does and says out loud and somebody hears, and nothing is a thing a place has not got.** The man of about forty-four then says the half of it, which is that he puts a hurdle across twice a day and takes it back, that his father did it and he is nine years dead and cannot tell him why, and that the whole of what nine hundred people could give is a habit and not a keeping. He walks the visitor back down the lane at the pace of a man going to do something. **Money: the man of about twenty-nine's fifth and last in the fen country, 79 pence, footed on the page.**

**Ch 466 · the second ebb after he came down off the made road, and the second hour after the water went out · the seaward end of a foreshore · coast frame, no month, no day of month, no weekday · a woman of about fifty-one asked what she wants, in public, by somebody who is not a knight.** He asks out loud at about nine paces off with his hands empty, and about nine hundred people can hear him because there is no other conversation on a foreshore at low water. **She does not answer for about nine seconds, and then the kind thing comes first, which is not what she does, and the true thing nine seconds after it, which is.** The driftwood has twenty-two notches cut in the edge of it with the twenty-second not cut. **The whole of the answer is a hundred and three words in two turns: nobody has asked her one thing in twenty-two years, and she wants to be asked for a day by somebody and told no, and she wants it in the winter, and she does not want a stone and does not want a head on anything, and she wants the count of that shore to stay exactly where it is, which is in her and in about nine heads of about nine people and nowhere else at all.** She then tells him he will go up the made road and tell somebody, and that the one thing she said out loud on that shore before today went four ways before the water came back in. **A woman of sixty-three on a low wall four hundred yards up the sand was not told and will not be told. Money: the man of about twenty-nine's sixth, 19 pence, footed on the page.**

**Ch 467 · the second ebb after the one where he asked the woman at the seaward end, and the third hour after the water went out · the back of the fourth house, on a low wall about four feet high that faces the wrong way for the water and faces the light · coast frame · a woman of sixty-three asked the same question, did not want to answer it, and answered it.** Nobody introduced them and the only things between the two of them are about nine feet of sand, a bowl of brine and a stick, and about nine people on that foreshore can hear both ends of a conversation at once. **The pan is a foot and a half across and the fire under it has not been out since a month in a year she does not give, and the answering for the fourth house is not for the fire and is for the landing slip at the front door, which has about nine notches cut in one edge of it.** She says no, in about two seconds, and about four of the nine think she has refused the question and about four do not. Then about nine seconds of bowl-turning, then **six sentences of why she is going to answer anyway, and then ten words: I want the road to stay exactly as it is. That is the whole of what she wanted, and the first part before it was longer than the want.** She is not thanked for it and says she is not to be. **Money: the man of about twenty-nine's seventh, 55 pence, footed on the page.**

**Ch 468 · the twenty-seventh day of Goatmonth, YR 318, a Monday and not a market day, at the ninth of eleven houses on a foreshore · the coast, in the main-world dateline, and no month and no weekday in anybody's mouth · the fifth thing, and nobody proposed it.** **`Tobin Kernick`, 53, has driven a salt wagon between the port four days up and that shore for nineteen years, and goes down on the ebb and stands about two hours and comes back on the flood, and nobody sets him a time and nobody has ever set him one.** He stops at four of the eleven because there is something to stop at: a pan at the fourth, a lease at the ninth, a widow with the last of a boat at the eleventh who buys four sacks and will not sell them back to him, and a house at the seventh that a man stands in and about four of them will not stop at and he will not say why and nobody asked. **And then the sixth answer, in a hundred and seventy-three words in two turns: nobody keeps it, the seven are the way out of the weather because nobody is standing in them, and not one person on that shore agreed to any of it.** And then, at the fifth hour, in a hundred and three words, the second time in about a year he has said no to a stranger about a house, and the first time he has said it twice. **His own want is that a man who cannot put a horse down is not a keeper of anything and never will be. Money: the man of about twenty-nine's eighth, 73 pence, footed on the page.**

**Ch 469 · the twenty-ninth day of Goatmonth, YR 318, a Wednesday and not a market day, at the landward end · in front of a weigh-house and a slipway and about four hundred yards of quay · the three rooms and what each will carry, and the one that will not, and a man of thirty-two with a limp asked whether he is needed.** Three people have come down the made road four days to find out what their own rooms will carry, and what came down it is four clauses and not one word about anything else. **The landward-end room will carry it and has already said it out loud once. The seaward-end room will carry it and will say it at the next ebb, and the woman of about fifty-one is not in that room and has not been told. The port four days off will not, and nobody refused anything: a weigh-house is a room where about nine hundred people are paid twice in seven days, and a man standing in it cannot say four clauses about a wagon out loud without making three of them a lie.** The man of about twenty-nine asks the man of about thirty-two with a limp whether he is needed, in the open, with nine people nine feet off, and nobody arranged it. **This is the protagonist's one chapter in the block and the craft repair is what put his name on the page: `Aren` now appears three times, in the narration, in the speech attribution and in the closing frame, against zero across the whole of Volume 10 before the pass and against a prompt that required it.** The chapter also now has what the outline specifies and the first printing did not: the stick he is not leaning on, the four seconds longer to get up off the shingle, the warm left palm and nobody on that sand he could have told it to, and the coast accent he does not know he has used and the woman of twenty-nine two hundred miles off who has not noticed it yet. **The answer is a hundred and eight words in the last of three turns and neither a yes nor a no: if you want one sentence carried, then I am needed, and if you do not, then I am not, and I have not asked which. And I have been asked to hold a thing four times in about seven months and I said yes to one of them and it is not this one.** **Money: the man of thirty-two's, 83 pence, footed on the page.**

**Ch 470 · the first day of Thawmonth, YR 319, a Friday and not a market day, and the volume's year turns on it · a foreshore · the block's last page, and nobody on it notices.** The county does not trade on a first of a month, because on a first of a month the cart is four days up the made road going the other way with what it has gathered in it, and about nine people found out at the third hour that nobody was coming. **What about nine hundred people do on a day when nothing is coming and nothing is going is what they did on every other day, and it cannot be written down because a column wants a head and this has never had one.** Nobody noticed that anything had changed, because nothing on that foreshore is counted in anything that changes. **The water did not go off the mark, and the twenty-second notch is not cut, and the man of about thirty-four with a sheet has been on that sand four times since the middle of a month in Wolfmonth and there is nothing in the four of them that says why he comes when he comes.** He asks a woman of about thirty-four with a barrow what the day is, and she gives him the number of times the water has been off and on since the boats went out that morning and will not count it for him. **She tells him that nobody has ever asked the eleven houses anything, that this is not a criticism, and that it is the first thing he did right.** **Ends on the driftwood with twenty-two notches and the twenty-second not cut, four boats, four pans, a fire that has not been out since a month in a year a woman of sixty-three does not give, and the fact that there is no day here for a year to turn on. Money: the man of about twenty-nine's ninth and smallest, 62 pence, footed on the page.**

**The three chapters a Volume 10 Batch 0003 writer most often needs, and why each of them is one. Chapter 0468** holds the sixth answer and the one name this volume has spent, and the man of about fifty-three's own want, and the exact wording of the four stops. **Chapter 0466** holds the want of a woman of about fifty-one, spoken once, in a hundred and three words in two turns, and the driftwood's rule. **Chapter 0461** holds the four clauses about a wagon and the fifth answer and the shape of the room that is let by the day, and Chapter 0469 holds what became of all three of them.**

## Volume 10, Batch 0003 — Chapters 471 to 480, days 1237 to 1284, 17,120 words in ten chapters

**This section is written by the phase that wrote Batch 0003 and it is the handoff for Block 0004. The block runs day 1237 to day 1284, all of it YR 319, and the ten days were derived from the day number twice, independently of the prompt, and the two halves are reported as two numbers in `state/continuity.md` 1157. Nothing in this section is a summary of the block and nothing in it is a plan.**

**Ch 471 · the second of Thawmonth, YR 319, a Saturday and a market morning · a market town of about two thousand people four days off that coast · the counting-house at the back of a street four hundred yards long, and the first arithmetic, and it is a difference and not a total.** A man of about forty-four has kept a book in one hand for twenty-two years and the only written price in the trade is six pence a cart-load. A cart-load of shell is worth nine pence to anybody who will burn it himself and he has been beaten nine times.
