**Ch 560 · the twenty-fourth of Greenmonth, YR 320, a Sunday and not a market day · the room over a saddler's, opened once and shut again · the floor, and a question going home in a coat.** **Four people are let in through a door the man with the badge has the key to, and the floor of that room has been washed, and nobody in nine counties knows who washed it and nobody was thanked, and the copyist writes the floor and the rule-not-broken on one bare leaf with no head over it.** He asks her the one question he has held for nine days: he wants three things in a line in a book that a clerk can read in about four years, that a place exists, that somebody there was asked, and that the somebody asked is a person and not an office. **She says he will have to ask her at the gate instead of in a room, that at the gate nobody is watching, and that the second of the three things has to be hers and not his.** The leaf is made, the fourth head is a sixteenth of an inch narrower than the three above it because the copyist ruled it that way in another county and has told nobody why, three heads are filled in the valley's words and not the letter's, and the question goes under the fourth one in the same nine words as Thawmonth and **is not reprinted a third time because the copyist says once is a copy and twice is a habit.** The pot goes down to the yard and goes home on a Saturday cart with her.

**The chapters a Volume 12 Batch 0002 writer needs.** **Chapter 553** holds the un-mended channel and why nobody may say it wants mending. **Chapter 554** holds the fifth document and what a mark in a tree is. **Chapter 555** holds the bell, the boy's board and the line about a thing nobody can ring. **Chapter 557** holds the voice that is not hers and the sentence the volume is built on. **Chapter 558** holds the finding and the admission that he was sent. **Chapter 559** holds the lane, the sentence she says in it, and the making of the leaf. **Chapter 560** holds the floor, the gate, and the about a sixteenth of an inch that the copyist has never explained to anybody in that county. **And one figure a Batch 0002 writer must not re-collapse: the party is eleven and the room's attendance is nine, the nine of them were the party on the Sunday the room was shut and the two who came on the Monday are the other two, and Chapter 0560 now says on the page that the two figures are one party on two days and are not two parties. The block's repeated *about four of the nine* and *about five of the nine* is a split of the room's opinion and is not the outline's *about four of the eleven have seen it and about seven have not*, which is not yet on the page and which only a person standing in that room may put there.**

**What changed in this block, in one paragraph.** A certification was asked for and refused, and the refusal is on the page in the valley's own terms rather than the room's. A document with nothing on it turned out to be a scar in a tree. A thing a place sent arrived and opened nothing, and a question went home instead. A man told a woman from a place that the place could not speak, in a lane, without asking her. A floor was washed and nobody knows who washed it. Two names were spent of five. Nobody was thanked.

---

## Volume 12, Batch 0002 — Chapters 561 to 570, "The Answer and the Cost of the Road"

**Ten chapters inside a span of days 1680 to 1722, of which days 1680 to 1684 and 1712 to 1722 carry no chapter, which is the ordinary condition for a block and is not a hole. **Two of the ten chapters print a main-world dateline — the thirtieth of Greenmonth YR 320 at Chapter 0561 and the twenty-sixth of Longlight YR 320 at Chapter 0569, both derived from day 930 — and eight print an Orchard one carrying a sitting and an hour and no month, no weekday and no tide, and the two sets are not added. The ten-day list this line first printed is withdrawn: seven of its nine values are not a date anywhere in the block and only two are on the page.** 18,429 words in ten chapters, plain whitespace split with the title line included, each file on its own and the ten added, recounted after the second review pass's last prose edit; per chapter 2,273 / 1,748 / 1,518 / 1,769 / 1,750 / 1,906 / 1,693 / 1,554 / 2,201 / 2,017.**

**Ch 561** · **the thirtieth of Greenmonth, YR 320, a Saturday and a market day · a made road out of a market town of about two thousand people · a cart, a pot, a leaf, and a man who gets on without being asked.** A cart goes out on a market Saturday loaded before the market opened, with a pot of loam and a scion of about nine inches under a wet sacking, and a woman of about thirty-eight who walked nine days with it in her hands, and **a man of thirty-two with a limp who gets on at the end of the lane and is not sent and was not asked, and who says he had about nine days to think about getting on and did not spend them.** About half way up the last hill a woman says his name out loud in a yard and the man in the yard does not use it. **The made road ends at a cross of two stones and a heap of broken metalling; the driver will not take a cart further and says the rest has not been a road in the memory of anybody he has spoken to.** A lane goes up out of the wet ground and ends at a bar of wood lying in the grass, where a woman of about fifty-five with a board asks four questions in order, which she has asked of everybody for about thirty years. **The fourth one is not a gate question and nobody has ever answered it, and the woman of about thirty-eight answers it: nobody in this valley has been asked anything for about twenty-two years and she is asking on her own account and not for the Standing.** **The bar is never lifted, there is no door and there is no sound, and he is on the same side of it at the sixth hour and is not at the seventh.** The cord on the scion is tight again and she says that is not a thing a pot does on a cart. A woman of about seventy looks at the leaf and says it is a leaf with four divisions and not four heads, and that the mark is written under the fourth and not in it, and nobody asks her why. Column **147 pence**, the woman of about thirty-eight's first in this block.

**Ch 562** · **the second hour of the swell sitting · the drying floor, the Low Gate, and four miles' worth of a made road below it · a crossing that is not a door and is not a bell.** He cannot place himself in the second hour of anything and **nobody in that valley is going to tell him, because nobody in that valley knows.** The woman of about fifty-five reads him all four questions because the fourth is the one he wants and the first three are how she knows which way he came. **The fourth is the question that went under a fourth head on nine leaves in a room two hundred miles off in Thawmonth, and the two wordings are not the same words and are one question and are not added.** The woman of about thirty-eight says she does not know whether it was the asking or the answering, and that she has decided not to find out. **Going out is free and takes about four hours and costs nothing; coming in is not.** A carter of about forty with two sacks of pears answers three of the four, says the fourth is a formality, and does not come in; the pears go into the press-house and are on no road in nine counties. **It is not a door, there is no building over it, no lock and no keeper of a lock; it is not a bell, and the press-house bell was rung at the fourth point of that sitting by a boy of about fifteen who says he rang it because the hour was the hour.** Column **128 pence**, the driver's. **The nine on the lane and the four near the bar, which the column sets aside, are not the nine who heard the bell and the four who did not, and the page says so two lines below where a reader would otherwise have had one pair of figures standing four lines from another.**

**Ch 563** · **the fourth hour of the swell sitting · the Long Set, about eleven terraces and about two hundred trees and a press-house at the foot of it · pruning, and a wall.** He is put on the second terrace with a pruning knife and is good for about nine minutes and then is not. **A bridge craft and a pruning knife are two trades: a bridge is cut in the direction you already believe in and fruit is not.** He takes out a limb the wrong side twice and a boy of about fifteen says *that is a good one* in the voice of a boy being shown something. **He says the load-bearing thing about the second wall out loud, in about nine seconds, to nobody in particular, and four people hear it, and a man of about fifty-one who keeps the water at the top of the Long Set hears it and does not say one word and goes up about eleven terraces and does not come back down.** He has not heard what is wrong with it. Column **159 pence**, the man of about forty-four's.

**Ch 564** · **the seventh hour of the swell sitting · the head of the Long Set · a spade, a root that has come up through a path, and the first thing anybody in that valley says that was not in answer to a question.** **Her name is Ilsa Wend** and she has been in that valley all her life and about nine people in it could have said the name and none of them did. She is going to lose a tree and is doing it in the swell sitting anyway, because in the pick there are hands to spare and in the swell there are not. **She says that she would like to change her mind about something and has not been able to for four years, and that he is the first person she has said it to who was not going to answer her.** The want, at length: to be able to say that a thing she did was wrong without it also turning out that she is the one who has to move. **The oldest voice in the valley comes out of her mouth when she says what the Standing would say about her being the answer, and she hears him hear it, and neither of them says anything about it; and then she goes back to her own voice in the middle of a sentence with no sound of a change at all.** She tells him about a strip of ground at the foot of the Long Set that asked to be let go, about ninety people going out on it, and that she was about sixteen and said yes and did not go. **She will not take him to the place where a thing is not said out loud, and she tells him there is one old tree on the slope and everybody knows it.** Column **173 pence**, hers.

**Ch 565** · **the ninth hour of the swell sitting · a room off the middle terrace with a board on the wall and four stools and a fifth empty · four lines under a fourth head, and what the woman who wrote them would have to do to be allowed to have written them.** The four lines: *nobody in this valley has been asked anything for about twenty-two years; I am asking on my own account; the name I use for the person who may speak for a road here is a person and not an office; I have not been made one.* **That is not a name for a road and not a promise of peace and not a list.** **The fourth head is still ruled and still empty and the lines are under it and not in it.** The man of about sixty says the answer is made and not found, and there has not been one in that valley in twenty-two years, and to be made a woman has to be put to the Standing, and **a woman who holds a block of ground cannot be the answer for the ground, because a person who can be moved off it will find that out before anybody else does.** He also says out loud, unasked, that **a person who has answered once can be told by that room that she has not**, and that he is not going to use it. The man of about thirty-one asks why that is a rule and not a way of keeping the valley as it is. She leaves the leaf on the board and asks for the board to be moved. Column **183 pence**, the man of thirty-two's, **and this block prints no column ordinal in any of its ten chapters**, which is a deliberate variation and not a missing figure.

**Ch 566** · **the second hour of the swell sitting · a night of which he can say nothing · the price of the crossing, on the page, and it is not a wound and it is not a panel.** He cannot place the night and **nobody in that valley will tell him what hour it is, because nobody in that valley knows.** **His left palm is warm and is not the fourth hour of anything he can name, and he has never told anybody in eleven years and does not now.** At about the fourth hour he is on the press-house floor with a woman of about fifty-eight and a hundred and eleven cloths, and **she names his habit in nine sentences: that he comes into a place and inside about nine minutes has said out loud what is wrong with it, in the voice a man uses when a thing is going to be mended tomorrow, and that nothing in that valley is going to be mended tomorrow, and that about two of the four men she has had off that hill in thirty years said a thing like it and about two said nothing, and that she cannot tell which kind does more good and is not going to work it out.** He is not asked to explain it and does not. **The oldest voice in the valley comes out of her mouth in the middle of a sentence and she stops and looks at her own mouth, and he does not ask her about it, because a man who will not ask a stranger about his own hand has no standing to ask a woman about her mouth.** Column **187 pence**, the woman of about fifty-eight's.

**Ch 567** · **the third hour of the pick sitting · the cut, about four miles of it, about two paces wide, with a lip on the high side · a fault at the fourth hundred pace and a rule about who may say it.** He sees it in about nine minutes: the lip on the high side carries for about nine yards on a bed that has gone, and the bed has gone because the water has been let out at the foot and put back in at the head for about twenty-two years, and what the lip is standing on is a line of rubble about two hands thick that is not doing one thing for the water above it. **The rule of the valley is that a man who can see what is wrong with that channel may say so to one other person and may not say it to a second, because the reason it wants mending is in a record that may not be read, and a fault with no reason behind it is not a finding, it is an accusation, and an accusation in this valley does not fall on the man who makes it.** He says nothing for four miles. A boy of about fifteen asks him one direct question and gets two facts and a refusal. **The man of about fifty-one comes back down at the seventh hour and says out loud that he knows he has a fault and is not going to ask for it and is not going to tell anybody that he has not got one, because that would be him saying the channel is sound and he does not know that and has not walked four miles of anything in about nine years.** His palm is warm on the lip of the high side. Column **199 pence**, the man of about fifty-one's.

**Ch 568** · **the eighth hour of the pick sitting · the oldest tree on that slope with a ring of stones set round it · a woman of about sixty-four who has counted them for thirty years and has never been asked.** Her work is cords: about nine hundred cut in a sitting, about a hundred and forty tied by her, paid in fruit and not in money. **She counts the stones at the eighth hour because that is the hour at which the cords are done, and because a woman who has been working for a hundred and forty cords needs to know the day is finished, and the number has not been a different number once in thirty years.** **He stands beside her at about two paces off for the whole of it and does not ask, and it is the only time anybody in that valley has stood next to that tree and not asked, and he has not decided that he was being careful.** She tells him it is not a thing she is keeping and not a secret and she has never wanted a man from off the hill to make her into one. **He says the first untrue thing he says in that valley, that he did not think she was keeping anything, and she says he did think it and that a man who thinks it and does not say it is about nine years old.** She asks him about the leg and he answers with a trade fact. She gives him forty cords and he ties them like a man tying a boat and she unties all forty. Column **178 pence**, hers.

**Ch 569** · **the twenty-sixth of Longlight, YR 320, a Thursday and a market day · the end of a made road about nine yards short of a cross of two stones · a folded paper, a man who waited two days, and the only person in nine counties who can make anything possible.** The man of about thirty-one opened by saying he asked her to be the answer and has spent two days walking to ask a man from off the hill for a paper instead. **Four sets of people on that slope, four of the eleven terraces under water for about nine days, nine households on the four lowest, and no third figure.** The paper is a statement that the cut is out of repair, and a county will pay for four miles of stone and will not send anybody, and there is no head on it for the county and a line at the top of it that wants one name. **He is one of the nine; his mother's door is open and there is a pot on the step; and he says it on purpose, and says that neither of the two possible outcomes being bad is a reason not to mend the channel.** **Aren Kest says no, and the reason is not that a name in a line is a name in a line: it is that a woman of about thirty-eight asked to be the answer in front of four people about nine days ago, nobody has told her whether she may, and the only people who own that question are four days and about nine below where he is standing.** And he says why he will not go and say the word either: **he told a woman in a lane behind a room in a market town that her place could not speak, nine days before he came up this lane, and decided it in about nine minutes in a room with about nine people in it, and had the days to ask her and used them on something else.** The man of about thirty-one's last turn is that he asked her to be the answer and then walked two days to ask a man for a paper, and has not once asked her whether she wanted the paper. Column **164 pence**, the man of about thirty-one's.

**Ch 570** · **the third hour of the pick sitting · the middle terrace and a board with four divisions on it · a question asked in the open and not answered, a year lost off a block, and a third line in an order he did not put in.** He is let back in behind a carter of about nineteen who answered all four; **he himself has now come in on somebody else's answer twice, and a carter of about forty who went out in the swell sitting with two sacks of pears has not come back in at all.** **At the second hour the woman of about thirty-eight asked four people one question in the open, at the hour the board sits, and two of the four were not there and one of those sent a child and the fourth said nothing.** The block on the second terrace is being thinned by a man doing it the way it is done on his own ground, **and a year of that block is gone and the man of about forty-four says so himself and says he does not mind and wants it said that he said so.** A woman of about forty-four who keeps the board has ruled four divisions and filled three, **and the order is a person first, then where it is, then who it is, then what goes on it, and the third line on that board is a name where the third line on the leaf in his coat is a trade, and he does not correct it and does not take the leaf out.** The five documents are named in five places on the page and none of them is the Crown's form, whose fourth head is ruled and empty and about six months empty and which nothing in that valley can fill. **And he says the decision out loud, once, to a person: he is not going to say what is wrong with the cut, she is going to have to say it, she is going to have to be asked first, and he is not going to ask her on anybody's behalf including his own, and he tells her that he noticed he was copying her and is not going to pretend he did not.** **And the road is open and he is on the wrong side of it, and the half leaf is still face down, and the floor of that room was washed before he came up here and nobody in nine counties knows who washed it and nobody was thanked.** Column **191 pence**, the man of about forty-four's. **The woman of about forty-four, who keeps the board, is named as the speaker of the order before the order is spoken, because the paragraph that establishes the order names two women about forty words apart and a reader had a one-in-two choice on the sentence the chapter turns on.**

**The chapters a Volume 12 Batch 0003 writer needs.** **561** holds the gate, the four questions, the answer and the geography. **562** holds the crossing as a condition, the carter who is out, and the fact that it is not a door and not a bell. **563** holds the habit that gets named in 566. **564** holds the putting-away's first telling and the voice that is not hers. **565** holds the four lines, the rule that an answer cannot hold ground, and the threat of being told she has not. **566** holds the night he cannot place and the path bleed in two other people's mouths. **567** holds the fault, the fourth hundred pace, and the rule that seeing it is not being allowed to say it. **568** holds the count and the tree and the restraint. **569** holds the ask, the refusal, and the nine households. **570** holds the decision, the third line, and the year lost off a block. **And the figure a Batch 0003 writer must not re-collapse: the crossing is one crossing with one bar, and it is on the page in 561, 562, 567, 569 and 570 and no chapter of this block adds a second gate.**

## Volume 12, Batch 0003 — Chapters 571 to 580, "What the Ground Holds"

**Ten chapters, days 1723 to 1758, the block's ten main-world days at 1723, 1727, 1731, 1736, 1740, 1746, 1750, 1753, 1756 and 1758, derived from day 930 and never from one half of the calendar to the other. **Two of the ten chapters print a main-world dateline and eight print an Orchard one carrying a sitting and an hour and no month, no weekday and no tide, and the two sets are not added.** 18,240 words in ten chapters, plain whitespace split with the title line included, each file on its own and the ten added; per chapter 1,774 / 1,769 / 2,017 / 1,732 / 1,957 / 1,680 / 1,823 / 1,873 / 1,980 / 1,635. **Those are the files as they stand after this block's seven changes and the review repair over them, and the figure was recounted after the last of them; Chapter 0574 is 1,732 because the review gave its added speech a line and an attribution, and the block is 18,240 and not the 18,234 the third pass printed.**

**Ch 571** · **the settlement at the middle terrace, the first hour of the bar sitting · a board on a wall with four divisions ruled on it and three of them filled · a leaf with four lines under a fourth head and one word in the margin · a woman of about thirty-eight who asked four people a question about herself and is going to ask about nine people a question about the ground.** The first work of the bar sitting is carrying dead wood down from the second terrace and about eleven people are on that ground. **Nobody has told her she may**, and she comes to the board at about the eighth hour and not the second, and says the difference between the two askings is not the hour. She asks: **is there anybody in this valley who will let a person dig at the foot of the oldest tree in the Long Set?** The man of about thirty-one says that is the ground the oath is on; the man of about sixty says it is not a block and it is not hers and there are about four people on that slope who could say yes this morning and every one of them is about thirty or less. **She refuses to have it written down, on the grounds that a written question is a question to an office and there is no office in this valley**, and says she would rather be refused in the open than permitted in a room and will not say it twice. The man of about thirty-one says a question about her can be refused in a room and a question about that tree cannot, and she says she has thought about the half of it where she cannot get the answer back. Aren Kest stands about nine paces off and says nothing and is not asked. The man of about fifty-one hears his own name and comes down the slope and says nothing about a tree. Column **174 pence**, the woman of about thirty-eight's.

**Ch 572** · **the Low Gate, the sixth hour of the bar sitting · a carter of about thirty with an empty cart and one wheel · a woman of about fifty-five with a board · nine paces off a woman of about thirty-eight with a barrow · and a word nobody in that valley had ever heard.** The carter answers three of the four things at the bar and then says, to nobody, that he has come out of a place they call the quiet. **He does not know what he has said and nobody stops him and the bar is not lifted.** She asks where he got it: a man with a sheet said it in a market town about four days of carting off, reading it out of a report with about four hands of writing on it, and the carter cannot say whose report it is because nobody told him, and **nobody in that town can produce it.** The woman of about thirty-eight asks him two questions from a cart's width. At the ninth hour she says the word once, out loud, at the head of the Long Set, with eleven people within nine paces, to nobody in particular, **and it is the first time in about twenty-two years anybody in that valley has heard it, and she has not said it since.** The man of about thirty-one says a paper went out of this valley in the year the strip was let go, with about four hands of writing, and it did not come back, and his father's father was on the Standing that year and never spoke of it. The man of about sixty says he has never used the word in his own mouth and will not start, because a word that came from outside is not a name for anything. A woman of about seventy comes down with a lamp and says she has a name for this place, that it is not that, that it is not a word anybody from outside can use, and does not give it. Column **160 pence**, the woman of about fifty-five's.

**Ch 573** · **the settlement at the middle terrace, the ninth hour of the bar sitting · a board on a wall · a man of about fifty-one who keeps the water who has come down nine terraces to argue · a woman of about thirty-eight who asked a question about the oldest tree and has not had it answered · and a man of thirty-two with a limp who is going down the lane in the morning.** He opens by saying that he told four households at the top of the slope that a man from off the hill walked four miles of his channel and said nothing, in the pick sitting, before there was a single fact behind it, **and that one of those four households has a daughter on the second terrace who heard him say it and came and looked at the man while he said it.** He wants the ground opened. She puts her knife in the ground, which she does not do. The man of about thirty-one says about ninety people are on the oath and two of them are dead. **The man of about sixty makes a rule and says out loud before anybody has to find out that it is not a good one: nobody who holds a block inside nine yards of that tree digs it, all four holders are asked, and a holder who says she would rather not does not stop it and does not have to give a reason and will be believed about anything else she says for the rest of her life.** The man of about thirty-one says that makes honesty in this valley cost a woman something and that the man of about sixty made it in about four minutes, and he agrees. Then the argument, in which neither of the two is right and both are right and neither says the other's half: his water is going and he wants the reason out before there is nothing left under the lip to find it; hers is that it will come out into eleven people's ears on a working morning and it will not be a finding, and she has been that person twice and will not do it a third time in front of people who will remember where she was standing. **She decides it: the eighth hour of a working sitting, when the cords are done, no room, nobody writing who does not have to.** Aren Kest says one sentence — that he does not know whether it should be opened, that he has learned nothing in nine days about how to do any of this differently, and that he is going down the lane in the morning and telling her so that she can tell the two people who are going to open it that he is not here. **All four holders said she would rather not and it made no difference.** Column **231 pence**, the man of about fifty-one's.

**Ch 574** · **the head of the Long Set, the eighth hour of the blossom sitting · about eleven people tying cords under the oldest tree · a ring of stones set in an arc on the side that faces down the slope · two women who have never once asked each other a question.** She works out, while tying cords, **that a number which has not been a different number once in thirty years is not a count of anything, because counts change.** She does not say it for two days because a woman who starts saying a thing everybody has relied on is not a thing has to be able to finish the sentence. At the eighth hour she asks the woman of about sixty-four what she is counting, and is told: **I am not counting. I say the next number out loud and put my hand on the next stone. It has taken about as long as a woman takes to say sixty and it has been the end of my day since I was about thirty-four.** The number is cut into a slate in her apron. The woman of about seventy gave it to her in her first year and told her to say that number and let nobody count for themselves. **Nobody has ever asked her what she is counting; the boy of about fifteen asked in the first year and the man of about sixty asked in about the eleventh and neither was told she does not count.** She says, and this is the second half of the finding, in a speech of her own on its own line and not welded onto the one before it: a man from off the hill stood where the woman of about thirty-eight is standing in the pick sitting, and she told him in her own mouth that she counts, and she did not find out until that morning that there was anything else in it. **The two lines the page gives her are *I am not counting* and *I told him in my own mouth that I count*, and Chapter 0568 has the second of them in her mouth at the eighth hour of the pick sitting, and the woman of about thirty-eight is nowhere in that chapter, so this is a disclosure made here about a scene two sittings and about eight days earlier, and it is canon at `state/continuity.md` 1389.** She does not know what the number is a number of and has had thirty years to want to know and never wanted it enough to ask. She says no to looking. Then she goes round the arc anyway with her hand off the stones, and comes back and says **it is not the number**, and will not say what either number is. **Nobody is told except the two of them.** Column **130 pence**, the woman of about sixty-four's.

**Ch 575** · **the oldest tree in the Long Set, the eighth hour of the blossom sitting · about eleven people tying cords · a spade, a barrow, and a plank about two hands long that has been in the ground for twenty-two winters · a woman of about sixty-four who arrived at her usual hour and found nine people at her tree.** It is dug at the eighth hour because the cords are done, and it is not called a sitting, and there is no room, and nobody writes anything who does not have to write something for a living. **The four who hold ground inside nine yards dig it, and the man of about fifty-one holds the spade and does not dig, and the woman of about thirty-eight digs and has not decided whether she is glad about that.** Under a flat stone there is a plank of green wood gone hard and black with the marks cut into it. She can read the first half and the man of about fifty-one the second, and neither will read the other's. The plank says a strip of ground asked to be let go, about ninety people went out on it, four sets argued about it for nine days, **they were right that it would have come to a quarrel that none of them could have stopped**, and then the remedy: **no argument about a set would ever be written down again.** The man of about sixty says the sentence that the sitting turned on: **then there has been an argument about a set written down in this valley for twenty-two winters and it has been under a tree.** Nobody argued with him. Then a woman of about sixty-four comes up at her usual hour, **says the number out loud because that is what she does, and it is the number cut into the top of the plank, and she is the first person in thirty years to say it correctly.** She says the woman of about seventy cut that number into the wood twenty-two winters ago and told her the same number in her first year and said she was copying it: **there was never a count; I have been a copy walking round a place for thirty years.** Then the man of about fifty-one reads the half nobody wanted to read, which is in another hand with no reason attached: water, out at the foot of a wall and in again at the head of it, on the day the strip was let go — **and he says he is not going to say the two things were done together unless somebody puts it in front of him, and nobody does.** Then the two halves are said rather than read, and the man of about fifty-one says they were right about us and the woman of about thirty-eight says they were right about us and they were wrong about the ground, **and she asks which of those two is the mistake and nobody answers.** The man of about fifty-one puts the plank down flat side up in the light and says out loud that it is not going back in the ground. Column **205 pence**, the man of about fifty-one's.

**Ch 576** · **the first of Harvestmonth, YR 320, a Thursday and a market day · from the second hour to the seventh · a market town of about two thousand people · a weigh-house with a board outside it · a hired room over a saddler's · a woman of about thirty-four who keeps a key · and a man of about fifty-eight with a chain of office that is a badge.** He says in the street outside the weigh-house that the hearing stands, that there is no bench in the room, that nobody is sentenced to anything, that the fourth head of the Crown's form has been ruled and empty about six months, **and that a thing that is true only when somebody says it is not true.** Nobody thanks him; a woman selling eggs asks him whether he wants one. Four people are in the room and one of them is a woman of about thirty-eight from a river city who came down on the cart and is going back up it. **The cost is a place in a room: from the first of a month the room opens at the second hour on market mornings and shuts at the seventh, and because the door has to be locked from the lane while people are on the stair the key is in the lane and the woman who keeps it is in the lane, and for the first hour since the room began she has not been in it on a sitting day.** She says it was a place in a room and not a punishment, because a punishment would mean somebody did it to her, and that thanking a man for telling you first is a way of making him feel better about doing it. The copyist of about twenty-six says again that she is not going to write the fourth head twice, **because once is a copy and twice is a habit.** The half leaf is face down upstairs, nobody turned it over, nobody has been booked to turn it over, and **nobody has said out loud how long it has been lying there.** Nobody knows who washed the floor and nobody was thanked. Aren Kest came down that lane nine days earlier, slept one night under a bed he paid for, and walked out onto the coast road before the second hour, and nobody in that town knows. Column **190 pence**, the woman of about thirty-four's.

**Ch 577** · **the fifth of Harvestmonth, YR 320, a Monday and not a market day · from the second hour to the seventh · a made road out of a market town running down toward the coast · a woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a room over a weigh-house with a door on a lane two hundred miles the other way · and nobody else on the road at any hour of it.** She is sitting on a milestone with her boots off and she says she is not going to ask where he has been, and that if he tells her she will have to write it down and she has a rule about the fourth line. **Neither of them says one word about the four years and a half, and there is no room on either side of that road with a third person standing in it, and nothing was repaired on a milestone and nothing was asked to be.** He tells her what a leaf with four heads and nothing under the fourth is, and she says **it is not a document, it is a hole cut in a leaf that every person who comes after can put something in, and the moment one of those lines has a thing in it that somebody is relying on, a drawer is not a drawer any more.** Then she says the sentence: **a beautiful place is not a free place.** That valley is the most beautiful ground she has stood on in about eleven years and it is beautiful for the reason it is not free, **and the peace is the holding and the holding is the beauty and they come apart the moment you ask a person what they think**, and the result is that about nine households of the low ground find out that the thing they were peaceable about was a holding, and they did not have a vote in it and are not the ones who were wrong. **She says in advance that she is not going to write it down for him, because a page with a finding on it is a document, and then it is a thing about four heads with something in the fourth, and whoever is holding it is whoever is standing nearest to that place.** He puts his hand flat on a gatepost and it does the thing it has done for eleven years and nobody is within nine paces of him. **You go up with a sentence that is not yours, worth more than a page and worth exactly nothing you can hand anybody.** Column **168 pence**, his.

**Ch 578** · **the settlement at the middle terrace, the ninth hour of the set sitting · a plank of green wood lying on the grass in the light with marks cut into it · a board on a wall with four divisions ruled on it and three of them filled · and the first argument this valley has had in about twenty-two years, going on now and not stopping, and nobody in it angry.** Nobody decided to have it; it came the way weather comes, at the ninth hour, because that is when about nine households on the four lowest terraces live most. The man of about fifty-one says he opened the ground and would open it again and has stopped finding reasons to be careful, **and that there is a water mark on a piece of wood under the oldest tree and he has carried a fault he could not say for about nine years.** The man of about sixty says nobody has said what it is a mark of; he says somebody will. The woman of about thirty-eight says she dug it, would open it again and would rather it had not been opened, and that **the record was opened for the water and not for us.** He says the water is the water; she says it is hers too because it is four terraces below her ground. **She says out loud that she would like to be told she was wrong, and the man of about fifty-one declines to, on the grounds that he did not ask to go and did not go either and was about nine, and that if he says it, it will be him being kind.** She says she had hoped he would. The woman of about thirty-one says that nine days ago that plank was in the ground and her four neighbours and she were not losing anything, and that the water question is about them and the four people who can read the plank are all above them on the slope, **and they are going to be the ones who pay for it.** The man of about forty-four says the peace was worth a year and the strip of ground was not worth one. The woman of about seventy says nothing. **The man of about thirty-one gives the only instruction anybody gave that day: whoever reads the plank out loud has to read it in their own voice and not in the one everybody in this valley was given at the table.** Nobody has been told she may. Column **216 pence**, the woman of about thirty-eight's.

**Ch 579** · **the cut, the fourth hour of the set sitting · the fourth hundred pace from the top · about nine yards of lip carrying on a bed that has gone and a line of rubble about two hands thick under it · a man of about fifty-one with a foot-rule · a boy of about fifteen who rings the bell · and a man of thirty-two with a limp who came up the lane in the morning and has been told there is a job.** He comes up the lane with a blister and no money for a cart and goes straight to the cut. The woman of about thirty-eight tells him he is not to ask where he has been and says that not asking is not the same as not wanting to. **The reason is there now: a plank in the daylight that can be carried to nine yards of lip and set beside it.** The man of about fifty-one says there is a job, that he is the only man in nine counties who can do it, and then says why he is not going to, in three reasons: a finding about a road in this valley has to be made by somebody who can be moved off the ground here, and holding nothing is exactly why he cannot be the one who writes it down; a man who finds a fault and gets it acted on gets put in charge of it inside about four days, and about four people have already stopped asking the man of about fifty-one things and started asking this one; and **the man of about fifty-one watched him hold a fault for about four hours in the pick sitting, came back down and asked him twice and was told no twice, and both of the answers were true and both were about a different thing, and he has never said a word about that since, and if a man from off the hill puts a straight edge down then every finding this valley ever makes about its own water has a stranger underneath it.** Aren Kest says no out loud: **the reason is not modesty and not the rule; he wants the job so much he could not account for how much, and a finding with a stranger's hand on it is a finding by a man who came up a lane and is going down it again.** Nobody thanks him for saying no and nobody says out loud that he should have done it. **He is given nine barrows of spoil to take down to the Wet Set and empties them, and the man of about fifty-one does not watch him, does not thank him and does not tell him he did it well.** The straight edge goes down at about the sixth hour with the man of about fifty-one on the lip, the woman of about thirty-eight at the far end, the boy of about fifteen holding the chalk, and **a line of chalk is not a finding until three people have written it down, and none of the three has written anything yet, and it is not a mend.** Column **135 pence**, the boy of about fifteen's.

**Ch 580** · **the board off the middle terrace, the ninth hour of the set sitting · a leaf with four lines under a fourth head · about nine people in the open air · a woman of about thirty-eight who has asked to be the answer for a road and has not been told she may · and a plank of green wood lying on the grass in the light a hundred yards up the slope.** She asks, standing nine paces off the board because she has decided the board is not hers: **will anybody in this valley say out loud that the water has been let out at the foot of a wall and put back in at the head of it?** Nine people are within earshot and **nobody answered.** A man of about forty-four says he has a tree to look at; a woman of about thirty-one says her block is not on the four lowest. **The man of about sixty says he is not going to answer it and says why: in about nineteen years the only questions put to that board have been ones somebody in this valley already knows the answer to, and if he answers this one then in about four years there will be a person whose job is to know things and say them out of a face he was given at the table when he was small.** She says nobody has told her she was wrong since she said it out loud, and says it twice, and will not say it a third time until somebody has actually done it. The man of about fifty-one stops her. Then the woman of about forty-four does the thing she does at the ninth hour and says out loud what is not on her board: a division on a board in a valley with no office in it is a division, is not a head, is not a fifth line and is not going to be a finding, **so that nobody can put a fifth thing on her board and say she let them.** Then somebody asks what is to be done about the carter of about forty who went out in the swell sitting with two sacks of pears and has not come back in, and the answer is that the lane will do what it does. **Nobody filled a head and nobody ruled one. The record is open and the answer is not made. Nobody has paid anything yet.** Column **172 pence**, the woman of about forty-four's.

**The chapters a Volume 12 Batch 0004 writer needs.** **571** holds the second asking, the rule that has no exit, and the refusal to have anything written down. **572** holds the word the outside uses and the report that cannot be produced, and the fact that the chapter does not say what the valley calls itself. **573** holds the argument and the three reasons and the decision to dig at the eighth hour of a working sitting. **574** holds the count as a copy and the one look. **575** holds the plank, what it says, the oath and the burial being the same act, the water mark, and the two halves being said rather than read. **576** holds the hearing standing and the key-keeper's place in the room. **577** holds the sentence and the refusal to write it down. **578** holds the reversal, the wanting to be told she was wrong, and the nine households. **579** holds the job he may not do and the three reasons. **580** holds the unanswered question and the opening state of the cost block. **And the four figures a Batch 0004 writer must not re-collapse: the crossing is one crossing with one bar and was not conducted again; the market town is about four days of carting from the end of the made road while the valley's own measure is about four days and about nine of hard going, and the two are not added and no third distance may be invented; the eleven households and the eleven terraces are one figure of a slope told two ways and are not a figure of persons; and the four heads, the four divisions, the fourth head, the fifth line and the mark at the top of a leaf about nine inches by six are five shapes and not one object and none of them is the Crown's form.**

**Ch 581** · **the head of the Long Set · the second hour of the swell sitting · a plank of green wood lying flat side up in the light a hundred yards up the slope · three people who have seen what is under a lip · a slate, a stick of chalk · and a boy of about fifteen who is not going to be paid for this.** The first trouble is not the writing, it is the three: a finding about a road here has to be made by three people who can each be found and told they are wrong, there is no fourth because nobody else stood on the bank, and the third being the boy is not a detail to be got round. He asks whether this is the bell, is told the bell is a penny and this is nothing, and asks for it to be written that he is doing it for nothing before he does it and not after, and the woman of about thirty-eight writes that down too. She then stops, mid-sentence, and says out loud that she has never been told she may speak for a road, that being let dig a hole is not the same as being told, and that she does not know how she came to be signing a finding about four miles of his water. He puts the chalk down and says the rest of it: a voice is what comes out of a person and can be told it is wrong; a finding is three names against a thing on a bank, and you may come and put your hand on the stone. She says it is three people being counted and that she knows that is not the same as being told and would not have asked if there had been anybody else on the bank. Then the sentence comes out in the oldest voice in that valley, four people on that grass have heard it a hundred times at a table, and she says she is going to sign regardless and would like the three of them to be clear she heard it. They write it in four lines in the valley's order, and the boy rubs out the two words she got wrong and they are the two that mattered. **Nobody put a name at the top of it**, because a name at the top is a thing somebody can be pointed at, and the boy said a slate with three names is also a thing somebody can be pointed at and nobody had an answer. What is under the lip is now on the page for the first time: the lip carries for about nine yards on a bed that has gone and under it is a line of rubble about two hands thick, and the reason is the water mark on the plank. Nobody has been given the finding and the four sets have not been asked. Nobody thanked anybody.

 **A second repair pass removed a penny from a list of four figures of persons and put the boy who is one of the three names in its place, because a penny is not a person and the clause exists to keep the boy's penny out of the column. Count held at four, the penny clause kept whole, and the finding is unchanged: three people, a slate, chalk, no foolscap, and no name at the top of it.****Ch 582** · **the Long Set from the top down · the ninth hour of the swell sitting · four sets of people asked the same question in the same voice on the same morning · about nine hundred drying cloths going over a line · about nine hundred picking cords going into a bundle · the fruit not six weeks off being picked · and a slate with three names on it carried from house to house by a man who keeps the water.** The question is on the slate under the three names and is printed in full and is not counted: *what is your share of four miles*. **It was printed as *four words long* over a question that is seven, and the repair removed the count rather than correcting it, because this block prints no speech-length figure at all; see `state/continuity.md` 1403.** Nobody added to it. The man of about thirty-eight insisted on the same voice in the same asking and she was right. First asked, the middle of the Long Set: yes, and the man of about thirty-one of the Standing gives the reason and puts the figure in his own handwriting and not on the slate, because a slate is a finding and a share of four miles is not a finding. Second, the top of that Long Set: no, for the best reason anybody gave all morning and it is not an excuse — nine terraces above the water and no money in a hand at the end of a pick, and if he puts a share of four miles on that slope he takes it off fruit not picked for about six weeks, and the water stops before that. The woman of about thirty-eight, his neighbour of nine years, lets the whole of it go past without one word in her own mouth and about four people have noticed that she let it go. Third, the four lowest terraces: **yes, before anybody had finished the question**, from a woman of about thirty-one with her four neighbours, of whom one said it was a mad thing, one said nothing, one said she would rather not and was not asked whether she would rather not because then she would have to stand there and watch, which is a thing she has watched a person do in this valley. Her share is the smallest and the dearest and the man who keeps the water reads that out and does not argue with her about it in front of these people. Fourth, the settlement: no, from the woman of about forty-four who keeps the board, who says the record read by four people and not by the valley is not a record the valley can fund, that in about twenty-two years this valley did one thing with a piece of writing and the thing it did was put it in the ground, and that the nine households are not the argument, the nine households are the bill. The woman of about sixty-four ties her ninth hundred cords and says she has walked round the old tree at the eighth hour for thirty years and has never counted the stones and is not going to start now because somebody wants a number off a slate: *you want four miles mended, go and mend four miles.* Nobody was asked to change. Nobody thanked anybody.

 **Checked in the second repair pass and not changed. The four sets are asked one question in the same voice at the ninth hour and answer **yes, no, yes, no**, and this chapter is the one place the page establishes that order, which is what makes the 0584 coda and the 590 coda checkable against it. The woman of about thirty-one says yes before anybody has finished the question and says that one of her four neighbours called it a mad thing, one said nothing and one said she would rather not, and that she has not asked the four of them whether they would rather not. Neither refusal is villainy, nobody was asked to change, and the money column is unchanged at 181 pence.****Ch 583** · **the second terrace of the Long Set · the third hour of the pick sitting · a house with the shutters still shut · a man of about thirty-one of the Standing at the end of a row · a woman of about thirty-eight on a ladder with her back to about nine people · and a thing that was never asked.** He does not ask her to come down, and that is the part he has gone over since. He tells her what the fund is going to do: the money is in for two of the four and the stone goes in at the top, because the men who can lay four miles are the same nine men whichever terrace it is, and at the top the stone is out where a cart can get to it and at the fourth hundred pace it is not. So the top first, then down, and the fourth hundred pace last, and it will be about nine days before the water reaches the four lowest. She asks where the nine came from and he says from two men, the man who keeps the water and a carter, and neither of them counted. She says then it is two men's figure and wants it written that she has said so, and that she is not saying it to make it longer. Nobody checked it and he says he will have it checked and she says she had not asked him to. Then she makes him answer the question he came to avoid: **he put a leaf in her coat about four months ago and never asked whether she wanted it**, and he admits he did not ask because he thought she would say no, and had nine days of walking and a made road and a place to sit down, and asked her the way you ask a person who has already been asked once and has not said no. Her voice does not change, which is what undid him, because she does not use the oldest voice on purpose. He says it out loud, badly, standing, in front of about nine people: that a person with a piece of paper to give should have asked. Nobody thanked him and nobody said he should have said it four months ago. **The arrangement that broke is about thirty years old: the paper out of this valley has gone out with whoever could be made to carry it for about thirty years, and a Standing that asks a person is a Standing that can be told no, and this one has never had to find out. He is the first in about thirty years to send out a person who holds ground, and he was handed the paper by a room and was not asked whether there was anybody else, and that is the whole of what is broken, and it was handed to him.** Nobody thanked him for thirty years of an arrangement that was not his. He is the one it happened to.

 **Checked in the second repair pass and not changed. The man of about thirty-one tells the woman of about thirty38 that he put a leaf about nine inches by six into a drawer in his mother's house about four months ago without asking her, and she makes him say it out loud in front of about nine people, and the arrangement that broke is about thirty years old and he did not keep it and did not break it either. The nine days is on the page as two men's figure and she asks for it written that she took it from two men and not from a count, which is the figure 0587 goes and checks. Nobody has filled a fourth head and nobody is going to, and the money column is unchanged at 184 pence.****Ch 584** · **the four lowest terraces of the Long Set · the sixth hour of the pick sitting · nine men on the channel bank four hundred paces above · a woman of about thirty-one who keeps those four terraces standing in a ditch line with a mattock · and about nine households who have not been asked anything and are not going to be.** **The first cost did not arrive as water.** It arrived as a mattock with nobody else holding it, about nine days before the water was due, and it landed on a woman of about thirty-one who is not in the argument and has never once been in it and has been in this valley all her life. The four lowest hold the water, and what keeps the water off them is not the channel and never was: it is a ditch about two paces wide along the top of the fourth terrace, cleared every sitting by six people for about nine days, and it is the reason four lowest terraces have not gone under in twenty-two winters of not arguing. Nobody has ever written it down. It is not in the plank and the man who keeps the water has said so out loud. The six are the four lowest and a boy of about nineteen. At the sixth hour there is one, and about four days of cutting left, and she comes up out of the ditch and says plainly that there is no water on the four lowest, which is the good news, and that she is the one person who can say so because she has been standing in the ditch for five days, and that in about nine days the water will cross the first terrace and in about sixteen all four, and that the reason it is nine and not sixteen is a ditch being cut this year by one. He offers four men back on the ditch that afternoon. She does the arithmetic out loud: put four men on my ditch and I have it in four days instead of sixteen and the four of us are under for sixteen instead of nine, and I have lost seven days of a season nobody is going to give back, and you have lost nothing. He says he worked it out on the second day and said nothing on the second day and said nothing on the third, and that the reason is that a man who takes four men off a channel in the first three days is a man who will take them off it in the first three days of everything for the rest of his life, and he had wanted very much not to have that thought. She tells him he has done nothing to her, that she said yes before he finished the question, that nobody is going to be at fault, and that it is a thing she has not got used to saying out loud and has had about four days to get used to and has not. He puts the barrow-wheel down in the grass, which he has not done in front of anybody in about nine years, and says he has a finding with three names on it and not one thing he can do with it that is not somebody else's ground. She says: then do not do anything, go up. He goes up. Nobody watched him go. She finishes about a third of what six people would have. Nobody thanked anybody.

 **The second repair pass fixed the chapter's central speech. As first written the woman of about thirty-one refused the man of about fifty-one's four men on the ground that putting them on her ditch would leave the four of us under for sixteen instead of nine, which is the opposite of what she says at the sixth hour, that a cut ditch is what has kept the water off the four lowest for twenty-two winters, so the refusal argued for the harm. The two clauses are rewritten: four men gives her the ditch in four days instead of sixteen and the four of them dry this year, she says she would take them and is not going to take them, and the reason is that a ditch is cut every sitting and the channel has not been mended in about nine years and four men on her ditch buys one year off him. Seven days of a season nobody is going to give back stays and is now stated once. The coda's *the four who said no* is also corrected to the two sets who said no, because 0582 and 0590 both have two of four. `state/continuity.md` 1398 quoted the defective arithmetic and has been corrected to the page.****Ch 585** · **the first of Fallowmonth, YR 320 · a Saturday and a market day · from the second hour to the seventh · a market town of about two thousand people · a weigh-house with a board outside it and a street about nine paces wide · a hired room over a saddler's · a woman of about thirty-four who keeps a key · and a man of about fifty-eight with a chain of office that is a badge who says one thing out loud on the first of a month and is not thanked for it.** He says it in the street outside the weigh-house, that the hearing stands, there is no bench, nobody is sentenced to anything, and there is a form on the side table with four heads and the fourth ruled and empty for about six months, and he will not rule a line above it or below it, and he says it on the first of every month because a thing that is true only when somebody says it is not true. **About nine people heard it and nobody came for it**, which is the whole of the first of Fallowmonth in that town, and a woman selling eggs asked him whether he wanted one. Then the reconciliation the block needed, said out loud in the street before anything else: **the four weeks he gave on the first of Harvestmonth were up on the twenty-eighth of that month, the Thursday after was the first market morning past them and the room was shut on it, he thought about it overnight and did not sleep much, and something has been said that makes it wrong, so the door stays open on market mornings for one more week and it is his and not hers and he said that to her on the day he changed it and she was not thanked and she was not.** So the key is in the lane and the person who keeps it is in the lane. Four people in the room: the copyist of about twenty-six, the woman of about thirty-four, a man of about thirty-eight with a satchel, and the man with the badge, and a fifth thing in the room was a chair and the chair was empty and nobody has said why. Between the fourth hour and the seventh the room does what a room does. **Nobody put a name at the top of anything; the copyist wrote on a bare leaf with no head over it and said again that she is not going to write the fourth head twice, and neither of the two was a slip; the pen was in a case and the case was shut.** **And upstairs the half leaf lay face down, nobody turned it over, nobody has been booked to turn it over, and nobody has said how long it has been there, which is the one figure the thing does not give up.** The woman of about thirty-four says two things to the lane, that a place in a room is a thing she had and has not got and is a place and not a punishment, and that there is a difference between a Thursday and a market Saturday that she has worked out and has not been thanked for working out. Nobody wrote it down. Nobody in that town has been told that nine men four days and about nine up a lane are laying stone badly and that about nine households have about four days of cutting left and no money and no vote.

 **The second repair pass fixed two things here. A sentence had come apart at the pot of earth and read *nobody in this room has been told that in nine days and has not asked*; it now reads *nobody in this room has been told any of that and has not asked*. And the half-leaf paragraph lifted forty consecutive words out of `chapter-0576.md` with only its commas moved, which was the longest run of words any chapter of this block shared with any of the five hundred and eighty before it; the paragraph now says the same four facts in its own order and the run is gone. The four facts are untouched: face down, a question under its fourth head that nobody in nine counties has answered, not turned over, nobody booked to turn it over, and no interval for it, which is the one figure the thing itself does not give up. The chapter also resolves on the page what the batch card left open, that the four weeks the man with the badge gave on the first of Harvestmonth, day 1746, were up on the twenty-eighth, that the Thursday after them, day 1774, was the first market morning past them and the room was shut on it, and that he is leaving the door open for one more week and says in the street that it is his and not hers.****Ch 586** · **the top of the cut · the second hour of the pick sitting · about three hundred paces of stone about two paces wide with a lip along the high side · nine people on it · a man of thirty-two with a limp off the hill with a barrow · and about four hundred paces of the same work still to be done by the same nine people.** The dateline's age was fifty-eight as the chapter was first written and is corrected here and on the page at `state/continuity.md` 1403, because the body of the same chapter establishes the man from off the hill as thirty-two with a limp, and the woman of about fifty-eight who turns the cloths is a different person. It is ordinary work and it is badly done and both are true on the same morning and neither is anybody's fault. They began on the low side because it is a foot and a half of soil and the high side is two feet, and then they laid the bed the way you lay a path, small down and big on, because that is what a path wants and a channel does not. The man of about fifty-one watches about nine men lay about three hundred paces of it wrong for about four hours and does not say so, and when he does say so he does not say it is a fault: there is no fault in it, there are nine people who have not laid a course, and he should not have let them do three hundred paces of it. Asked why he let them, he says he could not think of the sentence that would not sound like a man from this valley telling nine other people of this valley that they are doing their own water wrong, and that in about nine years of not saying things he has lost the habit of saying them and not only the reason. The man of about thirty-one of the Standing writes it on a slate, and the slate is not the finding. **The man from off the hill is not put in charge of anything, is not asked to inspect anything, and is not asked his opinion once**; he is given a barrow at the second hour and one only, and the job is spoil, about nine barrows to every four paces, about four hundred paces to go. He is good at it, he is a man of thirty-two with a limp and a ferrier's stick and eleven years of a job that is not barrowing, and the boy of about fifteen barrows with him, is faster, will not be, keeps the two of them in a line all morning unasked, and says nothing about it. Nobody thanked either of them and the man who keeps the water did not watch and did not say he had done it well. **And at the sixth hour he says out loud, to everybody, that three hundred paces is wrong and four hundred paces of the same work is still in front of nine people and four of them have never laid a course, and that when they get down there he is going to say they cannot do it — and he is saying it now, at the top, four hundred paces early, so that nobody can say afterwards that they did not know.** Nobody thanked him for saying it early and nobody said it should have been said four days ago. The work stops at the ninth hour. Three hundred paces of wrong bed lie in the light and nobody turns one stone over. Nobody thanked anybody.

 **The second repair pass corrected a figure of distance that stood four lines from another figure of distance in five places here. The dateline, the body, the man of about fifty-one's speech at the sixth hour, the coda and the closer all said about four hundred paces of the same work still to be done, on a morning when three hundred paces of it were already done, against 0590's own about a hundred paces nobody has touched at all between the three hundred and the four hundred and 0589's *I am not asking you for the four hundred, I am asking you for the forty*. All five now read about a hundred paces before they stand on the fault, and the four hundred is kept where it belongs, as the length of the mend beyond the fault; the speech's four hundred paces early became a hundred paces early, which is the same promise made at the same moment and now true of the ground. The coda's eight-figure list still has eight items. The chapter also now names him: Aren Kest is named in the paragraph that introduces him on the bank, he is given a barrow and nothing else, he is not put in charge of anything and not asked to inspect anything, he is good at it, and nobody told him he had done it well.****Ch 587** · **the four lowest terraces of the Long Set · the fifth hour of the pick sitting · water coming down a channel about two paces wide and standing in the first line of fruit trees · about nine households who were told on the day before · a woman of about thirty-one with a stick and a line of stones · and a boy of about fifteen who rings the bell and is paid a penny and has not been asked what the bell is for.** They were told the day before and not after it, by a woman of about thirty-one who is not a keeper of anything anybody can put a name to, walking nine doors with nine words the man of about thirty-one wrote on a slate at her asking and would not carry her round himself. Four of the nine said nothing, three said it would not happen, one said it had happened before and did not last, and she told that one it had happened once and the ditch was cut that year by six and is being cut this year by one. **And the nine days was counted**, by her, with a stick and about nine stones put down the high side at the water's pace, because a figure two men had is not a number and she wanted it counted by a person who was going to be standing in it. **Nine days and a morning.** That is what two men who had not counted had said, and the man of about fifty-one read it off the slate and said he had expected them to be wrong, and that being right by accident is not a thing to be glad about and is not a thing to be sorry about, and wrote the morning on it because she asked him to and he has not been thanked and is not going to be. The water comes at about the seventh hour and stands in the first line of trees by the eighth, and it is not a flood and it carries nothing away; it spreads, first to second in about a day and a half, to the third in three, to the fourth in five, and the fourth has a house on it. The bell goes at the four points and he is paid his penny and nobody has ever said which of the two the penny is for. **The fruit is the loss and the water is only the reason: about a fifth of what the Long Set will pick, the earliest and warmest, standing in nine days of water does not go on a cart, and a cart cannot get to the bottom of that slope in the wet, and it is on no road in nine counties and is not going to be on one, and the difference between losing a thing and losing a thing that can be sold is the whole of what has happened to her this month.** The settlement could take it; she says it said no to four miles of stone and she said yes on the same slate and this is not blame. About nine households are under water and none of them read the plank and none of them chose the oath and the man who reads half of it aloud is four hundred paces above them laying stone the wrong way up, and the woman of about thirty-eight who signed the finding is two terraces above the water line tying cords, and neither has come down. Nobody thanked anybody.

 **The second repair pass fixed a typo — *watching the channel hisself* is now *himself* — and nothing else in this chapter moved. The count is still nine days and a morning, put on a slate at the fifth hour on the morning the water arrived by a woman of about thirty-one with a stick and about nine stones, the distribution is unchanged at a day and a half to the second terrace, three to the third and five to the fourth, and the water still comes down at about the seventh hour on the first morning and stands in the first line of trees by the eighth.****Ch 588** · **the middle of the Long Set · the eighth hour of the pick sitting · about nine hundred drying cloths on about nine hundred feet of line on the only flat ground within four hundred paces of the fourth hundred pace · a woman of about fifty-eight who turns them · about nine people standing about · and a decision taken in the open by a person who was not asked to take it and is not a keeper of anything.** Nobody argued about it, and four people have put out one version of that and nine another and the two sets do not overlap. The fourth hundred pace is the only part that has to be right, nine people cannot do it, and the man of about fifty-one said so four days ago at the top so nobody could say afterwards they had not known. There are four men within four days and about nine who can lay that bed, one of them a man of about sixty-four on the second terrace whose hands will last about nine days. Getting there takes about nine barrows of spoil to every four paces, barrows need flat ground, and the only flat ground within four hundred paces is a strip about nine hundred feet long with about nine hundred drying cloths on it. The woman of about fifty-eight says the arithmetic out loud and says there is no second way of doing it and she is not going to pretend there is. He says the thing nobody else will say: then they come down. **She decides it, and the reason she gives is the chapter.** The cloths are not his: if they were his she would be asking a man to put nine hundred of his own on the ground for a channel that will keep working for him for thirty years, and he would have said yes because he is that sort of man, and she would have taken it. The cloths are not his. About four fifths are the fruit of people who cannot get at a barrow run and one is hers, and the person who should decide is the one who turns them, and she has not decided anything in her life. A day, four people from the third hour, into the press-house and under the middle terrace wall, and the ones under the wall come out damp, and **about two in three of the nine hundred come out dry** — a number she has not counted and had written down that she has not got it. **And the woman of about thirty-one on the four lowest is four terraces down under nine days and a morning of water, about a fifth of that fruit was on those lines, nobody was in that forty yards, nobody went down and told her, the cloths came down at the third hour and were under the wall by the ninth hour, and she was told at about the ninth hour of the day after.** The woman of about thirty-eight reads the slate and puts it face down on the grass, which she has never done to a slate, and will not let him pick it up: not now, it is hers and you would be standing in her light with a slate. It goes down the next morning and is put on a wall by the fourth terrace. She said the two in three was about right and that being pleased is not the same as being allowed and this was the first time she had watched the two of them come apart with a number in her hand. **Nobody was angry, and about four of the nine gave as their reason that nobody in that forty yards had done anything wrong, and about nine gave as their reason that it was worse than angry, because a thing nobody can be angry about has already been agreed to and nobody knows who agreed to it.** The man of about sixty is told at the ninth hour, asks whether he was asked, by whom, and whether it was right, gets no answer to the third, and says there is no room to put it in, that is not a rule and not a precedent, that in about four years somebody will say a woman once did a thing on her own and they will be right, and he is not going to say they are wrong and is not going to say they are right, and tells them not to write it down. Nobody thanked anybody.

 **Checked in the second repair pass and not changed. A woman of about fifty-eight who turns about nine hundred drying cloths, is not a keeper of anything and has never been asked the Standing's advice on a working morning, decides at the eighth hour that the cloths come off the lines, because the cloths are not the man of about fifty-one's and about four fifths of them are the fruit of people who cannot get at a barrow run and one of them is hers. The woman of about thirty31 on the four lowest is not in that forty yards, nobody went down and told her, and she is told at about the ninth hour of the day after. Nobody was angry. The man of about sixty is told at the ninth hour, asks whether he was asked and by whom and whether it was right, and is given no answer to the third. The two in three was not counted and the money column is unchanged at 227 pence.****Ch 589** · **the fourth hundred pace of the cut · the ninth hour of the pick sitting · about nine yards of lip carrying on a bed that has gone · a barrow standing empty on the low side · a man of thirty-two with a limp who has been given nothing to do since the second hour · and about nine people on a bank who are four days and about nine of hard going from the only person in nine counties who could do what is in front of them.** The job comes to him as it came the first time, offered out loud and in order, and this time there is one reason and it is the good one: put about forty paces of the fourth hundred in down in a day, not the four hundred, and the man of about fifty-one does not give the rest of the reason, because he gave it last time and has not found a better version in about nine days, and says the reason has not gone away and that Aren Kest knows what it is. **He does not say it.** He barrows from the second hour to the ninth hour and is good at it and the boy of about fifteen keeps the line with him unasked, and nobody watches and nobody says he has done it well. Then the woman of about thirty-eight comes down off the high side, stands at the end of about nine yards of lip, and says it **in the oldest voice in that valley**, and the page certifies no length, which both of them hear arrive in a voice she was given at a table when she was small: he was wrong about the first refusal and has been wrong every day since. The rule is good and she has said so twice. But the rule says *a person who can be moved off the ground here* and it does not say *a person who holds nothing*, and he has been treating those as the same thing for since the set sitting: she holds four blocks, has held them nine years, dug a hole at the foot of the oldest tree with a spade in her own hand in front of about eleven people, said yes to about ninety people when she was about sixteen and did not go, and nobody in this valley has ever been able to move her off that ground including her. So the rule is the rule and he is not outside it, and the only reason nobody asks him for a second thing is that he has not said yes and nobody here has worked out that a man who does not say no is a man who has already been told, which is the worst place in the world to be a man who does not say no, because it is the one place where being quiet is not a thing anybody can do for you. Then she stops, gets to her own voice, and says she is not saying it to be kind, he should be laying forty paces of that bed right now, she is not going to say it again, and nobody in this valley is going to thank him either way. **Nobody thanked him for not doing it and nobody said out loud that he should have done it**, and the man of about thirty-one of the Standing came down to ask the question and did not ask it and said nothing at all, the first time in about nineteen years he has stood on a channel bank and said nothing. He does not put a straight edge on that lip and does not hold one end of it; the chalk line is where it was, cut in the set sitting, now under about four inches of new course and invisible from the top, and nobody cut a second one.
