The finding with three names on it is on a stone at the head of that Long Set and is not his and will not be. **And the column below is not his and not hers and belongs to nobody on that bank except a boy and the woman of about thirty-eight**, and the boy was paid his penny. Nobody thanked anybody.

 **The second repair pass named him in the sentence that says nobody has asked him which of the two he believes, so that `Aren Kest` now stands at 3 in two of ten chapters against the 1 in one of ten the block was first measured at. Nothing he does was changed: he is offered about forty paces of the fourth hundred pace with the reason attached and the reason not repeated, he does not say no out loud, he barrows from the second hour to the ninth hour, the woman of about thirty-eight tells him in the oldest voice in that valley that he was wrong about his own refusal and that a man who stands on a rule is not obeying it and that in a place where nothing is voted a man who does not say no is a man who has already been told, and nobody thanks him for not doing it and nobody says out loud that he should have done it. He puts no straight edge on the lip and holds no end of one, and the chalk line cut in the set sitting is where it was.****Ch 590** · **the head of the Long Set · the ninth hour of the last day of the pick sitting · a finding with three names on it lying on a stone · about four hundred paces of stone about two paces wide with a lip on the high side · a barrow standing on the low side · water coming down a channel that is not mended · and the turn of the five sittings with nothing tidied.** The work stopped because the work stops, nobody stopped it, and the difference between those two things is the whole of what happened in this valley. About four hundred paces of bed of four miles has been laid at the fourth hundred pace, and it is good work, and the man of about fifty-one said so once and the man of about forty-four said it was the best course he had laid in about nineteen years and went and did not talk about it. The top three hundred paces is wrong and will stay wrong until a year nobody has arranged for, and about a hundred paces between them has not been touched. About nine yards of the lip is new stone on a bed that has gone, the new stone is holding, and the chalk line is under it and cannot be seen from the top, and nobody cut a second one. **The water is back where it was**: it came down at the seventh hour on the ninth day, the ninth day was a morning, and it went back to the four lowest at about the fifth hour of the day after, and the four lowest have their water and the top has its water, and the channel between them is what it was except for about four hundred paces, which is better and is not enough. The woman of about thirty-one is on the second of the four lowest with about nine people and about four neighbours, and the only thing anybody says that morning that is not about stone is *there it is*: the water back on four terraces in a turn of the five, a fifth of the pick off the bottom two, about a third of what was drying gone, picking in about three weeks, about two thirds of what she had, in a year when the four hundred paces of new bed has been under a winter and will be better then than now. Nobody thanked her and nobody said that was a good thing to have said; the man who keeps the water walked up for it unasked and said it was true and would not say whether it was good. **The four sets said yes and no and yes and no in that order and nobody was asked to change and nobody has changed; the money was found by the two that said yes, it is gone, and it bought about four hundred paces of bed, about nine hundred feet of barrow run, about nine hundred cloths off about nine hundred feet of line in one day, a month of bread for about nine men and a boy's penny on four days of a sitting, and about four days of a carter, and it did not buy four miles and was never going to and the two sets that said no were not wrong about that and have not been asked whether they were right.** Nobody has said thank you in this valley since the plank came out of the ground. The plank is on a stone with its marks uppermost, the hole is filled with its own loam and a flat stone on the loam and not on the plank, about nine yards of grass has grown up round the flat stone, and the man of about fifty-one said on the day it came out that it is not going back in the ground and nobody has said where it ought to be kept; the woman of about seventy came down and said nothing and nobody asked her why. **The answer is not made.** Nobody has filled a fourth head; the five documents are in five places and none of them is the Crown's form, the plank is a sixth object, the board's four divisions are divisions and not heads, the fifth line ruled on a Monday morning by nobody who ordered it is a fifth line and is not a fourth head and is not a sixth, and the half leaf is face down upstairs with nobody booked to turn it over and nobody who has said how long it has been there. The turn of the five sittings came at the ninth hour and the bar sitting begins, and no month is a sitting and no sitting is a month. **The bar of wood is still in the grass at the foot of the Low Lane, it was never lifted, nobody has asked whether it should be, and the carter of about forty has still not come back in and the fruit of that day is on no road in nine counties**, and the answer about him was that the lane will do what it does. Nobody thanked anybody. **The second repair pass made three changes here and all three are figures. The water came down on the ninth day as first written and the ninth day was called a morning; it now comes down on the first morning at about the seventh hour, as 0587 has it, and goes back off the four lowest at about the fifth hour of the day after the ninth, and the ninth is a morning as well as a day and that is on a slate in this valley and is not in a book. The dearest and smallest share was given to the woman of about thirty-eight and then to the woman of about thirty-one, so that two women held one superlative and one of them did not hold it at all, against 0582 which gives it to the woman of about thirty31 alone; the woman of about thirty-eight, who keeps four blocks on that middle, now gave a share and has not said what her part came to, and the share of the woman of about thirty-one who keeps the four lowest was the smallest of the four and the dearest of the four, and no household is given a figure. And the men's paragraph now reads the way the chapter's own end-state reads: about four hundred paces of good new bed at the fourth hundred pace, the top three hundred wrong and going to stay wrong, and about a hundred paces between them untouched.**

**The chapters a Volume 12 Batch 0005 writer needs.** **581** holds the finding's shape, the three, the boy who is paid a penny for a bell and nothing for this, the four lines and the refusal of a name at the top, and what is under the lip. **582** holds the four sets, the seven words of the question, the order yes-no-yes-no, both reasons for the two refusals, and the woman's nine doors and the two in three. **583** holds the leaf in a drawer, the thirty-year arrangement of sending out whoever can be made to carry it, the first man in thirty years to send a person who holds ground, and the admission in front of about nine people. **584** holds the ditch, the six people, the arithmetic of four days against sixteen, and the first cost arriving as a mattock with nobody else holding it. **585** holds the standing, the nine who heard it and the nine who did not come, the four weeks that were up on the twenty-eighth of Harvestmonth, the room open in the lane, the bare leaf with no head over it, and the half leaf face down. **586** holds the wrong bed, the two hundred paces of low side, the man's one reason and the refusal to give the rest of it, the barrow, and the announcement four hundred paces early that nine of them cannot do the fourth hundred pace. **587** holds nine days and a morning, the counted figure on a slate, the two men who were right by accident, and the fruit that is on no road in nine counties. **588** holds the decision, the two in three that was not counted, the four hours of darkness, and the man of about sixty's three questions and refusal to answer the third. **589** holds the speech in the oldest voice, which the page prints in six turns and certifies no length on, the rule that does not say a person who holds nothing, and the barrow standing empty from the seventh hour to the ninth. **590** holds what was mended and what was not, the water back, the four sets and the money and what the money bought, and the six things that did not move.

## Volume 12, Batch 0005 — Chapters 591 to 600, "What a Beautiful Place Is"

**Ch 591** · **Fallowmonth the twenty-first, YR 320, a Friday, day 1796 · a working morning on the fourth hundred pace · the second hour of the bar sitting · about nine men on a bank · a slate with three names · and a question nobody has put in about nineteen years.** The bar sitting opens with nothing tidied: about four hundred paces of good new bed at the fourth hundred pace, the top three hundred wrong, about a hundred untouched, nine days and a morning counted, the finding on a stone nobody has been asked to receive, the money spent, the ditch cut by one, about nine hundred cloths with about two in three dry and uncounted, and the bar of wood unlifted in the grass. The boy of about fifteen asks out loud, of nobody in particular: who here is allowed to say no — whether a finding stands with two if one of three refuses, since four holders said she would rather not without stopping anything and two sets said no without stopping the money. The man of about sixty, who made that rule in about four minutes, says nothing. The woman of about thirty-eight, who asked twice to be told she was wrong and was told nothing, is the only one who hears the difference between the question being asked and the question being allowed, and tells him so in her own voice: nobody here has been given the right to refuse, and a valley that cannot say no has not said yes either. Aren Kest barrows and says nothing. Nobody thanked anybody. Column **148 pence**, the boy's. Body 1,714 words, the longest in the block and the chapter the review named the best writing in.

**Ch 592** · **Fallowmonth the twenty-fourth, YR 320, a Monday, day 1799 · about nine hundred drying cloths on about nine hundred feet of line · the ninth hour of the bar sitting · a barrow run wanted on flat ground · and two women who disagree about a cord and a line and are both right.** The reopening is not a meeting, a room, a vote or a quarrel: the woman of about fifty-eight who turns the cloths wants them down so barrows can run, and the woman of about sixty-four who ties the cords wants the lines to stand so the posts hold, and both are right about the same nine hundred feet of posts. The argument is settled on the ground, not on the valley: a white groove in a line where a cord has been biting is shown to be the post moving, two boards are carried from the press-house and one of them puts its corner into the soft edge and stays there, and a cloth comes off and is smelled and not reported. About four standing by take it the cloths should come down and about five take it the lines should stand, and the two sets do not overlap, and the two who argued hardest are not the two who were wrong about the water. The middle three lines come down, the cords stay, about two in three come out dry uncounted, three posts are lost and tied again, and nobody on that strip got what it wanted. The man of about sixty is told and answers about the asking and will not answer about the right. Nobody was angry and nobody was thanked. Column **162 pence**, the woman's who turns the cloths.

**Ch 593** · **Fallowmonth the twenty-sixth, YR 320, a Wednesday, day 1801 · the third of the four lowest terraces · the fifth hour of the bar sitting · nine doors and a slate · and one household told a thing that cannot be refused.** This is the second walk of that row and it goes faster, because a slate is a poor thing to tell a door with and the woman of about thirty-one had the man of the Standing write it out for her at the fourth hour. The woman of about thirty-one tells one of her four neighbours that the household's back strip goes back for a season to hold stone for the untouched hundred paces, and will come back after the pick with new stone at its edge. It is not a punishment and not a payment and cannot be refused. The woman says she would rather not, and it does not stop it, and the teller writes it nowhere because there is no sheet and will remember it and will tell anybody who asks; the four-minute rule is neither repealed nor vindicated, and both facts sit on either side of it. The strip is then walked and stood on, because the woman who gave it away is the one who will stand in it, and it is the best ground on the row, and that is the whole of the reason it was wanted. The cost is read out plain: nine days and a morning of water gone, a fifth of the pick off the bottom two, and now the strip — landed on people who never had a vote and are not the people who were wrong. Aren Kest carries stone through it and stays, because to leave would call the telling shameful and it is not. The man of about sixty is told and will not say whether the rule is right. Column **170 pence**, the woman's of the four lowest.

**Ch 594** · **Fallowmonth the twenty-eighth, YR 320, a Friday, day 1803 · a man of thirty-two with a limp on the low side from the third hour to the ninth · about nine barrows of spoil · about four hundred holding one true thing in a market town and about four hundred holding another · and a thing he is not asked to do.** He has refused one job out loud and has not said no out loud to a second, and he decides neither again. The day is barrowing, from the third hour to the ninth, and the wheel finds the wet ground on the second run and the barrow goes a hand's width off the line and is lifted, and the boy comes back for it unasked and they take that run in single loads and lose about twenty minutes that nobody writes down. What is asked of him is barrowing, and he does it well, and what only he in nine counties could do is see that the low side wants a second fan cut or the bank will slip — and saying it to one other person would be acting on the load-bearing problem, which he may see and may not do, on this last ground where that rule holds him. He barrows past the place four times and says nothing, and on the third pass there is a stone in the face above it with a fresh crack and grit under it that no man in nine counties can read the way he can, and the man of about fifty-one has his back to it at the time, and the bank does not slip that day either and the stone is still there at the ninth hour. About four hundred in the town say the record stands up because of him and about four hundred say anything was believed because of him, and the two sets do not overlap, and he walks between them the way a man walks between two fields. The woman of about thirty-eight sees where his eyes went, looks at his leg without naming it, and says he barrows well. Nobody thanked him. Column **176 pence**, his.

**Ch 595** · **Embermonth the first, YR 320, day 1806 · a Monday and not a market day · the second hour in a lane outside a weigh-house · and a man of about fifty-eight with a badge who says the hearing stands.** He says it in the street and not in the room, on the only first of a month inside the block: the hearing stands, there is no bench, nobody is sentenced to anything, the room opens market mornings second to seventh hour, and the key stays in the lane because the door locks from the lane. The nine who came came on a market-place notice that said the first of every month without saying which first, so they walked down a lane on a Monday and found a lane and a key and a woman, and nobody in that lane says out loud that a room which stands monthly and opens on market mornings is two different rooms. Upstairs: a register at seven entries all questions and no findings, a day-book at nine, a press open with no date anybody will give, a drawer eleven inches deep with about four hundred returns and no count made of it, and three series — about nine hundred sheets a month out, about a hundred and four in one band on a fixed day, about four hundred and nine parish rolls in — none derived from another. The woman of about thirty-four keeps the key in the lane and calls her lost place a place and not a punishment. The copyist writes one line on a bare leaf, reads it back, puts the pen away, and will not write the fourth head twice. At the fifth hour the woman of about twenty-nine volunteers that a valley four days and about nine off has reopened a shut argument and lost nine households' ground to a mend, and the only other person in the lane does not ask her one question about it. The two of them repair nothing, share no room with a third, and the four and a half years are not said. Nobody thanked anybody. Column **179 pence**, the woman's who keeps the key.

**Ch 596** · **Embermonth the sixth, YR 320, a Saturday, day 1811 · three names on a slate at the sixth hour of the bar sitting · four blocks held nine years · a man of the Standing who would have to write a name he will not write · and a person who can be refused.** The slate has lain on its stone since before the bar sitting came in and nobody has been asked to receive it. The certification refused in Greenmonth stands refused before anybody in a room could say it, and the man of about thirty-one wants a name written to carry down, and the man of about fifty-one will not write one: a finding is three people who can be found, and what is asked for is one person to be pointed at, and if she is the water because she is the only one who will stand up then the water is one woman and the water is refused whenever she is. The woman of about thirty-eight says it in the valley's own words, in one breath: a road may be described only by its own answer, and the answer is a person who can be refused by her own valley, and she is not a keeper of anything and is on no roll and was not thanked. The person is the woman of about sixty-four who ties the cords, keeps nothing, is paid in fruit, and told a man from off the hill in her own mouth that she counts — and she is not on the slope that hour and is not asked. Nobody writes a name before the person is asked, because a name written first is a name somebody has been made to carry. The boy reads the three names aloud because nobody else will, and the man of about fifty-one says seeing with your own eyes is the only condition ever asked of that slate, and it is the only one on it. Aren Kest carries his barrow past and is not asked to witness. Nobody thanked either of them. Column **188 pence**, the woman's who keeps the block.

**Ch 597** · **Embermonth the eleventh, YR 320, a Thursday, day 1816 · the Low Gate at the eighth hour of the bar sitting · a bar of wood lying in the grass that nobody lifts · four questions on a board · and a man crossing out with a person and not with a leaf.** Aren Kest comes down with his stick and his coat, and beside him the woman of about sixty-four with her slate in her apron, neither carrying a leaf or a certificate. The keeper asks her three that are hers to ask, and the woman answers in the valley's own words with nothing above the answer but the sky: she will speak and her valley may refuse her, she keeps nothing and is on no roll, nobody sent her, and she goes down to say the valley will come to the gathering and may be refused, with its right to refuse explicit, or go back up. The keeper says pass and does not touch the bar, and no hand is laid on wood that day. Then the crossing is walked rather than asserted: the lane in three turns losing about a mile of height, the last house and a dog that does not come back, the channel heard and not seen, the woman talking about how loud it will be in a month, the stones of the made road showing in the last of the light, a stop at the trough because his leg wanted it, the mist coming up the lane and stopping, and the valley out of sight behind the ridge within about two hundred paces. Neither looks back and neither says why. The night's cost is spoken by the keeper after they go — he will not remember this night — and it is not a wound and not a leaf. Nobody thanked anybody. Column **185 pence**, the woman's who keeps the gate.

**Ch 598** · **Embermonth the sixteenth, YR 320, a Tuesday, day 1821 · the middle terrace at the third hour of the bar sitting · a delegate chosen without a vote · a written refusal of the right to be thanked · and nobody angry.** Nothing is voted, and a man with a basket of small stone stops walking the doors and stands there through all of it with the basket on his hip. The woman of about sixty-four will go to the gathering the low roads are making, keeping nothing and on no roll and able to be refused, and nobody writes her name at the top of anything. The man of about sixty asks his three questions and answers none: who is going, what she holds while she is gone, and who could call her back — the second is answered out loud and what she holds is nothing. Her first act, out loud, believed and not tidied: the valley's right to refuse contact stays explicit, and she refuses to be thanked for coming, and the woman of about forty-four writes both down unsoftened on a bare leaf and reads them back before the ink sets and asks whether she wants a word changed. Nobody thanks the delegate. A document about ground where every argument was once put under a tree lies in the room and is not the Crown's form, and the man of about thirty-one counts the objects out loud and stops at the delegate's own leaf and does not say which of the others it is like, because it is not like any of them. The man of about sixty will not say whether going is right. About four of nine say the valley sent its oldest counter, about five say it sent the one nobody would miss, and the two sets do not overlap. The Low Gate is three miles down the lane and the bar of wood is still in the grass and still there when she comes past it. Column **189 pence**, the woman's who keeps the board.

**Ch 599** · **Embermonth the twenty-first, YR 320, a Sunday, day 1826 · the four lowest terraces at the fifth hour of the bar sitting · about nine households a season poorer on their own ground · and a bill presented and not paid.** The woman of about thirty-one reads it to the ground, twice, once by the woman who owes it and once by nobody, and neither reading is disputed: the households and their ground, the valley's twenty-two years of not arguing given up and not got back, the woman whose count was never a count, the cutting that arrived and opened nothing, and a man of thirty-two with a limp who never touched the lip and is further from the work than at the start — and that last is not made into a safe place to end. The two unchosen pairs are now two separate conversations rather than one paragraph: about four would lay the bill at the door of the two sets that said no and about five at the door of the two that said yes, and the nine households that paid cannot tell from the shape of the reading which door it belongs behind; and the man has stood in the middle of both halves of the market town's opinion of him without being asked which he holds, and the man of about fifty-one says none of it out loud because it is not the fifth hour of a Sunday for it. The woman of about sixty-four stands at the ditch, ties a hundred and forty of about nine hundred cords, works one round a thorn without dropping it, and says she counted and did not count and both are true, and when a neighbour asks which of the two it was the woman of about thirty-one refuses to pick one to make the reading easier. Aren lays one stone wrong and is shown and lays it right, and that small rightness stands beside the large unpaid bill without excusing it. Column **193 pence**, the woman's of the four lowest.

**Ch 600** · **Embermonth the twenty-fifth, YR 320, day 1830 · a Thursday and a market day · the second hour in a lane · a hearing that stands with no bench · a fourth head still empty · a floor washed by somebody nobody knows · and a leaf face down on a table upstairs.** Aren Kest comes up the lane dusty with four days and about nine, at the pace his leg allows, and the woman of about twenty-nine stands in it with the key and lets him get all the way to her, and no third person enters it the whole hour. The form lies with three heads filled and the fourth ruled and empty at about six months, unfilled and unsaid to be filled anywhere. The floor was washed by somebody nobody knows, with no interval given, and nobody was thanked. The half leaf lies face down, unturned, unscheduled, undescribed. The copyist's bare leaf carries the delegate's written refusal to be thanked for coming, unsoftened and unthanked, beside a document about put-away ground that is not the Crown's form. A man with a sheet asks whether the hearing is over and is told it stands on market mornings and had begun at the second hour, and takes away the one of her three true sentences he can use. He says it is a beautiful place; she says the sentence he needed, in her words and not his: a beautiful place is still an owned place. The non-repair is shown rather than listed — she turns the key over without putting it in the lock, he does not go on, and neither of them says why — and neither says anything of the four and a half years. A cart comes up the lane too fast, she moves the key out of its way without hurrying, and later she puts the key in her apron, which is not where it lives. The two readings of him stand unchosen. Nobody thanked anybody. Column **195 pence**, the woman's who keeps the room.

**The chapters the Volume 12 close needs.** **591** holds the turn: the question of who may say no, asked by the boy, heard aright only by the woman of about thirty-eight, and the standing fact that no right to refuse has been given. **592** holds the first public argument, ordinary and unangerly, between the cloths and the cords. **593** holds the cost landing on a household with no vote, and a refusal that stops nothing and is remembered. **594** holds the man's last ground under the seeing rule, the uncut fan, and both readings of him unchosen. **595** holds the standing said on the only first in the block, in the street, with the three series unmerged. **596** holds the valley's own sentence for the refusal of a certificate, said whole in one breath with its three facts. **597** holds the crossing out with a person and no leaf, free-going, unlifted bar, unnamed mechanism. **598** holds the delegate and the believed refusal, written and unthanked. **599** holds the bill presented and not paid, and the man left further off and not made safe. **600** holds the last main-world page: standing hearing, empty head, washed floor unknown, face-down leaf, written refusal, day 1830, and her sentence. **And the figures the close must not re-collapse: yes-no-yes-no in that order and never again asked; nine days and a morning counted and never recounted; the two in three uncounted; the bar unlifted; the fourth head empty; the leaf face down; and nobody thanked at any point.**

---

## Volume 12, closed — Chapters 551 to 600, read whole

**The close read all fifty chapters and the ten weakest and the three strongest are a reading and not a measurement. The full argument, with the ten named and the three named, is `outline/volumes/volume-12-close.md` §2.14 and §9 item 7. This entry is the index.**

**The three that would survive a reader.** **`chapter-0568.md`**, the shortest chapter in the volume at 1,554 words and the smallest scene body in the volume at 1,161, and its best material is a woman of about sixty-four counting stones she has never once looked at and a man standing beside her not asking. **`chapter-0575.md`**, the volume's centre, and its best scene is a woman arriving at her usual hour at a tree and finding nine people at it. **`chapter-0600.md`**, the last page, and its best paragraph is four sentences about a key turned over once and not put into a lock.

**The ten that would not, in the order a reader would lose them, and none of the ten is here for a figure — every figure defect in this volume was in a different chapter.** **`chapter-0573.md`**, which is the longest chapter in Block 0003 at 2,017 words and carries the volume's largest money column and two long speeches that are both about the same rule and could be one. **`chapter-0581.md`**, the longest chapter in the volume at 2,378 words, whose subject is a woman noticing halfway through that she is about to sign her name to a finding and stopping, and which then spends 500 words on the signature after the moment has passed. **`chapter-0578.md`**, which is the first argument in twenty-two years and prints it as six speeches in a row with nobody moving. **`chapter-0582.md`**, which asks four sets the same question and gives each answer the same shape, and the shape is the cost column's shape. **`chapter-0591.md`**, whose best moment is a boy asking who is allowed to say no, and which then explains at length why nobody answers. **`chapter-0563.md`**, whose chapter is a man being shown to be bad at the only thing he is good at and which repeats the point in three places. **`chapter-0569.md`**, whose refusal is the volume's turn and is delivered by two men standing in a made road talking, and which gives each of them the same speech length. **`chapter-0553.md`**, which opens with a market and a man counting carts and reaches its real sentence, a woman saying why a channel is not mended, in its eighth paragraph. **`chapter-0572.md`**, which is the block where the word *quiet* enters the valley and gives the word to a carter who does not know what he is carrying. **`chapter-0586.md`**, which is three hundred paces of a job done wrong told by a man who watched it happen.

**The pattern in the ten is legible and it is the volume's own. Seven of the ten are a speech where a scene was wanted, and the three best chapters are the three in which a person does a small physical thing — counting stones, arriving at a tree at the hour she always arrives, turning a key over in her hand and not putting it in the lock — and no instrument in this project counts any of those three.**

---

## Volume 13, Batch 0001 — Chapters 601 to 610, "The Cheapest Thing on the List"

**601** — Embermonth 26, YR 320, a Friday and not a market day, the second hour, the room opened on a day it is shut. A carrier lost two days at a ford and brought the band; the copyist of about twenty-six reads the fourth sheet twice and says *it is not a return*. Four hands of writing, no seal, nothing on the back, and **Aren Kest at the top of the first page**. It stands a keeper down for nine days and shuts a road for the same nine days, the matter being under question, and names the road by its two ends. The woman of about twenty-nine says a name at the top is the whole of what makes a direction. The man of about thirty-four with a sheet says it names a district, not a road. The copyist says the second true thing of the day: **then it is a direction, and a direction is obeyed**, and nobody has to, and everybody will, because a world in which nothing with a name at the top is obeyed is a worse world, and that is true. Cost: the copyist's, 214.

**602** — Wolfmonth 2, a Thursday and a market morning, the fifth hour. The hearing stands, said in the street first. **The man of about fifty-eight with a badge refuses to call the sheet false**, because it is false only when somebody can show it false and this room has no way of showing anything false, and put a name at the bottom and it is slander today, leave the name at the top and it is a direction and no county can act. Then the second true thing: **a direction obeyed because of a name and not because of an office can be written by anybody in nine counties with a pen, and in thirty-one years it has never been done, and he wants it on a bare leaf with his name at the top of it.** The copyist will not put his name at the top, having made that point herself in the first week of a month, and writes seven words instead: *the man with the badge said it.* The man of about thirty-four with a sheet asks whether he is to send it and is told that is not the badge's office, and that is exactly why it will be done. Cost: the sheet man's, 232.

**603** — Wolfmonth 6, a Monday, the room shut, the counter under the weigh-house. Two sheets about the same four miles of channel, ten days apart in the writing and three weeks apart in the books, in two different series, and one of them is a lie. **The copyist says the rule is the wall and the wall is what a road is put behind**, so that one bad return cannot be set beside one good return and made to look like a pattern, and here are two of the sheets on it. Nobody in that room is permitted to notice that a road can be two things for three weeks. The woman of about thirty-four who keeps the key says out loud, once, that Aren Kest and the keeper of the room were in the lane together on Saturday and not on Thursday, the hearing stood on both, and nobody has said why, and she is not asking. The two sheets are laid side by side on the counter at the seventh hour, which is not a practice, and go back into two books. Cost: the woman of about twenty-nine's, 198.

**604** — Wolfmonth 11, a Saturday and a market morning, second hour to ninth, the culvert at the town's gate. A cart's off-side wheel, a hand's depth of silt, water across the made stone. A boy of about seventeen fetches the man with the limp and he clears it with about nine barrows and a hook. **Three courses of sill laid in lime with the middle course gone between two clean joints, and a new course laid dry across the bed** by men from the district, three weeks before, on an instruction on a sheet with a name at the top. A man of about fifty who lays stone: *we were told to keep the water off the road and we did.* At about the fourth hour his left palm is warm in the silt and he does not look at it. The only number taken off that street all day is the number of barrows. Cost: his, 245.

**605** — Wolfmonth 16, a Thursday and a market morning. The hearing stands and nobody is seated. **The man of about fifty-eight with a badge asks, in the room, in front of nine people, what roads are open.** Seven are named in plain terms with no figure and no distance, the seventh *and a small one, about which I am going to say only that it is small*. Nobody writes a list, because a list with a name at the top can be argued with and a list without one can be added to and taken from. **He answered for the ferry city and the fruit valley and said out loud that he answered for the two he has been in, and the badge said the true thing: you have not chosen, you have been the last one there, and a man who has been last somewhere cannot be argued with at all.** And he says the limit out loud and names nothing: seven, no eighth, and a thing he has run out of that does not ask his permission. The woman of about thirty-four who keeps the key says she has never once in four and a half years been asked which roads are open. Cost: hers, 249.

**606** — Wolfmonth 19, a Sunday, the ninth open crossing, the second hour, a lime kiln on a slope. **Nell Craddock, 40, runs the kiln alone and has crossed twice in two years and was sent by nobody.** A boy of about eleven gives the whole count straight off: nine open crossings, four shut weeks, the longest eleven days. **She will not be the first to say the number, and the reason is on the page: the first number anybody says out loud about a place is the number that place has for the rest of its life, and she is the one who walks it.** A woman of about sixty has held the far end for about thirty-one years and has never been asked what the road is. The finding arrives in his own mouth: **somebody local has to say it out loud in front of people who will remember, and be wrong if they are wrong, and be believed about everything else for the rest of their life.** He walks a quarter of a mile in front of the sledge and is not thanked. Cost: hers, 182.

**607** — Wolfmonth 23, a Thursday and a market morning. **Garvin Slade, 47, brings a page with seven lines, six marked, and the seventh with nothing against it and not struck out.** He is right about the strain, twice, out loud, and does not hide the cost of being wrong. **The block's truest sentence: a road with a figure on it is a weight; a road with nothing on it is not a light thing, it is a gap, and a gap is not carried by anybody, and a gap goes first, and I have never once in my life had to be cruel to close a gap.** The copyist says she could name the gap inside an hour and will not, because the three series are not derived from one another and making one out of another hands nine counties a single number anybody can check. **Aren Kest says the seventh has a woman on it who runs a lime kiln and a boy of about eleven who can say its whole count, and does not say the rest.** Six marks asked for and none given; the page goes back inside a coat. Cost: Slade's, 217.

**608** — Wolfmonth 26, a Sunday, the room shut, the counter underneath. The man of about thirty-four with a sheet cuts the direction to about nine lines, prints it in a district hand, and posts it on a Friday. **A gatekeeper with about eleven years at the ferry stone reads it out and does not check the name, and the page says why: the man is not in the district and nobody there can say who he is.** The keeper of the road is told at the gate, asked nothing, shown nothing, and goes home for nine days. **Nothing rotted, nobody was late, nobody was hurt, and no wages went anywhere.** The badge says the true thing: *it does not need to be believed, it needs to be delivered, and there is not one person in this county who has done a dishonest thing*, and then says out loud in the lane that he said in a room on the second of a month that it had never been done, and it has now been done, and it cost about nine hundred pence of paper and nobody's livelihood. Cost: the badge's, 260.

**609** — Frostmonth 5, a Tuesday, nine days up. The keeper of the road is back and the road is open and the grass is coming up in the middle of it. **She is not here to complain. She is here to say a number**, and the number is nineteen columns of days-passable with nine days taken out in a hand that is not hers, on a slate in her apron, and it is hers and stays in her apron. **The nine days cost her nothing, and the whole of the matter is that for nine days nobody in about nine houses knew whether she was stood down or had gone home for a bad week**, and the reason there was no arrangement is that the standing down came off a gate and the page has her name nowhere on it. She asks for one thing and is told there is no way to agree it. The badge says *I want you to do nothing, and I have never in thirty-one years wanted a man to do nothing.* Cost: hers, 225.

**610** — Frostmonth 7, a Thursday and a market morning four days off, the tenth open crossing, the sixth hour, the kiln again. She came again with a boy and a rope, and said she knew what the seventh one was and that she was not going to stop him, and **then asked him do you want it, twice, the second time in a working voice with a spade in her hand.** The rider: the second she knows, she can stop bringing him things; and there is a woman nine days off who has been stood down nine days for a page with his name on it and does not know it was his name. **He did not answer, and the chapter says why on the page: the answer he had was a yes, and a yes said on that slope at the sixth hour would have made him the seventh thing on the page she had just described.** The boy of about eleven stands four paces off with a sledgehammer up and will not remember any of it. **Nobody thanked anybody and nobody wrote it down.** Cost: his, 213.

## Volume 13, Batch 0002 — Chapters 611 to 620, "Three Blanks and Three Marks"

**611 — Hearthmonth the first, YR 320, a Sunday, the first and second hour at the counter under the weigh-house, the first of a month, 2,757 words, column 202 pence.** The room is shut and the counter is taking about four hundred and nine parish rolls a month. The man of about fifty-eight with a badge says the hearing stands in the street, says there is no stair open, and says it at a counter so that the counter knows it was said. Garvin Slade comes at the second hour with a page that is not the page: a bigger sheet folded in two, printed twice, with six ruled spaces against six lines and the seventh bare and not struck out. He says what is on it, and the one new sentence is that a mark in a space is not a vote but a statement that you will not stand in the way. The man with the badge asks whether the page names anybody, and the page names six places and one man and cannot be shown false. The copyist of about twenty-six says the thing that turns the morning over: it is not a form, a form has a head that says what the thing is for, and this one's head says who sent it, and it is the first thing in four and a half years she cannot tell you what it is. A woman of about fifty comes four days on a cart and says she would give the man a figure for her own road if he were asking about her own road and he is not. Aren Kest says the seventh can be named and has no figure and that a description is not a weight, and that the man may write down which road it is and it will not be a figure. The copyist writes one question on a bare leaf, *what a page with six ruled spaces and no head is*, and it goes in the drawer under the counter because the room is shut and she will not open a room on a Sunday.

**612 — Hearthmonth the fifth, a Thursday and a market morning, the second to the seventh hour in the room and then the lane, 2,403 words, column 215 pence.** The page comes back after four days with one mark in it, in the space against the waterworks country, put in by a woman who keeps a channel by hand for about twenty-two years. The man of about thirty-four with a sheet says a person can only agree not to stand in the way about a thing they can name in a figure. Then the second fact of the morning: the keeper of the room did not come down to the lane, and the copyist says it out loud and nobody agrees with her, and what she says next is the volume's finding, that nothing in that room depends on the woman who keeps it being in it and three series would go on being kept correctly for a month with her out of the lane, and that she has wanted a room like that since she was about nineteen, and that she is saying it in front of the woman. The man with the sheet then says the block's finding about the page itself: a man who will not put a mark in a space is a man who has refused, and the page has no way to hold a refusal, so the refusal comes back as nothing, and nothing in a ruled space is the same as a space nobody has reached. Aren Kest is in the room to the fifth hour and says a page that cannot be disproved is not a page that is wrong, and that a refusal that comes back as a blank means a road everybody refuses will have been shut by a printing press, and that the page is only dangerous for about nine days and after that it is a piece of paper in a coat. He goes down. She comes up at the seventh hour behind the copyist. They pass in the lane and neither says anything.

**613 — Hearthmonth the seventh, a Saturday and a market morning, the second to the seventh hour, 2,324 words, column 229 pence.** A man of about thirty-one from the road office comes four days on a made road with a letter and no paper entitling him to an answer, because his office has a page going round nine counties with a line on it for a road it cannot find. He asks the three questions a clerk is taught to ask, and is told no three times, and then says he will read his own correct reading of the answer before he puts it in his letter: a rule that keeps three series apart is a rule that says no two returns may be read together, and a page that wants six marks cannot be read any other way, so the rule is a wall in front of the only door there is and the wall is doing the work of the man who wrote the page. Nobody says he is wrong. The copyist is not defended and does not want to be; she says the rule was made for a different reason, that a pattern is a thing a district can be shown and the road will not care which half of it is wrong, and then she says the cost out loud, that four years of correctly kept series is why two warnings about the same four miles of channel sat in two books for three weeks, and that she laid them side by side once on a counter and put them back, and that there is no second practice in the room to replace the one she has just called a blind spot. The page comes back with two more blanks, three of six, and a man from a road office calls them refusals and the man with the sheet says he has three blanks and cannot tell. The badge-man asks whether he is going to fill the fourth head and is told there is no form for it and nobody may rule a fifth.

**614 — Hearthmonth the ninth, a Monday and not a market day, the second to the ninth hour at the culvert under the made road at the town's gate, 2,240 words, column 234 pence.** The frost has been and gone twice. The man of about fifty who lays stone comes at the fifth hour with a hod and says he had about nine days to decide whether to come at all and is glad he said that first. He goes under the arch and finds the course laid dry across the bed is gone, and under it a scour about a foot and a half across and a foot and a half deep at the low side, and the bed below it hard and clean, and the two lime-keyed courses where they always were. He says the middle course is the one that goes, because the top course has the road on it and the bottom course is keyed to the sides, and the middle one has nothing above it that would fall and nothing below it that would hold it, and nobody has ever asked him to look at a middle course. He digs to sound twice, lays three courses in lime, and refuses to lay a fourth, and says so before he starts rather than after, because afterwards you would have had a reason. The boy of about seventeen is paid a penny into his own hand. The man will not have the day written down anywhere, because in about nine years there will be a man who says it was always three courses and he will be right about the three and wrong about everything else. And he says the true thing of the afternoon: the worst job he ever saw was not one where the stone was wrong, it was one where the instruction was right.

**615 — Hearthmonth the twelfth, a Thursday and a market morning, the second to the seventh hour, 2,189 words, column 243 pence.** A man of about thirty-six walks four days with a paper in his coat and asks the room for a figure for the seventh road, because he has read that a road with a figure on it is a weight and a road with nothing on it is a gap and he cannot do anything about it for about nine days. The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps the room gives the correct answer in three parts: the figure is not the room's to give and not the room's to hold and there is no figure in here for most of the roads people ask her about; a road may be described only by its own answer, and if this room makes one series out of another for one man with a paper then every number it holds becomes a number that man is entitled to; and a figure about that road has to be said out loud by a person who walks it, in the place, in front of people who will remember, by somebody who can be moved off it, and there is nobody in that room who can be moved off it and the room is not going to ask, because the moment a room asks a person for a figure the room becomes the party that wants one. She then says plainly that the page will go round twice while he walks four days, that the correct position is not a comfortable one, and that the road will be shut inside a fortnight and there is nothing anybody in that room can do about it. He asks what to take back and is told to write that he asked, that the room would not give, and that the room was right. The copyist repeats that she could name which of the seven is the gap inside an hour and will not, and nobody defends her. The badge-man says there is no form in nine counties for asking a person for a figure or for refusing to give one, and the only place either has ever been written is a bare leaf in a drawer, and that is not a way of doing things.

**616 — Hearthmonth the fourteenth, a Saturday and a market morning, about the second hour in the lane, 2,381 words, column 251 pence.** The man of about thirty-four with a sheet asks Aren Kest to look at a thing outside, and shows him a copy of the forged page in his own one hand, made at the counter and carried to the district and kept in a satchel for about a month, and tells him in advance that he was not going to keep it and is not going to put it in the drawer upstairs. At the top of the first page, in the place where a name goes on a thing that is going to be acted on, is his own name, in a hand that is not his. He reads it twice. Nothing is at the bottom and nothing is on the back. He says the page is not his and is also nobody's, and the man with the sheet tells him the second half of that: a man who says a page is not his is making a claim, a claim has to be believed, and being believed is a thing you are given and not a thing you do, and there is no form in that room for it. He then says the third thing himself, that about four hundred people hold one true thing about him and about four four hundred hold another and he said no when he was asked whether he would put them straight. Then he names the one thing he could do and says he is not going to do it: he could stand in a lane on a market morning and deny the page in front of nine people, and a man with a limp denying a page in a market is a man telling nine strangers which of the two sets he is in, and he has not asked either set anything. The man with the sheet writes on the back of the copy, unasked, that the man named was shown it on that day and did not say the writing was his. The page has also come back with a second mark, from a man on the coast who keeps his count in tides and who put a mark in because he wants the arithmetic honest and because he is the only person within four days of the gap.

**617 — Hearthmonth the fifteenth, a Sunday, the third to the seventh hour at the counter, 2,206 words, column 262 pence.** Garvin Slade says he has been right about the water and is going to be right about a second, smaller thing: a direction that leaves a room on a Friday and is read at a gate on the Friday has no check in it, and a keeper has not been told and there is nobody to tell her. The fix is one day. The man of about thirty-four with a sheet then says what it costs, which is nothing about any road and the whole of his office, because his office is the day. He says a delayed thing has nobody's name on the delay: the blame moves off the gate onto the room, and the room has a door and a number, and he has a face. The badge-man says a rule needs a head and this room has none and neither has the district, and a rule that is right and has nowhere to go is worth exactly nothing, and this is the first time in thirty-one years he has watched one of them be right. Slade says he knew that when he said it and that nobody in nine counties has ever asked him what it costs. Aren Kest then says the argument out loud, in a working voice, to about four people at a counter: a mark is not a vote, a person can only agree to be held about a thing they can name in a figure, and in about nine days nobody will be able to tell a man who said no from a page nobody got to. He is asked what he would do and he does not answer, and the chapter gives the reason and it is not modesty. He says he is going to go and clear a grating because there are nine barrows of silt on that street and nobody has taken them.

**618 — Hearthmonth the sixteenth, a Monday and not a market morning, the second to the eighth hour on a made road four days down, 2,120 words, column 271 pence.** Ordinary work: a bridge over a ditch with about nine yards of parapet gone on the low side, three men and a boy of about seventeen and a cart and a load of stone, and the wages counted out on the sound parapet into nine hands. A carter carrying the district's paper stops and leaves a page on the parapet, and the man of about fifty who keeps the made road reads it standing up and marks a space in his own line without any speech, and says the reason when asked: because I can be found, because he has a figure in yards, because if he is wrong about anything on that page there is somebody he can be told about it and he can say so to his face, and a mark from anybody who cannot be found is worth nothing at all. A woman of about fifty arrives having walked about nine hundred paces and asks for a ditch, about nine hundred paces of it, nine days of three men, about four hundred pence of a county's money, and she has brought the numbers because he will not take her word for one. He says he will cut it in the next dry week, that there is no hearing and no paper, and that he is cutting it because the water is coming over a made road and the made road is his, and he would have cut it in the thaw about four hundred paces at a time over two years and got it wrong in about nine places. She says the one true thing: a page wants a mark and a ditch wants a spade, and only one of them has a head over it, and the one that has a head over it is going round nine counties. He tells the carter not to write the day down. Nobody thanked anybody.

**619 — Hearthmonth the eighteenth, a Wednesday and not a market morning, the second hour at the gate at the low end of a made road, 2,402 words, column 283 pence.** The man of about fifty-eight with a badge has come four days on his own account because a road he keeps is on a page, and says so first, and refuses to say the rest of it again at a gate. The gatekeeper of about eleven years reads the page out a second time to about nine people who did not hear it the first time, and does not check the name, for the same reason as last time. The woman of about fifty asks what her own line is for and is told that her line is the sixth, that the sixth is to be kept, and that the page says nothing at all about the sixth because the sixth is assumed, and that about nine people at a gate have been asked to agree to shut a road on a page that has never once said in nine counties that the road they are being asked to keep exists. She then puts a mark in the seventh space and says why, in a working voice: an empty space is a road nobody is carrying, and she would rather be argued with than be left alone on a page. She reads her count out loud at the gate: nineteen columns, one for each year, of the days that road was passable out of the days in that year, the last column unfinished, and a nine taken out of it in a hand that is not hers that she has never asked about. She says again that she would like somebody in that county to tell her which way her count runs. The man with the badge says nothing for half an hour and the chapter gives the reason. A carter waiting with his horse yoked asks whether this means a road can now be shut on a count, and she says no, and that a count shuts nothing and is the only thing anybody can be argued with, and that one time is one time and nobody may cite it, and the badge-man says *it is not a practice* and she agrees.

**620 — Hearthmonth the nineteenth, a Thursday and a market morning, the second to the seventh hour in the room and then the lane, 2,801 words, column 294 pence.** The keeper of the room did not come down for the second market morning running and the copyist says so out loud again and says she will not say it a third time. The page comes back from nine counties and the chapter sets out exactly what is on it: three blanks, two marks, and a sixth space with a mark and nineteen columns of figures ruled long afterwards in a different hand with a nine taken out of the last one in a third hand, and the seventh line bare. The man of about thirty-four with a sheet keeps the only record in nine counties of what the page did, on his own sheet with no head over it, and it goes down the lane in his coat, which is the third time a number has gone down that lane in a coat in about six weeks. The register takes its eighth entry, the question a woman of about fifty asked in the first week of a month about being told before a page about her road is read at a gate, which the badge-man answered by saying there is no way to agree it, and no finding goes under it. And then the copyist says out loud that she wrote the nine into the last column of a slate in an apron on a Tuesday, that she did not know which way the woman's count ran either, that the end is where a figure you do not understand goes, and that she had not told her in about six weeks and is telling her now because the count has been read out loud by a man with a page. Nobody thanked her. The man with the sheet asks whether anything on the page is wrong, and Aren Kest says yes and will not say what and gives the reason: if he says it in that room then the next man to come up that stair with a page and a name at the top of it will be able to say that a man with a limp has told this room that a road is saved by somebody saying a number, and that is a page and he would have made it. He names the three people who acted without being sent and says he did not send them and will not claim them, and goes down. She comes out twenty minutes later. They pass. Nothing between them was described and no finding is under the eighth question.
